He was the source of many of the documents produced by the 100 Parties to support their case on that issue. Mr Cooper submitted in closing that Mr Henderson had shown himself to be dishonest and, accordingly, the authenticity and accuracy of those documents cannot be assumed. I do not accept that. Those documents were included in the common bundle without the defendants raising any objection to the application of the default rule (in r 9.5(1)(c) of the [High Court Rules](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_reg/hcr2016170/index.html)) that the documents are what they appear to be. Mr Henderson was not challenged on their authenticity and accuracy.

<!-- al:478 -->

*Mr Whitley*

<!-- al:479 -->

\[152\] Mr Whitley failed to discover the deed of indemnity and the associated correspondence. In cross-examination, he said he did not know why the indemnity had not been discovered but that he did not omit it deliberately. I accept his evidence, for two reasons. First, in cross-examination Mr Whitley was not at all evasive about his funding arrangements with Mr Henderson and his entities. He readily disclosed the existence of the indemnity. He did not claim, as Mr Henderson did, to have forgotten such an important part of his funding arrangements.

<!-- al:480 -->

\[153\] Secondly, it became clear that Mr Whitley was in some significant ways a passive participant in these proceedings. There were several instances in which Mr Whitley appeared to be unaware of positions that had been taken on his behalf. For instance, he said he had not instructed his lawyers to resist Mr Walker’s December 2023 discovery application (they did). The most striking instance concerned discovery of the recording of his interview with Mr Eathorne. He said his position “all the way” had been to make full discovery and that if he had been asked about the defendants getting access to the recording he would “\[a\]bsolutely” have said

<!-- al:481 -->

> yes. But he said he had never been asked. This was puzzling, given that I had had to determine LPF’s challenge to Mr Whitley’s claim to privilege over the recording on the second day of trial. Re-examination did not clarify these matters.[13](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark12?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:482 -->

\[154\] It is troubling that Mr Whitley was such a passive participant in this litigation. But his passivity is consistent with him being unaware that his January 2024 discovery affidavit was incomplete. I consider it was likely a result of inattention rather than deliberate concealment on his part.

<!-- al:483 -->

\[155\] Generally, I found Mr Whitley to be a credible witness. He was frank in answering questions. He made appropriate concessions. That said, Mr Whitley had little involvement in the key events in dispute.

<!-- al:484 -->

*Mr Sullivan*

<!-- al:485 -->

\[156\] Mr Sullivan was subpoenaed by the Whitley Parties to give evidence. He gave evidence of events from 2012 to early 2018. He said he had reviewed emails and documents from that period to refresh his memory. A will-say statement had been prepared for him, though he had edited it. That statement was measured, albeit it tended to focus on events or documents to which the plaintiffs wished to draw attention. In cross-examination, Mr Sullivan readily answered questions about those events and documents, and about others, in a balanced way.

<!-- al:486 -->

*Mr Scutter*

<!-- al:487 -->

\[157\] Mr Scutter was also subpoenaed by the Whitley Parties to give evidence. A will-say statement was prepared for him, which he reviewed. He said at the outset that his intention was to assist the Court by being open and clear about what he recalled about the events in issue. He did exactly that, both in his evidence-in-chief and in cross-examination.

<!-- al:488 -->

13 Mr Whitley clarified his evidence on these matters in an affidavit that he made a few days after his evidence had concluded. The affidavit was made in opposition to an application by the defendants for orders preventing Mr Hucker and Mr Moss from continuing as counsel. I took Mr Whitley’s affidavit into account in determining that application. However, in determining the substantive issues in this judgment I have put his clarifying evidence to one side. That evidence was not given under the constraints that apply to re-examination during trial and I was not asked to consider the evidence for the purposes of determining the substantive issues.

<!-- al:489 -->

*Mr Walker*

<!-- al:490 -->

\[158\] Mr Walker gave evidence for two and a half days, most of it under cross-examination. He was open and often fulsome in his answers. This was reflected in the plaintiffs’ closing submissions, which drew on many of his answers. He was sometimes argumentative, but that was not surprising given the subject-matter of some of the questions. He was not evasive. Overall, I found him to be a credible witness.

<!-- al:491 -->

\[159\] Mr Walker acknowledged that there were some events (such as telephone calls) recorded in contemporaneous documents of which he had no memory. This was not out of the ordinary, given the age of some of the events.

<!-- al:492 -->

*Mr Newland and Mr Woodhams*

<!-- al:493 -->

\[160\] Mr Newland and Mr Woodhams were both credible witnesses. They openly answered questions in cross-examination. Their answers were measured, albeit sometimes firm, particularly when characterisations of events or documents were put to them. Both readily acknowledged that there were some events that they could not recall from many years before.

<!-- al:494 -->

*Mr Justin Smith*

<!-- al:495 -->

\[161\] Mr Justin Smith gave evidence about his involvement in the PVL proceedings. In cross-examination, Mr Barker KC and Mr Moss explored aspects of his involvement. Mr Smith was open and measured in his answers.

<!-- al:496 -->

**Issue 1: Did SPF reach a binding agreement with Mr Walker that it would hold any surplus payable under the Allied security for the benefit of unsecured creditors?**

<!-- al:497 -->

\[162\] The Whitley Parties plead that LPF agreed with Mr Walker that SPF would purchase the Allied security for the sole purpose of protecting its fees under the funding agreement and that “LPF/SPF” would hold the balance of any net proceeds payable under the Allied security (after payment of fees to SPF and any balance of the payment to Allied) for the benefit of unsecured creditors. I refer to this alleged agreement as the “**Allied security agreement**”. The Whitley Parties allege that the Allied security agreement was reached through correspondence and conversations

<!-- al:498 -->

> between representatives of LPF and Mr Walker from 29 August 2012 to 19 February 2013. Finally, they allege that the Allied security agreement was a term of the funding agreement between SPF and PVL, albeit they acknowledge that the Allied security agreement’s terms were not referred to in the Services Deed.

<!-- al:499 -->

\[163\] The 100 Parties do not plead the Allied security agreement. However, they opened and closed their case on the basis there was such an agreement.

<!-- al:500 -->

\[164\] The defendants deny there was an Allied security agreement. Whether there was such an agreement is directly relevant to the Whitley Parties’ third cause of action (which alleges SPF breached the agreement). It is also relevant to other causes of action relating to the entry into the Services Deed and the Distribution Agreement.

<!-- al:501 -->

*Legal principles governing the formation of contracts*

<!-- al:502 -->

\[165\] Both the Whitley Parties and the 100 Parties are claiming that there was a binding Allied security agreement — that is, a contract. The parties did not address me on the principles governing the formation of contracts, though their submissions were generally reflective of the principles that I am about to state.

<!-- al:503 -->

\[166\] In *Fletcher Challenge Energy Ltd v Electricity Corporation of New Zealand Ltd*, the Court of Appeal said there were two prerequisites to the formation of a contract: (a) an intention to be immediately bound (at the point when the bargain is said to have been agreed); and (b) agreement, or the means of achieving agreement, on every term which (i) was legally essential to the formation of such a bargain or (ii) was regarded by the parties themselves as essential to their particular bargain.[14](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark13?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) These two matters are determined objectively. In doing so, it is permissible to look to the background matrix of facts, including statements made orally or in writing in the course of negotiations.[15](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark14?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) It is also permissible to consider subsequent conduct, including what the parties have said to each other after the date of the alleged contract.[16](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark15?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) The Court of Appeal expressed reservations about the help that could be

<!-- al:504 -->

1.  *Fletcher Challenge Energy Ltd v Electricity Corporation of New Zealand Ltd* \[2002\] 2 NZLR 433 (CA) at \[53\].

<!-- al:505 -->

15 At \[54\].

<!-- al:506 -->

16 At \[56\].

<!-- al:507 -->

> obtained from internal memoranda or statements of subjective intention made by individuals when giving evidence.[17](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark16?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:508 -->

*Was there a binding Allied security agreement?*

<!-- al:509 -->

\[167\] The agreement was alleged to be one between LPF and Mr Walker, whereas the Services Deed is between SPF and PVL. No issue was taken in this regard by the defendants. That was appropriate. The relevant communications by LPF’s representatives were made on behalf of SPF (including in advance of SPF being incorporated). Mr Walker’s communications were as liquidator of PVL.

<!-- al:510 -->

\[168\] On behalf of SPF, Mr Woodhams sent an initial offer of funding to Mr Walker and Mr Sullivan in a letter dated 21 August 2012. The letter said the offer was subject to two matters: (a) satisfactory completion of due diligence by SPF; and (b) signing formal funding documentation. The letter included a paragraph addressing the Allied security:

<!-- al:511 -->

The offer is further conditional on the entry into satisfactory arrangements with Allied Finance Limited and Grant \[Thornton\] with respect to the first ranking security over the assets of Property Ventures Limited currently held by Allied Finance Limited in respect of which Grant \[Thornton\] has been appointed receiver.

<!-- al:512 -->

\[169\] Mr Sullivan responded to the offer in a letter dated 29 August 2012. In relation to the Allied security, he said:

<!-- al:513 -->

1.  The proceeds of any claims taken against directors and auditors are likely to be assets of the company and subject to Allied’s security (unless there were any contrary orders of the Court). Conceptually, our position is that an agreement should be reached as to how SPF’s claim as secured creditor interrelates with the \[funder’s\] return. The liquidator understands that SPF will agree to cede rights under its security over the proceeds of any settlement or award after payment to it of the project costs and the funding return as well as the payment of any sums required to be paid to Allied Farmers and Grant Thornton in accordance with the terms of the assignment.

<!-- al:514 -->

1.  ... I note there may need to be some work done on the safest way to cede or surrender the claim under the security so that the monies can be directed to unsecured creditors.

<!-- al:515 -->

17 At \[56\].

<!-- al:516 -->

\[170\] Mr Woodhams then presented a revised offer to Mr Sullivan in a letter emailed on 17 September 2012. Mr Woodhams’ covering email said:

<!-- al:517 -->

As discussed, please see attached the reworked offer letter in respect of the PVL litigation.

<!-- al:518 -->

I note that LPF is in agreement that, to the extent that it has received the repayment of all project costs paid by it, \[its\] Services Fee and any other amounts owed to it, the balance of any funds received (or to be received) under the Allied Security shall be held to \[Mr Walker’s\] order.

<!-- al:519 -->

We also confirm that it is contemplated that this will be formally documented once the assignment arrangements with Allied have been worked out and agreed.

<!-- al:520 -->

\[171\] The revised offer was again expressed to be subject to due diligence and the signing of formal funding documentation. A sentence had been added to the paragraph addressing the Allied security. It now read:

<!-- al:521 -->

The offer is further conditional on the entry into satisfactory arrangements with Allied Finance Limited and Grant \[Thornton\] with respect to the first ranking security over the assets of Property Ventures Limited currently held by Allied Finance Limited in respect of which Grant \[Thornton\] has been appointed receiver. It is acknowledge\[d\] that these arrangements will include arrangements between the liquidator and SPF in respect of any assignment of the benefit of the security over the assets of Property Ventures Limited\[.\]

<!-- al:522 -->

\[172\] Mr Walker accepted this revised offer on 18 September 2012.

<!-- al:523 -->

\[173\] Mr Woodhams then emailed a draft of the formal funding documentation, in the form of a draft Services Deed, to Mr Sullivan and Mr Walker on 27 September 2012. Mr Walker obtained advice from MinterEllison on the draft, following which some minor amendments were made to it. Mr Walker signed a final version of the Services Deed on behalf of PVL on 31 October 2012. Clause 3.1(b) of the Deed referred to the Allied security, saying that SPF’s obligation to provide funding was conditional on:

<!-- al:524 -->

\[A\]rrangements satisfactory to SPF in all respects being entered into with Allied Finance Limited and Grant Thornton, pursuant to which SPF shall obtain a first ranking security interest over all the assets and undertaking of the Plaintiff (including arrangements as to any assignment of the benefit of their security over the assets and undertaking of the Plaintiff).

<!-- al:525 -->

\[174\] The Services Deed signed by Mr Walker did not oblige SPF to hold any surplus received under the Allied security to Mr Walker’s order. It said nothing on that matter.

<!-- al:526 -->

\[175\] It was many months before SPF signed the Services Deed. On 18 February 2013, at which time SPF had still not signed the Deed, Mr Sullivan telephoned Mr Woodhams. Mr Sullivan’s evidence was that they discussed that it had been agreed that any surplus received under the Allied security would be held by SPF for Mr Walker and PVL’s unsecured creditors. The next day, 19 February 2013, Mr Sullivan emailed a draft letter to Mr Woodhams. His covering email said:

<!-- al:527 -->

Further to our call yesterday I drafted up the letter we discussed so that we are all on the same page when the funding deed is released by SPF. Can you have a read and make sure it covers the issues adequately and let me know if you have any concerns. It struck me that we should coordinate our views on this before I finalise the letter and sign it.

<!-- al:528 -->

\[176\] The attached letter had a “draft” watermark and was unsigned. It said:

<!-- al:529 -->

**Funding creditors**

<!-- al:530 -->

1.  As discussed with you, it is important that there is no misunderstanding between SPF and Robert as to the agreement reached by Robert with other funding creditors as well as the effect, if any, of SPF taking Allied’s secured creditor position on the terms of funding agreed. It seems from our phone call that we are in full agreement, but you have suggested I set this out in writing and I do so now.

<!-- al:531 -->

1.  With respect to the funding creditors, the funding deeds signed between Robert and Smith Crane & Construction Limited (**Smith Crane**) and MWH New Zealand Limited (**MWH**) grant the funding creditors a priority position in accordance with clause 1(1)(e) of the 7th schedule of the [Companies Act 1993](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/index.html). ... \[E\]ven if there was not to be a full recovery by SPF, it is expected that any sums set aside for Smith Crane and MWH will not significantly reduce the return to SPF.

<!-- al:532 -->

1.  It is, however, important to Robert that SPF expressly recognises Robert’s obligation to the other funding creditors ..., particularly as it is not expressly dealt with in the funding deed Robert has signed with SPF.

<!-- al:533 -->

**SPF as secured creditor**

<!-- al:534 -->

1.  With respect to the position of SPF as secured creditor, it is also important to record how the underlying security will be treated on resolution of the proceedings. In my letter of 29 August 2012 I had recorded Robert’s view that SPF will cede its rights under its security over the proceeds of any settlement or award after payment to it of the

<!-- al:535 -->

project costs / funding return and payment of any sums required to be paid to Allied Farmers and Grant Thornton in accordance with the terms of the assignment. That remains \[Robert’s\] position and it would be appreciated if this could be confirmed by SPF.

<!-- al:536 -->

1.  With respect to the underlying security I had also noted in my letter of 29 August 2012 that there may need to be some work done on the simplest way for SPF to cede or surrender its security, so that the proceeds of the litigation can be directed by Robert to unsecured creditors, rather than any second or third ranking security holders.

<!-- al:537 -->

1.  Robert is comfortable that the details of ceding priority are resolved in the future, but for now wishes to record his agreement with SPF that it will use its secured creditor status to protect its return of its project costs / funding fee as well as the entitlement of Allied Farmers and Grant Thornton to a share of the proceeds. Otherwise SPF will cede or surrender its security, as the case may be, for the benefit of the liquidator and unsecured creditors.

<!-- al:538 -->

...

<!-- al:539 -->

14 Can you come back to me as soon as possible to confirm our understanding of the position as set out in this letter is correct? Can you also confirm how you wish to ensure that these issues are incorporated into the funding terms between Robert and SPF?

<!-- al:540 -->

\[177\] In cross-examination, Mr Woodhams accepted that his recollection “\[i\]n broad terms” was that in the 18 February 2013 telephone conversation with Mr Sullivan he agreed that SPF would cede its rights to any surplus received under the Allied security. He accepted this was the express basis on which, a few days later (on 22 February 2013), he sought approval from LPF’s board for the funding of the PVL litigation. The board gave that approval.

<!-- al:541 -->

\[178\] At no point did Mr Woodhams or anyone on behalf of SPF respond directly to Mr Sullivan’[s 19](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s19.html) February 2013 email and draft letter. Mr Sullivan never finalised his draft letter. SPF signed the Services Deed in early March 2013. The Deed was in the same terms as the Deed signed by Mr Walker. It was not amended to incorporate either of the two issues raised by Mr Sullivan in his draft letter.

<!-- al:542 -->

\[179\] Mr Barker and Mr Moss submitted it was clear from the correspondence and conversations between Mr Woodhams and Mr Sullivan that there was a concluded and binding agreement that SPF would hold any surplus of funds received under the Allied security (after repayment of the Project Costs and payment of its Services Fee) for Mr Walker and the unsecured creditors.

<!-- al:543 -->

\[180\] I disagree. When Mr Walker accepted SPF’s revised offer on 17 September 2012, SPF and Mr Walker were agreed that SPF would hold any surplus to Mr Walker’s order. But the parties did not intend to be immediately bound by that agreement. The revised offer was still expressed to be subject to signing formal funding documentation, a point reinforced in the last paragraph of Mr Woodhams’ covering email of 17 September 2012. Where parties negotiate on the basis that their agreement will be recorded in a formal agreement signed by both parties, the ordinary inference is that the parties do not intend to be bound until both have signed that formal agreement.[18](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark17?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) In the present case this was not a mere inference — SPF’s offer was expressly subject to the signing of formal funding documentation. Objectively, the parties did not intend to be bound until formal funding documentation was signed by both parties.

<!-- al:544 -->

\[181\] When Mr Sullivan had his telephone conversation with Mr Woodhams on 18 February 2013, they again agreed that SPF would cede its rights to any surplus. But, objectively, they still did not intend that agreement to be binding. SPF’s commitments to Mr Walker had always been expressed to be subject to the signing of formal funding documentation. Mr Walker had signed the Services Deed, but SPF had not. It also appears from paragraph \[3\] of Mr Sullivan’s draft letter of 19 February 2013 that in the conversation Mr Woodhams asked Mr Sullivan to set any agreement out in writing.

<!-- al:545 -->

\[182\] That draft letter, and Mr Sullivan’s covering email, were consistent with the absence of an intention to be bound. The letter said that Mr Walker wanted to record his agreement with SPF and asked Mr Woodhams to confirm the position set out within it. The letter expressly contemplated that the two issues raised within it would be incorporated into “the funding terms between \[Mr Walker\] and SPF”. The email said that Mr Sullivan did not want to finalise and sign the letter until the parties’ views were coordinated.

<!-- al:546 -->

18 *Carruthers v Whitaker* \[1975\] 2 NZLR 667 (CA) at 671–672; *Concorde Enterprises Ltd v Anthony Motors (Hutt) Ltd* \[1981\] 2 NZLR 385 (CA) at 388–389; and *Verissimo v Walker* [\[2005\] NZCA 491](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/cases/NZCA/2005/491.html "View Case"); \[2006\] 1 NZLR 760 (CA) at [\[31\]](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/cases/NZCA/2005/491.html#para31)–\[34\].

<!-- al:547 -->

\[183\] Given that Mr Sullivan’s email and draft letter repeatedly asked SPF to confirm the position, the lack of any direct response by SPF to the email and draft letter indicates the absence of any intention on the part of SPF to be bound (rather than, as the plaintiffs’ submission would have it, acquiescence to a binding agreement). Nor did SPF respond indirectly to the draft letter. The letter contemplated that the two issues raised by Mr Sullivan would be incorporated into the funding terms between SPF and Mr Walker. Instead, in early March 2013, SPF signed the Services Deed without making any amendments to incorporate those issues. Objectively, that is consistent only with SPF not intending to be bound to the agreement set out in the draft letter.

<!-- al:548 -->

\[184\] The absence of any concluded agreement on the Allied security is also consistent with the parties’ subsequent conduct. On 20 March 2015, there was an email exchange between Mr Tim Smith, Mr Walker and Mr Woodhams, to which Mr Sullivan was copied. It concerned a letter that Mr Tim Smith had drafted to PwC’s solicitors relating to PwC’s application to stay the PVL proceeding. Mr Tim Smith said he understood that neither Mr Walker nor Mr Woodhams wanted to refer to the “side deal” between Mr Walker and Mr Woodhams “re SPF not keeping the entirety of the amounts to which it is entitled”. Mr Walker responded:[19](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark18?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:549 -->

I don’t want to disclose the ‘side deal’ *if one exists at all* ... If SPF wants to keep a surplus above fees and expenses ... then so be it. I won’t object.

<!-- al:550 -->

\[185\] To similar effect, also on 20 March 2015, Mr Walker emailed a memorandum regarding the PwC application to Mr Tim Smith, Mr Woodhams and Mr Scutter. Mr Walker referred to the matter of “SPF’s (possible) future intention to abandon its rights” as secured creditor. On that topic, Mr Walker said:[20](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark19?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:551 -->

Though I was oblivious to it at the time SPF undertook to assert no more than its entitlement under the actual funding agreement. *This was not (so far as I know) included in the contract*.

<!-- al:552 -->

\[186\] In June 2015, PVL filed affidavits in support of its opposition to PwC’s application to stay the proceeding. One was from Mr Woodhams. He deposed that in his discussions with Mr Walker (in 2012 and subsequently), Mr Walker had said that

<!-- al:553 -->

19 Emphasis added.

<!-- al:554 -->

20 Emphasis added.

<!-- al:555 -->

> the creditors of PVL should benefit from the PVL proceeding in the event of a successful outcome. Mr Woodhams deposed that:

<!-- al:556 -->

1.  ... Whilst not a legal commitment, LPF has indicated to Mr Walker that it will work with him to allocate proceeds of any Allied loan recoveries to appropriate interested parties.

<!-- al:557 -->

1.  ... LPF’s reputation and belief is that litigation funding should play an important role in providing access ... to justice for those that are not able to afford litigation without our assistance. Our expectation is that the allocation of proceeds of the Allied loans will be consistent with this. There is a clear understanding that this will be considered at the time a judgment is received.

<!-- al:558 -->

1.  However, both the liquidators and LPF (and SPF) have always been conscious of the fact that until there has been a successful outcome in these proceedings, there may be nothing to allocate. Accordingly, whilst there is this understanding, we have not seen the need to engage in the complex process of reaching agreement until such time as there is something to allocate. This is especially the case because, when SPF acquired the Allied loans, no particular thought was given to the precise distribution of any judgment sum: acquiring the Allied loans was a purely defensive move to prevent Mr Henderson buying the loans and associated security as opposed to being a stratagem designed to garner all the proceeds of the action to SPF.

<!-- al:559 -->

\[187\] Mr Walker also made an affidavit in support. He confirmed what Mr Woodhams had said about the content of their discussions, and said he shared the understandings referred to in paragraphs 4 and 6 of Mr Woodhams’ affidavit. These affidavits are inconsistent with there being a binding agreement that SPF would hold any surplus under the Allied security for Mr Walker.

<!-- al:560 -->

\[188\] In 2017, further affidavits and submissions were filed in the Supreme Court in relation to PwC’s appeal on its stay application. The affidavits and submissions are inconsistent with there being a binding agreement as to the Allied security. However, I put no weight on them in respect of this issue. By that time, SPF and PVL were in dispute as to whether there was a binding agreement. They would naturally tend to say things that supported their position on the dispute. Although the submissions were a collective effort, Mr Walker and Mr Scutter said (through Mr Sullivan) that they agreed to them only on the basis that their agreement was not to be taken as a waiver of their position on the dispute. For the same reason, I have put no weight on what the parties said to each other as the dispute intensified through 2017. Nor have I put any weight on Mr Sullivan drafting a heads of agreement on the Allied security matter in

<!-- al:561 -->

> April 2017. That drafting was equivocal: it was as consistent with wanting to formalise an existing binding agreement as with wanting to conclude a binding agreement.

<!-- al:562 -->

\[189\] I therefore conclude that there was no binding agreement between SPF (or LPF) and Mr Walker that SPF would hold any surplus under the Allied security for Mr Walker or for the benefit of PVL’s unsecured creditors. In reaching this conclusion, it has not been necessary for me to consider the effect of the entire agreement provision (cl 16.9) in the Services Deed.

<!-- al:563 -->

\[190\] It is not necessary for me to find why the parties did not reach a binding agreement on the Allied security. However, parties sometimes prefer not to reach a concluded and binding agreement on matters that are not essential to their bargain, particularly where the matters depend on future contingencies and so may never arise. One or both parties may be concerned that if they try to reach a concluded and binding agreement on those matters, this will jeopardise the conclusion of a binding agreement on the essential matters, and so they may prefer to leave those non-essential matters to be worked out, if necessary, in the future.[21](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark20?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) I am satisfied this is why the parties did not reach a binding agreement on the Allied security (or the other matter addressed in Mr Sullivan’s draft letter). On 18 February 2013, Mr Sullivan emailed Mr Walker, telling him that he wished to speak to Mr Woodhams about the other funding creditors (Smith Crane and MWH). Mr Walker responded with a short email: “Have the conversation in a way you see fit. But try to avoid causing him to back out!”

<!-- al:564 -->

\[191\] When taken in cross-examination to that email response, Mr Walker said that at that time he knew SPF had not signed the Services Deed, said that “they still had the right to bail, didn’t they”, and readily accepted the proposition that he was keen to keep SPF happy and avoid taking unequivocally firm positions. That was consistent with Mr Woodhams’ evidence, in cross-examination by Mr Barker, that Mr Walker made it clear that he did not need the Allied security issue to be finalised.

<!-- al:565 -->

1.  For a similar observation, see *Fletcher Challenge Energy Ltd v Electricity Corporation of New Zealand Ltd*, above n 13, at \[51\].

<!-- al:566 -->

**Issue 2: Did Mr Walker breach any fiduciary or other duty by entering into the Services Deed?**

<!-- al:567 -->

\[192\] The Whitley Parties plead breaches of duty by Mr Walker in entering into the Services Deed. In their second cause of action, they plead that Mr Walker failed to obtain court approval for the Deed and it was therefore unlawful. In their fourth cause of action, they plead that Mr Walker breached his fiduciary or statutory duty by failing to consider any litigation funding other than that provided by LPF/SPF and by entering into the Deed for the primary purpose of securing payment of his fees rather than for the benefit of the creditors of PVL and its subsidiaries.

<!-- al:568 -->

\[193\] The Whitley Parties did not pursue these allegations in closing. They focussed instead on Mr Walker’s decision to enter into the Distribution Agreement and on the payments that were made from the settlement proceeds. Nonetheless, they did not formally abandon their claim that Mr Walker breached his fiduciary and other duties by entering into the Services Deed.

<!-- al:569 -->

\[194\] The 100 Parties plead, in their fourth cause of action, that Mr Walker breached his fiduciary duty when entering into the Services Deed in two respects. First, they say he failed to review the Deed and consider its terms before signing it. Secondly, they say he failed to obtain court approval for the Deed.

<!-- al:570 -->

\[195\] In closing, the 100 Parties did not pursue the latter allegation, though they did not formally abandon it. The 100 Parties did pursue the allegation that Mr Walker failed to review the Services Deed or consider its terms before signing it.

<!-- al:571 -->

\[196\] In the absence of any formal abandonment, I will determine the following issues:

<!-- al:572 -->

> <!-- al:573 -->
>
> (a) Was Mr Walker under a duty to obtain court approval for the Services Deed?
>
> <!-- al:574 -->
>
> (b) Did Mr Walker fail to review the Services Deed and consider its terms before signing it?
>
> <!-- al:575 -->
>
> (c) Did Mr Walker fail to consider any litigation funding other than that provided by LPF/SPF?
>
> <!-- al:576 -->
>
> (d) Did Mr Walker enter into the Services Deed for the primary purpose of securing payment of his fees rather than for the benefit of the creditors of PVL and its subsidiaries?

<!-- al:577 -->

*Was Mr Walker under a duty to obtain court approval for the Services Deed?*

<!-- al:578 -->

\[197\] A liquidator has the power to “act in the name and on behalf of the company and enter into deeds, contracts, and arrangements in the name and on behalf of the company”.[22](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark21?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) Mr Walker exercised that power when entering into the Services Deed on behalf of PVL.

<!-- al:579 -->

\[198\] Liquidators can seek court approval for their decisions by making an application for directions under [s 284](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s284.html) of the [Companies Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/index.html). However, the courts will rarely make such a direction if a purely commercial decision is in issue[.23](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark22?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) Plainly, this means a liquidator is not generally under any duty to obtain court approval for commercial decisions.

<!-- al:580 -->

\[199\] I accept the submission, made by both Mr Salmon and Mr Cooper, that entry into the Services Deed was a commercial decision for Mr Walker, as liquidator, to make. This is consistent with the Supreme Court’s holding in *Waterhouse v Contractors Bonding Ltd* that it is not the role of the courts to act as general regulators of litigation funding arrangements, to give prior approval to such arrangements, or to assess the fairness of a funding arrangement as between the funder and the funded party[.24](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark23?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:581 -->

22 [Companies Act 1993](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/index.html), [s 260(2)](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s260.html) and sch 6 cl (h).

<!-- al:582 -->

23 See Paul Heath and Mike Whale (eds) *Heath and Whale: Insolvency Law in New Zealand* (5th ed, LexisNexis, Wellington, 2025) vol 1 at \[22.1\] and \[22.8(e)\], referring to *Re CBL Insurance Ltd (in interim liq)* [\[2018\] NZHC 2547](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/cases/NZHC/2018/2547.html "View Case"), \[2019\] 2 NZLR 262 at [\[20\]](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/cases/NZHC/2018/2547.html#para20), *Manning v Smith* [\[2020\] NZHC 1888](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/cases/NZHC/2020/1888.html "View Case") at [\[30\]](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/cases/NZHC/2020/1888.html#para30), *Re Blastclean Services Ltd (in rec)* (1985) 2 NZCLC 99,282 at 99,285 (in the context of directions given to receivers), *Re HIH Casualty and General Insurance (NZ) Ltd (in liq)* \[2011\] NZCCLR 12 (HC) at \[62\], and *Dalton v Mackley* [\[2021\] NZHC 2329](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/cases/NZHC/2021/2329.html "View Case") at [\[21\]](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/cases/NZHC/2021/2329.html#para21).

<!-- al:583 -->

24 *Waterhouse v Contractors Bonding Ltd* [\[2013\] NZSC 89](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/cases/NZSC/2013/89.html "View Case"), \[2014\] 1 NZLR 91 at [\[28\]](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/cases/NZSC/2013/89.html#para28) and \[48\].

<!-- al:584 -->

\[200\] It follows that Mr Walker was not under a duty to obtain court approval for the Services Deed.

<!-- al:585 -->

*Did Mr Walker fail to review the Services Deed and consider its terms before signing it?*

<!-- al:586 -->

\[201\] In closing, Mr Barker submitted that the evidence showed that Mr Walker did not care about the terms of the Services Deed. He emphasised the following:

<!-- al:587 -->

> <!-- al:588 -->
>
> (a) Mr Walker said that he did not “remember having any great thought about” the maximum Services Fee of 42.5 per cent that SPF could potentially charge and did not spend much time considering whether that would be a fair return to SPF.
>
> <!-- al:589 -->
>
> (b) Mr Walker obtained advice from MinterEllison that the draft Services Deed was drafted heavily in favour of SPF, that the Services Fee seemed very high, that the definition of Project Costs was very wide, and that if instructed to negotiate on his behalf they expected the Deed would be significantly altered from its current form. Mr Barker said that Mr Walker did not follow that advice because it was not consistent with his overall objective, which was to begin the PVL proceeding. Mr Barker emphasised Mr Walker’s acceptance of the proposition that “the actual fee they were going to charge wasn’t really your primary concern, it was the fact that they were going to fund you”?
>
> <!-- al:590 -->
>
> (c) Mr Walker did not seek to negotiate any of the terms of the Services Deed with LPF.
>
> <!-- al:591 -->
>
> (d) Mr Walker accepted that he was not that concerned about the content of the Services Deed.
>
> <!-- al:592 -->
>
> (e) Mr Walker, having been taken to statements he subsequently made (to Mr Newland on 5 February 2016 and in a memorandum filed with the Court on 15 July 2021) to the effect that he had never read the Services Deed, said that he did not study it intensely but “scanned” it.

<!-- al:593 -->

\[202\] This evidence, particularly when considered in the context of contemporaneous evidence, falls short of establishing the allegation that Mr Walker failed to review the Services Deed or to consider its terms before signing it.

<!-- al:594 -->

\[203\] Mr Walker did not sign the Services Deed in isolation. Mr Woodhams sent Mr Walker and Mr Sullivan a funding offer on 21 August 2012. This set out the essential basis on which SPF would fund the litigation, including the proposed percentage return to SPF. Mr Sullivan responded on Mr Walker’s behalf in a letter dated 29 August 2012. The letter recorded that “\[a\]fter further discussions with you \[Mr Walker\] does not take issue with the percentage return” but nonetheless proposed two changes to that return. One of the proposed changes was that to the extent a settlement or award (after Project Costs) exceeded $8 million, SPF’s return would be 20 per cent (rather than 42.5 per cent). Mr Woodhams presented a revised offer on 17 September 2012. The offer did not accept the 20 per cent proposal but did accept the other change that Mr Sullivan had proposed.

<!-- al:595 -->

\[204\] This contemporaneous evidence shows that Mr Walker did negotiate (to some extent, successfully) the terms of the proposed funding and did give thought to SPF’s percentage return, including the proposed 42.5 per cent return. Mr Walker referred to this in the course of cross-examination. When Mr Barker put to him that he had not sought to negotiate “better” funding terms with SPF, Mr Walker said that “\[Mr Sullivan\] had been negotiating with \[SPF\]”.

<!-- al:596 -->

\[205\] Turning to the Services Deed itself, after Mr Walker accepted the revised offer Mr Woodhams emailed Mr Walker (and Mr Sullivan) a draft of the Services Deed on the morning of 27 September 2012. Mr Walker replied to Mr Woodhams a few hours later, saying “I have read the thing myself” and raising a concern about the expert nominated in respect of the dispute resolution clause in the draft Deed. The concern raised, and the clause itself, were relatively insignificant. It is most unlikely that Mr Walker could have raised such a concern unless he had, as he said at the time, read the draft Deed. This contemporaneous communication is a much surer guide to what Mr Walker did than his later statements to the effect the never read the Services Deed. When he made that statement to Mr Newland in February 2016, it is telling that Mr Newland did not take Mr Walker seriously. When Mr Walker made that statement in

<!-- al:597 -->

> a memorandum to the Court in July 2021, he had been unwell for some time. The contemporaneous record is also a surer guide than Mr Walker’s recollection when giving evidence, almost 12 years after the event, that he merely “scanned” the document. A scan would not have picked up the point of detail that Mr Walker raised with Mr Woodhams. I therefore find that Mr Walker did read the Services Deed before he signed it.

<!-- al:598 -->

\[206\] Not only did Mr Walker read the Services Deed, he also forwarded the draft Deed to MinterEllison and instructed them to review it and to provide high-level, “red flag” comments. MinterEllison provided Mr Walker with more than four pages of comments on the draft Deed. Mr Walker was of course entitled to engage a professional adviser to assist him to review the Deed (and Mr Sullivan was also advising him in this regard). In this way, then, Mr Walker clearly reviewed the Deed. That Mr Walker may not have followed MinterEllison’s advice (to have them negotiate with SPF over the terms of the Deed) does not mean that Mr Walker failed to review the Deed before signing it.

<!-- al:599 -->

\[207\] After Mr Walker received MinterEllison’s comments, some amendments were made to the draft Services Deed. For example, the nominated expert was changed. There seem to have been some other minor amendments, as the numbering of the clauses does not in every case reflect MinterEllison’s advice. Mr Walker signed a final version of the Deed on 31 October 2012. When doing so, he made some further minor handwritten changes, which he initialled.

<!-- al:600 -->

\[208\] In summary, I find that Mr Walker reviewed and considered the terms of the Services Deed before he signed it.

<!-- al:601 -->

*Did Mr Walker fail to consider any litigation funding other than that provided by LPF/SPF?*

<!-- al:602 -->

\[209\] Mr Walker did consider other litigation funding opportunities. He tried, without success, to interest Allied and DFG in funding the litigation. Through Mr Sullivan, he identified a Wellington family as a potential litigation funder. The Wellington family showed some interest but was put off by the prospect of what Mr Henderson might do.

<!-- al:603 -->

*Did Mr Walker enter into the Services Deed for the primary purpose of securing payment of his fees rather than for the benefit of the creditors of PVL and its subsidiaries?*

<!-- al:604 -->

\[210\] As noted, the Whitley Parties pleaded this allegation but did not pursue it in closing. The 100 Parties did not plead the allegation, but it was a focus of Mr Barker’s closing and his cross-examination of Mr Walker.

<!-- al:605 -->

\[211\] Mr Barker submitted that for all the work that was to be undertaken in respect of the PVL proceedings, Mr Walker never thought there would be any return to unsecured creditors. Mr Barker referred to the following matters:

<!-- al:606 -->

> <!-- al:607 -->
>
> (a) In Mr Walker’s report to creditors of PVL and its subsidiaries dated 30 April 2012 (the first report after the lifting of the stay of execution of the liquidation order), he said “\[i\]t is unlikely at this stage that unsecured creditors will receive any recovery of monies owing.” When questioned on that statement, Mr Walker agreed that at the start of the litigation process his view was that it was unlikely that any unsecured creditor would be paid anything as a result of the litigation, given the “vast array of \[GSAs\] all over the place”.
>
> <!-- al:608 -->
>
> (b) When Mr Walker was asked about the proposal that SPF cede, for the benefit of unsecured creditors, any surplus recovered under the Allied security, he said that at the time he viewed the prospect of there being a surplus as “fantastical”. Mr Walker said that “\[i\]n a pessimistic frame of mind”, he thought at that stage there would be no money available for unsecured creditors after payments to SPF and salvage costs but that “\[h\]opefully it would be more ... but whether that was ever going to happen I don’t know, right, and I didn’t really turn my mind to it then”.

<!-- al:609 -->

\[212\] Building on that submission, Mr Barker said that Mr Walker’s primary motivation in entering into the Services Deed (and pursuing the PVL proceedings) was to recover fees he believed were due to him as liquidator, rather than to seek a return for unsecured creditors. The position of the creditors, Mr Barker submitted, was an irrelevancy to Mr Walker. Mr Barker relied on the following:

<!-- al:610 -->

> (a) When Mr Walker referred in his evidence to unsecured creditors, he generally meant himself. For example, Mr Walker was asked about SPF’s initial funding offer being conditional on SPF coming to terms with Allied over the Allied security and whether he was concerned that this could mean there would be no return to any unsecured creditor. Mr Walker’s response was: “No. Other than me of course because I would have been an unsecured creditor.”
>
> <!-- al:611 -->
>
> (b) Mr Barker submitted that Mr Walker effectively saw himself as an economic party within the liquidation itself, there to generate his own return. Mr Walker said that other creditors would be entitled to recover “once I’ve had my fill of bread”. Mr Walker made it clear where his priorities lay, saying “\[m\]y first priority is to me”.
>
> <!-- al:612 -->
>
> (c) Mr Walker agreed that in negotiating with SPF his main concern was to ensure funding was in place so he could prosecute the proposed claims.

<!-- al:613 -->

\[213\] I do not accept that Mr Walker never thought there would be a return to unsecured creditors from the PVL proceedings. He merely thought a return was unlikely. That was a realistic assessment, given the uncertainty involved in complex litigation and the GSAs. But he did not think there would never be a return to unsecured creditors. Had that been Mr Walker’s thinking, there would have been no point in him trying to reach an agreement with SPF that it hold any surplus received under the Allied security for unsecured creditors.

<!-- al:614 -->

\[214\] Mr Walker’s attempts to reach that agreement also show that in entering into the Services Deed and pursuing the PVL proceedings he was not primarily motivated by a desire to recover or secure his fees. His motivation in attempting to reach that agreement was to seek a return for unsecured creditors. It is true that his attempts did not result in a binding contract. But that does not change his motivation.

<!-- al:615 -->

\[215\] There was, with respect, a disconnect between the plaintiffs’ allegation with respect to the Allied security agreement and their allegation that Mr Walker entered

<!-- al:616 -->

> into the Services Deed for an improper purpose. In respect of the former, the plaintiffs contended that Mr Walker had been successful in his attempts to protect the position of unsecured creditors. But in respect of the latter those attempts were put to one side.

<!-- al:617 -->

\[216\] I acknowledge that in his evidence Mr Walker repeatedly referred to himself as being an unsecured creditor in the PVL liquidation. He also said, several times, words to the effect that he had some priority over other creditors. None of this indicated some improper purpose. He was a creditor of the PVL companies for his costs and remuneration. In that respect he enjoyed, under the seventh schedule to the [Companies Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/index.html), a priority over other unsecured creditors. He even enjoyed a priority over secured creditors to the extent of any salvage rights he might have (a matter I explore below). It was very clear to me, having observed Mr Walker give evidence for two days, that this was all he meant. For example, after Mr Walker said “\[m\]y first priority is to me”, he went on to refer to his priority under the seventh schedule and to his priority for any salvage rights.

<!-- al:618 -->

\[217\] For these reasons, I do not accept that Mr Walker entered into the Services Deed for the primary purpose of securing payment of his fees, rather than for the benefit of the creditors of PVL and its subsidiaries. He may have thought that a return to unsecured creditors was unlikely, but he was motivated to seek a return for them.

<!-- al:619 -->

\[218\] For completeness, I accept Mr Cooper’s submission that there is nothing improper in a liquidator pursuing litigation, and entering into a litigation funding agreement, even where it is unlikely there will be any recovery for unsecured creditors (after payment of the liquidator’s fees, litigation costs and the funder’s fee). In *Robt Jones Holdings Ltd v McCullagh (No 2)*, the Court of Appeal said:[25](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark24?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:620 -->

\[148\] As to the issue of the purposes for which recovery is sought, the liquidators do not dispute, at least for these purposes, that the recovery will be used to pay the costs of the liquidation, including their fees. There is nothing objectionable in that, in and of itself. The \[Companies\] Act gives priority to payment of liquidators’ fees in a liquidation because there is a public interest in the orderly winding up of insolvent companies. We see the position as well-encapsulated in the following passage from the judgment of the New South Wales Court of Appeal in *Hall v Poolman*:

<!-- al:621 -->

1.  *Robt Jones Holdings Ltd v McCullagh (No 2)* [\[2018\] NZCA 358](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/cases/NZCA/2018/358.html "View Case"), citing *Hall v Poolman* [\[2009\] NSWCA 64](http://www.austlii.edu.au/au/cases/nsw/NSWCA/2009/64.html "View Case"), (2009) 75 NSWLR 99 at [\[157\]](http://www.austlii.edu.au/au/cases/nsw/NSWCA/2009/64.html#para157) (footnotes omitted).

<!-- al:622 -->

While it is plain that liquidators should not “churn and burn”, in the sense of pursuing litigation simply in order to generate fees without any view to the interests of creditors or the public interest, we disagree with this passage if and to the extent that it is intended to convey that liquidators are never entitled to bring proceedings where the only prospect of recovery is reimbursement of the liquidators’ own fees and expenses.

<!-- al:623 -->

*Conclusion*

<!-- al:624 -->

\[219\] The plaintiffs have failed to establish that Mr Walker breached any duty when entering into the Services Deed. Given this finding, it is not necessary for me to address whether any of the duties were fiduciary in nature.

<!-- al:625 -->

**Issue 3: Were the PVL subsidiaries bound by the terms of the Services Deed?**

<!-- al:626 -->

\[220\] The Whitley Parties initially pleaded that the PVL subsidiaries were not parties to, and therefore were not bound by, the Services Deed. The Whitley Parties formally abandoned this position during the trial, filing a fourth amended statement of claim that removed this pleading.

<!-- al:627 -->

\[221\] The 100 Parties also pleaded that the PVL subsidiaries were not bound by the Services Deed. The 100 Parties opened their case on that basis. Mr Barker did not address me on this point in closing, other than suggesting that, given that the 100 Parties were not bound by the Deed, it made little if any difference to their claims whether the PVL subsidiaries were bound by it. However, the 100 Parties did not formally abandon the allegation, so I will determine it.

<!-- al:628 -->

\[222\] The Services Deed was signed on behalf of SPF and PVL. They are stated to be the parties to the Deed. It became binding between those parties when SPF signed it in early March 2013 and delivered the signed version to PVL.

<!-- al:629 -->

\[223\] No one ever signed the Services Deed on behalf of the PVL subsidiaries. The Deed does not state that the PVL subsidiaries are parties to it.

<!-- al:630 -->

\[224\] Because the PVL subsidiaries never signed the Services Deed, they cannot be bound by it as a deed. But that does not prevent them from becoming bound by the terms of the Deed as a matter of contract. Parties may become bound to a contract by

<!-- al:631 -->

> conduct. The test is whether, viewed as a whole and objectively, the conduct shows that the parties intended to be bound and had reached agreement on all essential terms.[26](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark25?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:632 -->

\[225\] The reason the PVL subsidiaries were not stated to be parties to the Services Deed is that at the time the Deed was drafted in September 2012, it was not contemplated that the PVL subsidiaries would be plaintiffs in the PVL proceedings. That remained the case through to SPF signing the Deed in early March 2013. The PVL subsidiaries did not issue their own proceeding until late April 2013.

<!-- al:633 -->

\[226\] Mr Walker, as liquidator of the PVL subsidiaries, consulted SPF on the decision that the subsidiaries issue their own proceeding, and SPF consented to it (and to the subsidiaries being represented by the same lawyers as PVL). I am satisfied that Mr Walker and SPF went through that process on the assumption (perhaps giving it little thought) that the subsidiaries were bound to consult and obtain consent by the terms of the Services Deed. Over the next four years, Mr Walker requested that SPF fund the subsidiaries’ costs of pursuing their claims, and SPF funded them. Again, both Mr Walker and SPF assumed that the subsidiaries were entitled to be paid those costs, and that SPF was obliged to fund them, by the terms of the Deed. This mutual assumption that the subsidiaries were bound by the terms of the Deed continued through to final settlement of the claims. Mr Walker authorised SPF to represent both PVL and the subsidiaries in settlement negotiations, and Mr Walker entered into the Distribution Agreement with SPF on behalf of both PVL and the subsidiaries.

<!-- al:634 -->

\[227\] This conduct was mutual and consistent. It all points one way. Objectively, SPF and Mr Walker, on behalf of the PVL subsidiaries, intended to be bound by the terms of the Services Deed.

<!-- al:635 -->

\[228\] I therefore conclude that the PVL subsidiaries were, as a matter of contract, bound by the terms of the Services Deed.

<!-- al:636 -->

26 *Boulder Consolidated Ltd v Tangaere* \[1980\] 1 NZLR 560 (CA) at 563 per Cooke J; and *Savvy Vineyards 3552 Ltd v Kakara Estate Ltd* [\[2014\] NZSC 121](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/cases/NZSC/2014/121.html "View Case"), \[2015\] 1 NZLR 281 at [\[111\]](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/cases/NZSC/2014/121.html#para111) per William Young, Glazebrook and Arnold JJ.

<!-- al:637 -->

**Issue 4: Did Mr Walker breach any fiduciary or other duties, or was he unduly influenced, in entering into the Distribution Agreement?**

<!-- al:638 -->

\[229\] The plaintiffs criticise Mr Walker’s entry into the Distribution Agreement. The 100 Parties allege, in their fourth and fifth causes of action, that Mr Walker breached his fiduciary duty by entering into the Agreement without court approval. In their fifth cause of action, they also plead that Mr Walker failed to ensure the terms of the Services Deed were complied with when entering into the Agreement.

<!-- al:639 -->

\[230\] The Whitley Parties make wide-ranging allegations about Mr Walker’s entry into the Distribution Agreement. These allegations are found in several causes of action. In their second cause of action:

<!-- al:640 -->

> <!-- al:641 -->
>
> (a) They allege the Agreement and any payments under it were unlawful because all such payments required court approval. They repeat the same allegation as part of their fifth cause of action, which is against SPF and LPF in knowing receipt.
>
> <!-- al:642 -->
>
> (b) They allege the Agreement is not binding on PVL and the PVL subsidiaries because, in entering into the Agreement, Mr Walker breached his duty as liquidator of PVL and the PVL subsidiaries to act in their interests and without any conflict, and SPF and LPF knew or should have known of the breaches. The alleged breaches include that Mr Walker entered into the Agreement:
>
> <!-- al:643 -->
>
> (i) under mental distress, and duress from SPF and LPF (though, in closing, they pursued this allegation as part of their undue influence cause of action);
>
> <!-- al:644 -->
>
> (ii) against legal advice; and
>
> <!-- al:645 -->
>
> (iii) without the consent of Mr Scutter.
>
> <!-- al:646 -->
>
> (c) They allege the Agreement is not binding also because Mr Walker entered into the Agreement under undue influence. No particulars are
>
> <!-- al:647 -->
>
> provided, but the eighth cause of action alleges, with particulars, that LPF and SPF unduly influenced Mr Walker to enter into the Agreement.
>
> <!-- al:648 -->
>
> (d) They allege the Agreement is not binding also because it was contrary to the Services Deed.
>
> <!-- al:649 -->
>
> (e) They allege, in the alternative, that if the Agreement is binding on PVL and the PVL subsidiaries, a number of payments under the Agreement were unreasonable expenses in the liquidations because they were not valid Project Costs under the Services Deed or the Agreement.

<!-- al:650 -->

\[231\] The Whitley Parties’ fourth cause of action also alleges that Mr Walker breached his fiduciary and statutory duties in entering into the Distribution Agreement. The allegations include that he failed to obtain court approval and that he misled or failed to advise the Supreme Court of the correct position in relation to the Allied security agreement and as to the distributions made following the Distribution Agreement.

<!-- al:651 -->

\[232\] As can be seen, the plaintiffs’ allegations (particularly those of the Whitley Parties) about Mr Walker’s entry into the Distribution Agreement are very serious. The defendants strenuously contested them. All this meant that Mr Walker’s entry into the Agreement was one of the focal points of the trial.

<!-- al:652 -->

\[233\] I have to determine the following issues:

<!-- al:653 -->

> <!-- al:654 -->
>
> (a) Was Mr Walker under a duty to obtain court approval for the Distribution Agreement or for payments under it?
>
> <!-- al:655 -->
>
> (b) Was Mr Walker under a duty to ensure the terms of the Services Deed were complied with when entering into the Distribution Agreement?
>
> <!-- al:656 -->
>
> (c) Did Mr Walker breach any fiduciary duty by entering into the Distribution Agreement?
>
> <!-- al:657 -->
>
> (d) Did Mr Walker breach any statutory duty by entering into the Distribution Agreement?
>
> <!-- al:658 -->
>
> (e) Did SPF and LPF unduly influence Mr Walker to enter into the Distribution Agreement?

<!-- al:659 -->

\[234\] Resolution of these issues first requires an examination of the events that led to Mr Walker signing the Distribution Agreement. Given that this was a focal point of the trial, and given the number of issues that arise from it, I have found it necessary to examine those events closely.

<!-- al:660 -->

*The context for the Distribution Agreement*

<!-- al:661 -->

\[235\] The Distribution Agreement stemmed from a dispute between the liquidators and SPF as to whether there was a binding Allied security agreement. To recap, the question was whether there was a binding agreement under which SPF would hold any surplus received under the Allied security (beyond its entitlement under the Services Deed) for the benefit of unsecured creditors.

<!-- al:662 -->

\[236\] This dispute arose in early 2017, after the Supreme Court granted PwC leave to appeal with respect to its application to stay the PVL proceedings. To provide context for the dispute and the discussions that then led to the Distribution Agreement, it is necessary to summarise the positions that the liquidators and SPF expressed (to each other) on this matter when PwC first applied for a stay in early 2015:

<!-- al:663 -->

> <!-- al:664 -->
>
> (a) On 20 March 2015, Mr Walker said that when funding from SPF was negotiated, he was “oblivious” as to SPF’s undertaking to assert no more than its entitlement under the Services Deed and that it “was not (so far as I know) included in the contract”. He said he did not want to disclose any such agreement “if one exists”.
>
> <!-- al:665 -->
>
> (b) In June 2015, Mr Woodhams and Mr Walker each made affidavits deposing that although SPF had indicated to Mr Walker that it would work with him to allocate the proceeds of any recoveries under the Allied security, it was “not a legal commitment”.

<!-- al:666 -->

\[237\] The Supreme Court granted PwC leave to appeal on 13 December 2016. The appeal was to be heard promptly, with PVL’s submissions due in early March 2017. The Court asked the parties “to address the propriety of the position of the liquidator, given the unsecured creditors are unlikely to be recipients of any proceeds of the proceedings, should they be successful”.[27](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark26?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:667 -->

\[238\] The grant of leave in those terms focussed the minds of the liquidators and SPF, and their advisers, on how the settlement proceeds from the PVL proceedings would be divided between SPF and the liquidators. Mr Walker and Mr Scutter sought the assistance of Mr Sullivan on that issue. Mr Sullivan had notionally remained junior counsel to Mr Smith KC on the PVL proceedings but had had little involvement in the day-to-day conduct of the proceedings since their initial stages in late 2012 and early 2013.

<!-- al:668 -->

\[239\] In an email on 15 December 2016, Mr Sullivan said to Mr Walker that SPF had taken the Allied security to protect the funding position and that “\[t\]here was a specific agreement reached not to take 100% of the proceeds but to only take the funding return and then to work with you to agree how best to distribute the balance”. Mr Sullivan proposed that there be a discussion with Mr Woodhams about how any settlement proceeds would be distributed. Mr Walker agreed. Mr Sullivan then approached Mr Woodhams, who suggested there be a meeting early in 2017.

<!-- al:669 -->

\[240\] Shortly thereafter, on 21 December 2016, Mr Smith KC sent a letter to PwC offering to settle the claims against PwC. The offer was for a sum well below that claimed in the proceedings but significantly more than the eventual settlement. The offer, together with the existing recovery from Fright Aubrey and any further recovery from the directors, would have given Mr Walker and SPF a sense of a possible upper limit of total settlement proceeds. It was not *the* upper limit, given that the plaintiffs in the PVL proceedings were claiming substantially more than the settlement offer.

<!-- al:670 -->

27 *PricewaterhouseCoopers v Walker*, above n 3, at \[2\].

<!-- al:671 -->

*Discussions leading up to the filing of the Supreme Court submissions in March 2017*

<!-- al:672 -->

\[241\] On 1 February 2017, Mr Woodhams and Mr Walker met and discussed, at a general level, how any settlement funds might be distributed. The following day, Mr Woodhams sent an email to Mr Walker proposing the following:

<!-- al:673 -->

In the interests of providing the liquidators with certainty, I am happy to confirm on behalf of SPF No.10 (“SPF”) that, should the proceedings be successful, following payment to SPF of the funded project costs, other costs and the Service Fee in accordance with clause 4.1 of the Services Deed, the following payments will be made to the liquidator:

<!-- al:674 -->

1.  An amount equal to 10% of the first $75m of net proceeds of the secured creditor claims received by SPF. The “net proceeds” will be the amount received by SPF in its capacity as secured creditor in relation to the debts acquired from Allied Farmers Limited and from Dominion Finance Group Limited after deduction of any costs or taxes payable by SPF on those receipts; and

<!-- al:675 -->

1.  50% of any net proceeds received from the same above $75m.

<!-- al:676 -->

We acknowledge that you have asked that we consider that the percentage be increased in respect of the first $75m. We are and will continue to consider this request but we are not in position to agree to this at this point in time.

<!-- al:677 -->

\[242\] Mr Walker forwarded that email to Mr Sullivan on 3 February 2017.

<!-- al:678 -->

\[243\] The same day, Mr Smith KC emailed Mr Woodhams and Mr Walker about the need for an updating affidavit for the Supreme Court hearing to explain SPF’s recent purchase of the DFG security. Mr Smith also noted the Court’s concern that unsecured creditors were unlikely to receive anything from a successful resolution of the PVL proceedings. Mr Smith raised the prospect of an agreement which formalised a more significant provision for unsecured creditors. Mr Walker responded: “This is a rather delicate subject in respect to which preliminary discussions have begun.”

<!-- al:679 -->

\[244\] Mr Walker forwarded Mr Smith KC’s email to Mr Sullivan. On 5 February 2017, Mr Sullivan replied to Mr Walker, copying Mr Scutter. Mr Sullivan said the issue had been “covered off” with LPF when the litigation funding had been agreed, namely that:

<!-- al:680 -->

LPF would get its funding fee and hold the balance \[(under the Allied security)\] to \[Mr Walker’s\] account. That is, was and remains the position. Why isn’t this simply stated. It is the elephant in the room!

<!-- al:681 -->

\[245\] Later that day, Mr Sullivan sent another email to Mr Walker and Mr Scutter, forwarding SPF’s reworked funding offer of 17 September 2012 in which Mr Woodhams had confirmed it was agreed that SPF would hold any surplus under the Allied agreement to Mr Walker’s order. Mr Sullivan commented that SPF seemed to be trying to wriggle out of this agreement.

<!-- al:682 -->

\[246\] Mr Sullivan then, on 7 February 2017, sent an email (approved by Mr Walker and Mr Scutter) to Mr Smith KC, Mr Woodhams and Mr Tim Smith. Mr Sullivan said that the funding agreement had been agreed expressly on the basis that SPF would hold any surplus under the Allied security to Mr Walker’s order. Mr Sullivan attached SPF’s reworked funding offer of 17 September 2012. Mr Sullivan said that the position had not changed as far as Mr Walker and Mr Scutter were concerned.

<!-- al:683 -->

\[247\] Mr Smith KC was supervising the preparation of any updating affidavits for the Supreme Court. He was doing so as counsel for the plaintiffs in the PVL proceedings. By then, SPF was already separately represented (by David Bigio KC) in its capacity as intervener on PwC’s appeal. Mr Smith KC’s evidence before me, which I accept, was that he was not concerned about SPF’s position other than to ensure that any dispute between the plaintiffs and SPF did not “pollute” the Supreme Court appeal. Mr Smith KC believed (correctly, in my view) that any dispute between the plaintiffs and SPF would be exploited by PwC in settlement negotiations.

<!-- al:684 -->

\[248\] On 10 February 2017, Mr Smith KC emailed to Mr Walker and Mr Woodhams (among others) a draft updating affidavit for Mr Walker. This was intended to supplement an updating affidavit from Mr Wilson. Mr Wilson’s draft affidavit was to the effect that there was no Allied security agreement but said that SPF had undertaken to pay a minimum share of any recoveries (including 10 per cent of the first

<!-- al:685 -->

> $75 million in net proceeds) to the PVL liquidators for the benefit of unsecured creditors. Mr Walker’s draft affidavit commented on that undertaking.

<!-- al:686 -->

\[249\] Mr Walker’s draft affidavit was forwarded to Mr Sullivan. Mr Sullivan sent an email to Mr Smith KC on the evening of 10 February 2017 saying that he had spoken with Mr Walker and that it seemed there were “some significant differences between the position set out by Bill Wilson QC / LPF and the position as I understand

<!-- al:687 -->

> it and recall it from \[Mr Walker\]”. He said if the differences could not be bridged, Mr Walker’s affidavit would have to be significantly altered to the point that it was inconsistent with Mr Wilson’s.

<!-- al:688 -->

\[250\] Mr Smith KC responded promptly to Mr Sullivan that evening. He said that while there might be differences in the parties’ respective positions, the draft affidavits were not intended to record any final agreement between them on how any settlement proceeds should be distributed but rather to record that SPF had undertaken to pay a minimum amount.

<!-- al:689 -->

\[251\] On 12 February 2017, Mr Sullivan sent an email to Mr Walker and Mr Scutter advising them to “take up arms now”, otherwise they would be “impotent later”. He said that if the PVL proceedings settled for $40 million then on Mr Woodhams’ proposal (to pay 10 per cent of the net proceeds up to $75 million), the liquidators would receive only $2 million.

<!-- al:690 -->

\[252\] Later that day, Mr Sullivan emailed Mr Smith KC, copying Mr Woodhams. He said that his and Mr Walker’s position was that SPF had agreed to hold any surplus from the Allied security to Mr Walker’s order. The next day, Mr Sullivan and Mr Woodhams discussed the dispute. They remained apart. Mr Sullivan made a file note of the conversation. In evidence, Mr Woodhams accepted that the note broadly recorded the positions that they took. The note records:

<!-- al:691 -->

I said to Jonathan that we needed to discuss how to move forward as the affidavits and current offer made by LPF were not acceptable to Robert.

<!-- al:692 -->

Jonathan sought to assure me that the 10% was a minimum offer and that the position that Robert wanted more was still with the LPF board. He said the board were not saying no, but they simply had not agreed as yet.

<!-- al:693 -->

...

<!-- al:694 -->

Jonathan said that ... \[pending any resolution\] Robert needed to sign the affidavit and reserve his position.

<!-- al:695 -->

I said that was not acceptable unless significant changes are made to the affidavits. ... I said that all of our negotiations with LPF were on the basis that the security would be a protection mechanism and LPF would only get its funding fee.

<!-- al:696 -->

\[253\] There were numerous further emails over the next two days, with the parties expressing much the same positions. On 14 February 2017, Mr Wilson made an affidavit. It said:

<!-- al:697 -->

1.  ... as previously stated to the Court, it has always been, and remains, the intention of SPF to make funds available to the liquidator for the benefit of the unsecured creditors of PVL. SPF has undertaken that the liquidator of PVL will receive, should the proceedings be successful, and following payment to SPF of the funded project costs, other costs and the Service Fee in accordance with clause 4.1 of the Funding Agreement, a minimum of the following payments:

<!-- al:698 -->

> <!-- al:699 -->
>
> (a) An amount equal to 10% of the first $75m of net proceeds of the secured creditor claims received by SPF. The “net proceeds” in this context will be the amount received by SPF in its capacity as secured creditor in relation to the debts acquired from Allied Farmers Limited and from Dominion Finance Group Limited after deduction of any costs or taxes payable by SPF on those receipts; and
>
> <!-- al:700 -->
>
> (b) 50% of any net proceeds received from the same above $75m.

<!-- al:701 -->

\[254\] The same day, Mr Woodhams emailed Mr Walker and Mr Scutter (among others) expressing concern about Mr Sullivan’s “sudden and unexpected interference in the conduct of the case”. Mr Woodhams said Mr Sullivan appeared to be using the Supreme Court hearing as leverage for a deal on the distribution of proceeds. He said LPF would not engage further with Mr Sullivan. He added: “We have confirmed to you in writing our position and that we will consider the request for a greater share in due course.”

<!-- al:702 -->

\[255\] Mr Scutter, Mr Walker and Mr Sullivan then exchanged drafts of an email response to be sent by Mr Scutter to Mr Woodhams. These exchanges show that all three were aware of the need to protect and promote the liquidators’ negotiating position in relation to the distribution of any settlement proceeds. They discussed how soft or firm the response should be and the need to reserve the liquidators’ position. In particular, Mr Walker made the draft “much softer” by removing a claim that there was an “express” Allied security agreement and instead saying that the original “negotiations in the arrangements” were not “wholly consistent” with SPF’s position.

<!-- al:703 -->

\[256\] Mr Walker showed an awareness of negotiating strategy. On 15 February 2017, he emailed Mr Scutter and Mr Sullivan saying: “I think I’ll tell Woodhams today that

<!-- al:704 -->

> I’ll be absenting myself from the negotiations and that he’ll have to deal with \[Mr Scutter\] ... which means \[Mr Sullivan\] as well!”

<!-- al:705 -->

\[257\] Mr Sullivan responded, on 15 February 2017, by suggesting that the liquidators engage senior counsel to advise on the position with respect to the Allied security. He said it was critical that he and the liquidators communicate without information flowing to Mr Woodhams (or to Mr Smith KC or Mr Tim Smith).

<!-- al:706 -->

\[258\] The same day, Mr Scutter sent the final version of the email to Mr Woodhams. It said:

<!-- al:707 -->

Kevin Sullivan is assisting Robert and myself. Kevin is to be copied into all emails and correspondence in respect to the matters on which he is acting.

<!-- al:708 -->

...

<!-- al:709 -->

Unfortunately it now appears that the agreement about the net proceeds being available to us as liquidators which was recorded in emails and letters, as well as various assurances from LPF that the balance of settlement funds would be held to Robert’s order, have been resiled from by LPF. We wish to work with LPF to resolve this matter constructively and expeditiously. We will however be considering all immediately available legal remedies in this regard and as a matter of urgency.

<!-- al:710 -->

...

<!-- al:711 -->

With regard to the latest version of the affidavit of Robert from Justin Smith QC, I will discuss this with Robert this morning but understand that he is prepared to sign this.

<!-- al:712 -->

...

<!-- al:713 -->

... I further note that if Robert affirms the affidavit that cannot be taken as any concession or agreement that LPF is entitled to any portion of the settlement funds or judgment monies beyond what was expressly agreed in the funding deed.

<!-- al:714 -->

\[259\] Mr Walker did sign the affidavit later that day. In it, Mr Walker referred to the undertaking recorded in Mr Wilson’s affidavit, saying:

<!-- al:715 -->

1.  In the final paragraph of Mr Wilson’s affidavit, there is a reference to the undertaking which SPF has given. The undertaking is to provide a minimum percentage of funds to me as liquidator of the plaintiff companies. These funds are for the unsecured creditors (including the claim of Mr Scutter and I as liquidators). These funds are to be derived from any amounts SPF receives as a secured creditor (being the assignee of the debt assigned by Allied Farmers Investments

<!-- al:716 -->

Limited (**Allied**)) by means of recovery of damages awarded in any judgment or settlement, and which are applied in discharge or partial discharge of that debt. They are therefore the net proceeds which remain after SPF has been paid its funding and services fee under the funding agreement with SPF (**Funding Agreement**).

<!-- al:717 -->

1.  As stated above and in Mr Wilson’s affidavit the percentages referred to in his affidavit are minimum percentages. Depending on the results of the litigation I intend to seek more for unsecured creditors and am at liberty to do so. But that is a matter for resolution between the funder and the liquidators. However, an arrangement in which SPF uses the Allied security to ensure a significant portion of the net proceeds of the litigation are paid back to the liquidators of the plaintiff companies is consistent with the discussions which we had with the funder leading up to the entry into the funding agreement and the affidavit of Mr Woodhams filed in the High Court ...

<!-- al:718 -->

...

<!-- al:719 -->

7\. I acknowledge that the amount of the payment to unsecured creditors will be dependent on the timing, enforceability, amount and other pertinent factors relating to any judgment that is obtained. However, for the time being, I am proceeding in what I regard as the reasonable expectation of being able to make a significant distribution to unsecured creditors if the plaintiffs succeed against PwC.

<!-- al:720 -->

\[260\] At that point, then, there was a resolution of sorts. SPF had undertaken to the liquidators to pay them a minimum percentage amount. The liquidators had noted the undertaking but reserved their right to seek more. SPF had acknowledged that the liquidators were reserving their right to do so.

<!-- al:721 -->

\[261\] On 16 February 2017, the day after Mr Walker signed his affidavit, he sent an email to Mr Woodhams noting that the recent email exchanges had been “a little testy at times” and may have reflected “misunderstandings” about “the division of spoils”. He then made the following conciliatory comments:

<!-- al:722 -->

As you observe such a debate may be premature as there are no spoils and we may be cast in the role of two bald men fighting over a comb. Indeed, I have to admit that I have a superstition about, to use another cliché, counting chickens before they hatch.

<!-- al:723 -->

I think, notwithstanding what I say about superstition, the sooner we clarify the better. I would prefer, for reasons you well understand, for \[Mr Scutter\] and \[Mr Sullivan\] to be involved.

<!-- al:724 -->

I do agree that it is crucially important to continue with the model of partnership (*commenda*) that has suffused all we have done hitherto.

<!-- al:725 -->

\[262\] Later the same day, Mr Sullivan emailed Richard Fowler KC asking if he could assist the liquidators in relation to the Allied security issue. Mr Sullivan referred to there being an “arrangement” that SPF would hold any surplus under the security to Mr Walker’s order. He acknowledged there was no “formal agreement” recording this. Mr Sullivan copied the liquidators to the email. Mr Walker responded by raising some points of clarification, including:

<!-- al:726 -->

LPF are concerned not to show a disunited front. I am not so sure that is in our interest. A nuclear option would be for \[Mr Scutter\] and / or I to fully reveal the scope of what LPF seem now to want to do.

<!-- al:727 -->

\[263\] Mr Fowler responded by email on 16 February 2017, accepting the engagement and noting that careful analysis needed to be given to how to test and resolve the dispute in the middle of the litigation against PwC. Mr Walker replied the same day, noting that while it was the “nuclear option”, he and Mr Scutter had a brief opportunity “to spell out that LPF are behaving in bad faith and wreck their position”.

<!-- al:728 -->

\[264\] I pause to observe the stark contrast between Mr Walker floating this “nuclear option” and the conciliatory statements he made to Mr Woodhams a few hours earlier that day. This indicates someone acting strategically in the negotiation of a resolution of the dispute with SPF.

<!-- al:729 -->
