They are not entitled to advance the claim for the first time in closing. Moreover, the breaches of statutory duty advanced by Mr Moss were

<!-- al:996 -->

> essentially the same as those advanced in relation to Mr Walker’s alleged breach of his duty of good faith, all of which I have rejected.

<!-- al:997 -->

\[394\] Mr Moss also submitted that a liquidator has a duty to have regard to the views of creditors and that Mr Walker failed to do so. This also was not pleaded and so cannot be advanced. Moreover, the duty arises only in the circumstances set out in [s 258](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s258.html) of the [Companies Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/index.html). There was no suggestion those circumstances had arisen here.

<!-- al:998 -->

*Did SPF and LPF unduly influence Mr Walker to enter into the Distribution Agreement?*

<!-- al:999 -->

\[395\] The Whitley Parties allege that LPF and SPF unduly influenced Mr Walker to enter into the Distribution Agreement and thus it is unconscionable that LPF and SPF retain the benefit of that Agreement. This pleading does not distinguish between LPF and SPF. It will suffice to refer to SPF.

<!-- al:1000 -->

\[396\] In order to succeed on a claim of undue influence, the claimant must prove that the alleged influence led to the making of the impugned transaction, and that the influence was undue in the sense that the transaction was not the result of the free exercise of an independent will by the counterparty. A claimant can establish undue influence either by proving actual undue influence or by recourse to an evidential presumption of undue influence.[37](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark36?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) In closing, Mr Moss confirmed that the Whitley Parties were seeking to establish actual rather than presumed undue influence.

<!-- al:1001 -->

\[397\] Mr Moss submitted that SPF influenced Mr Walker “by applying substantial pressure on him to enter the Distribution Agreement”. He emphasised two aspects of the way SPF applied the pressure. First, he said SPF would try to get Mr Walker to disregard the advice of his lawyers or Mr Scutter. He referred to Mr Woodhams’ emails to Mr Walker of 14 February 2017 (which complained of Mr Sullivan’s “interference”) and 20 February 2017 (which asserted that Mr Sullivan and Mr Scutter had made “baseless” allegations regarding SPF’s conduct), and to Mr Sheppard’s

<!-- al:1002 -->

37 *Green v Green* [\[2016\] NZCA 486](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/cases/NZCA/2016/486.html "View Case"), \[2017\] 2 NZLR 321 at [\[35\]](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/cases/NZCA/2016/486.html#para35) and \[48\], approving the principles summarised by Winkelmann J in *Green v Green* [\[2015\] NZHC 1218](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/cases/NZHC/2015/1218.html "View Case"), (2015) 4 NZTR 25-017 at [\[100(c)](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/cases/NZHC/2015/1218.html#para100)–(d)\].

<!-- al:1003 -->

> email to Mr Walker of 20 July 2017 (which said Mr Walker was “hiding” behind his lawyer). Secondly, Mr Sullivan said SPF refused to abide the request not to deal directly with Mr Walker on the dispute and instead “continued to pepper him with phone calls”.

<!-- al:1004 -->

\[398\] In respect of the first aspect emphasised by Mr Moss:

<!-- al:1005 -->

> <!-- al:1006 -->
>
> (a) I accept that Mr Woodhams’ emails of 14 and 20 February 2017 were an attempt to get Mr Walker to disregard Mr Sullivan’s and Mr Scutter’s advice and views. This is clear from the face of the emails. But I do not accept that those attempts influenced Mr Walker to enter into the Distribution Agreement. He did not enter into the Agreement for a further six months. In the meantime, Mr Walker retained Mr Sullivan’s services and both Mr Sullivan and Mr Scutter regularly conveyed their views to Mr Walker. The compromise reached in the Agreement reflected those views (in that it was a considerable way from SPF’s undertaking in the Supreme Court), albeit not fully.
>
> <!-- al:1007 -->
>
> (b) I do not accept that Mr Sheppard’s email influenced Mr Walker to enter into the Distribution Agreement. As noted earlier, I accept Mr Walker’s evidence that he was not “bullied” by this email and did not care what Mr Sheppard said. This is consistent with the evidence of those who dealt with Mr Walker at the time (to which I will shortly refer), which is to the effect that Mr Walker was not one to be “bullied”. Also, Mr Sheppard’s email was sent a month before Mr Walker entered into the Distribution Agreement.

<!-- al:1008 -->

\[399\] As to the second aspect, Mr Newland and Mr Woodhams each contacted Mr Walker directly on the distribution dispute after there had been a request that communications on that matter be between Mr Bigio and Mr Fowler. Mr Newland and Mr Woodhams contacted Mr Walker directly several times in July 2017. However, that contact did not lead to Mr Walker entering into any agreement. To the contrary, on 3 August 2017 Mr Walker emailed Mr Woodhams telling him that Mr Bigio’s latest proposal was “wholly unacceptable”. A few days later, on 7 August 2017, he called

<!-- al:1009 -->

> Mr Woodhams and expressed annoyance with SPF’s position and reminded Mr Woodhams that he (Mr Walker) and Mr Scutter were in control of the PVL proceedings. So, despite the direct contact by Mr Newland and Mr Woodhams, Mr Walker was still expressing himself forcefully and reminding SPF that he and Mr Scutter held some leverage. Further, Mr Walker was doing so in direct contact that he instigated with Mr Woodhams.

<!-- al:1010 -->

\[400\] Mr Woodhams also had one or more conversations directly with Mr Walker either on the afternoon of 18 August 2017 or the next morning. In those conversations, Mr Walker agreed to the essential terms of the Distribution Agreement that Mr Eathorne had set out in his email of 17 August 2017. Shortly thereafter, Mr Walker signed the Agreement. Obviously, there is an immediate connection in time between the conversations and Mr Walker entering into the Agreement. I am nonetheless not satisfied that Mr Woodhams’ direct contact with Mr Walker led to Mr Walker entering into the Agreement. The sequence of events is as follows:

<!-- al:1011 -->

> <!-- al:1012 -->
>
> (a) On 8 August 2017, Mr Walker recommended “the silent treatment”. This appears to have been employed until 17 August 2017. That day, Mr Sullivan sent an email recording Mr Walker’s proposal that Mr Eathorne and Mr Fowler meet with Mr Woodhams.
>
> <!-- al:1013 -->
>
> (b) Mr Eathorne met with Mr Woodhams later that day (I infer that Mr Fowler was unavailable) and sent a reporting email to Mr Walker, Mr Scutter, Mr Sullivan and Mr Fowler that evening. His email set out the most that Mr Woodhams proposed for distribution to PVL.
>
> <!-- al:1014 -->
>
> (c) Mr Walker responded promptly, saying “I think we agree to that” and that a fighting fund could be obtained in another way.
>
> <!-- al:1015 -->
>
> (d) Mr Sullivan then responded. The only concern he raised was the lack of a fighting fund.
>
> <!-- al:1016 -->
>
> (e) The next morning, 18 August 2017, Mr Scutter said he thought they had to seek directions to deal with the “large storm” led by Mr Henderson.
>
> <!-- al:1017 -->
>
> He did not otherwise express concern with the proposed distribution terms that Mr Eathorne had reported and to which Mr Walker thought they should agree.
>
> <!-- al:1018 -->
>
> (f) Later that morning, Mr Sullivan conveyed Mr Fowler’s recommendation that directions be sought because of the lack of a fighting fund (but not for any other reason).
>
> <!-- al:1019 -->
>
> (g) About an hour later, Mr Francis sent an email to Mr Woodhams and Mr Walker, noting the option of seeking directions from the court in order to be able to rely on an immunity and noting his understanding that this was “not the preferred option”. He asked Mr Woodhams and Mr Walker to send through “the anticipated potential distributions”. Mr Walker said merely that he was “not averse” to getting directions. Mr Scutter said he thought directions should be sought. At 12.28 pm, Mr Woodhams said his view was that directions should not be sought. He was the only one of the three to explain his view.

<!-- al:1020 -->

\[401\] Mr Walker’s response to Mr Eathorne’s email shows that Mr Walker was already agreeable to what Mr Woodhams had proposed to Mr Eathorne and that he did not think a fighting fund was necessary. Subsequent communications from Mr Sullivan, Mr Scutter and Mr Fowler addressed the lack of a fighting fund and whether there should therefore be a directions application but raised no other concern about the proposed distribution. So, from Mr Walker’s perspective the only outstanding issue was whether directions should be sought. In response to Mr Francis’s email, Mr Walker expressed his view on that issue in lukewarm terms and Mr Woodhams explained his view. In these circumstances, I am not satisfied that Mr Woodhams’ direct contact with Mr Walker later that afternoon and the next day led to Mr Walker entering into the Distribution Agreement.

<!-- al:1021 -->

\[402\] In conclusion, I reject the submission that the two aspects on which Mr Moss focussed influenced Mr Walker to enter into the Distribution Agreement.

<!-- al:1022 -->

\[403\] As well as focussing on those two aspects, Mr Moss submitted more broadly that SPF applied “substantial pressure” on Mr Walker and that this led to him entering into the Distribution Agreement. It is difficult to disagree with a submission pitched in such broad terms. The negotiations leading to the Agreement went on for months. Some of SPF’s written communications on the dispute were worded forcefully. Mr Walker said that some of his discussions with Mr Newland and Mr Woodhams were “heated”. All this can be characterised as SPF applying “substantial pressure” on Mr Walker. I am satisfied that this pressure (among other things) led to Mr Walker entering into the Agreement. But this is a rather uninteresting conclusion. Substantial pressure is commonly found in commercial negotiations. And here, substantial pressure was exerted by both sides. The written communications from Mr Walker and his lawyers were forcefully expressed. Mr Walker said he was the “tempestuous party” in the discussions. Mr Walker threatened to withdraw negotiating authority or to seek directions. All this led to SPF entering into the Agreement.

<!-- al:1023 -->

\[404\] I now turn to consider whether any influence from SPF was undue, in the sense that the Distribution Agreement was not the result of the free exercise of an independent will on the part of Mr Walker. In addressing this issue I will assume that SPF’s influence that led to the Agreement was not only the “substantial pressure” it exerted but also the two specific aspects that I have rejected (in case I am wrong in that rejection).

<!-- al:1024 -->

\[405\] Mr Moss submitted that the influence was undue because SPF was a well-resourced funder that was able to exploit Mr Walker’s vulnerable state of mind arising from his mental and financial distress, in impairment of his free will. He said that SPF knew Mr Walker was mentally and financially unstable.

<!-- al:1025 -->

\[406\] I first clear one matter out of the way. Mr Walker’s evidence was that his depression started some time after the Distribution Agreement was entered into. That evidence was not challenged, and the plaintiffs did not adduce any expert evidence or any medical records from the time. In closing, the plaintiffs did not suggest that Mr Walker was suffering from any mental illness when entering into the Agreement. To be clear, I find he was not.

<!-- al:1026 -->

\[407\] I accept that Mr Walker was experiencing stress during the negotiations for the Distribution Agreement. That is hardly surprising. The negotiations occurred against the backdrop of Mr Walker (and others) being involved in briefing witnesses and otherwise preparing for a 12-week trial that was to commence in early 2018. By that time, Mr Walker had committed time and resources to the PVL proceedings and the PVL liquidations for five years.

<!-- al:1027 -->

\[408\] The plaintiffs emphasise some of the statements that Mr Walker made to his team or to Mr Newland and Mr Woodhams about the stress he was experiencing and the pressure he was being placed under by SPF. Having read numerous of Mr Walker’s emails (even more than the many I have quoted or referred to in this judgment), and having heard Mr Walker’s evidence over two days, I consider those communications need to be read in context and treated with care. Mr Walker often uses florid language. He resorts to parable. He is prone to exaggeration. For example:

<!-- al:1028 -->

> <!-- al:1029 -->
>
> (a) Mr Moss submitted that Mr Walker had “buck\[led\]” following two phone calls from Mr Newland and Mr Woodhams on 3 July 2017, referring to Mr Walker’s statement in his email to his team on 5 July 2017: “I am drained of psychic resources”. It is hard to know what “psychic resources” would be needed in this context, but in any event it is clear Mr Walker was not writing literally — he immediately went on to say it was as if he had “won WWII only \[to\] have to fight the Russians”.
>
> <!-- al:1030 -->
>
> (b) Mr Moss relied on Mr Walker’s email to his team on 20 July 2017 that “I have been put under intolerable pressure and I have given in” and “I cannot fight them and they know that”. But Mr Walker had not actually given in and did continue to fight, as can be seen in his firm email to Mr Woodhams on 3 August 2017 that Mr Bigio’s draft agreement was “wholly unacceptable”.
>
> <!-- al:1031 -->
>
> (c) Mr Walker concluded that same email by saying “\[t\]he constant harassment to which I am subject is ... having an adverse impact on my health. You have been warned.” In evidence, Mr Walker said this was
>
> <!-- al:1032 -->
>
> “perhaps an exaggeration”. I am confident it was. Immediately after sending that email to Mr Woodhams, Mr Walker forwarded the email to Mr Scutter and Mr Sullivan. In so doing, he did not refer to harassment or his health, merely saying “\[k\]eep you off to try and defuse what has become tense again”. Mr Scutter responded a few minutes later. He did not express any concern.

<!-- al:1033 -->

\[409\] Mr Walker insisted in his evidence that his free will was not impaired. He said he understood the Distribution Agreement, received advice throughout and believed at the time (and still does) that the Agreement was a “reasonable compromise”. Under cross-examination, he said he did not think he was being bullied by SPF and that he was “not that easily bullied”.

<!-- al:1034 -->

\[410\] Mr Walker’s evidence is not determinative. But it is supported by the evidence of those who dealt with him at the time. Mr Sullivan said that Mr Walker would have periods were he was “strong and aggressive” and others where he was more passive and became reliant on others to take the lead. Mr Scutter said that he, Mr Walker and their legal team were thinking actively about their legal options and strategy and tactically about how to communicate with SPF on the distribution dispute. He said that Mr Walker was a “great” tactical thinker.

<!-- al:1035 -->

\[411\] Mr Smith KC dealt with Mr Walker over an extended period, including for several months after the Distribution Agreement was entered into. He described Mr Walker as clever, energetic in mind and insistent when he thought he was not being listened to. He said that Mr Walker looked physically unwell and at times behaved as if he was stressed. Under cross-examination, Mr Smith KC said he did not see the stress as remarkable. He said nobody told Mr Walker what to do or what to think (or, if they tried, they did not get very far) and that Mr Walker challenged and questioned advice regularly. Mr Smith KC concluded:

<!-- al:1036 -->

At no time that I recall did he compromise other than on a principled basis or allow himself to be directed as opposed to being advised. He never shrunk from an argument: quite, I may say, the contrary. I would describe him as one of the less biddable lay clients I have acted for.

<!-- al:1037 -->

\[412\] Then there is Mr Eathorne. As explained earlier, Mr Whitley examined Mr Eathorne under [s 261(3)(c)](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s261.html) of the [Companies Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/index.html). Mr Whitley accepted in cross- examination that the impression one would get from listening to the three-hour examination would be “quite different to the depiction of Mr Walker advanced in \[Mr Whitley’s\] case”. The cross-examination continued:

<!-- al:1038 -->

Q. \[The impression is\] much more favourable, isn’t it?

<!-- al:1039 -->

A. Yes.

<!-- al:1040 -->

Q. It presents in a much more professional, \[sic\] doesn’t it?

<!-- al:1041 -->

A. Yes.

<!-- al:1042 -->

Q. It presents him as not being pushed over by \[LPF\], doesn’t it?

<!-- al:1043 -->

A. Yes.

<!-- al:1044 -->

...

<!-- al:1045 -->

1.  And the way Mr Eathorne talks about him we’d sort of like our staff to talk about us like that, wouldn’t we?

<!-- al:1046 -->

A. Absolutely.

<!-- al:1047 -->

1.  And the way he talks about LPF’s influence is at odds with Mr Walker being a pushover who was suborned, agree?

<!-- al:1048 -->

A. That’s Mr Eathorne’s view, yes.

<!-- al:1049 -->

\[413\] Mr Eathorne’s view is quite important. He worked more closely with Mr Walker than any of the witnesses. Mr Whitley accepted that Mr Eathorne was “almost uniquely placed” to inform the Court as to what had really happened.

<!-- al:1050 -->

\[414\] In summary, the evidence is very clear that Mr Walker entered into the Distribution Agreement through the free exercise of his own independent will. The plaintiffs have fallen well short of proving otherwise.

<!-- al:1051 -->

*Summary*

<!-- al:1052 -->

\[415\] I reject all the allegations that Mr Walker breached his fiduciary or other duties in entering into the Distribution Agreement. I reject the claim that SPF and LPF unduly influenced Mr Walker to enter into the Agreement.

<!-- al:1053 -->

**Issue 5: Was the Services Deed, as amended by the Distribution Agreement, unlawful as an abuse of process?**

<!-- al:1054 -->

\[416\] Mr Moss submitted that the Services Deed, as amended by the Distribution Agreement, was unlawful because it amounted to an impermissible assignment of a bare cause of action and was therefore an abuse of process. He advanced this submission by comparing the position under the amended Deed with the position presented to the Supreme Court (including the undertaking by SPF) when it heard PwC’s appeal. Mr Moss submitted that if the Supreme Court had known of the amended Deed, it would have found it to be an abuse of process.

<!-- al:1055 -->

\[417\] Mr Moss submitted that on this ground — presented as additional or alternative to the many other challenges to the Distribution Agreement that I have just addressed

<!-- al:1056 -->

-   — the Agreement and Deed should be declared unlawful. Mr Moss sought that declaration either under [s 284](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s284.html) of the [Companies Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/index.html), the Court’s inherent jurisdiction or [s 73](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/cacla2017238/s73.html) of the [Contract and Commercial Law Act 2017](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/cacla2017238/index.html).

<!-- al:1057 -->

\[418\] I have concerns as to whether this claim was properly pleaded. The Whitley Parties’ second cause of action makes many disparate allegations about the Distribution Agreement, including that it was unlawful because of the absence of court approval. But they did not clearly plead that the Services Deed as amended by the Agreement was unlawful as an abuse of process. They do plead that the Supreme Court would have found the Deed unlawful had the Court been told of the ultimate distribution of the settlement proceeds. But they do not plead that the Court would have done so on the ground of abuse of process.

<!-- al:1058 -->

\[419\] In any event, there is no merit to the claim. It proceeds on the assumption that the Distribution Agreement produced a worse outcome for the unsecured creditors than SPF’s undertaking. That assumption would be correct only if SPF’s undertaking was to provide a minimum return to unsecured creditors *other than the liquidators*. That was not how the undertaking was presented to the Supreme Court. As I have shown, on the (correct) basis that the liquidators were unsecured creditors, the Distribution Agreement produced a better outcome for unsecured creditors than SPF’s undertaking.

<!-- al:1059 -->

**Issue 6: Are the 100 Parties secured creditors of the PVL subsidiaries?**

<!-- al:1060 -->

\[420\] The 100 Parties plead that each of them is a first ranking secured creditor of one or more PVL subsidiaries. They plead that their securities over the subsidiaries were obtained by assignment from the original creditor or an intermediate assignee. They plead that, as at 30 May 2019, the securities secured loan balances totalling more than $216 million.

<!-- al:1061 -->

\[421\] Mr Walker, LPF and SPF deny all these allegations. They therefore put the 100 Parties to proof, in particular as to the validity of the alleged assignments and the loan balances owing at the date of the alleged assignments. LPF also contended that if the 100 Parties had ever held securities in the PVL subsidiaries, they had subsequently surrendered those securities.

<!-- al:1062 -->

\[422\] It is necessary to deal with this issue by reference to each of the 100 Parties. All of the alleged debtor companies to which I refer are PVL subsidiaries (and are parties to each proceeding).

<!-- al:1063 -->

\[423\] The defendants ultimately did not dispute that any securities that were validly assigned extended (as a matter of interpretation) to the PVL subsidiaries’ causes of action in the PVL proceeding. Nor did they suggest that any securities were invalid by reason of non-registration on the PPSR. I therefore do not refer to those matters in the analysis that follows.

<!-- al:1064 -->

*100 Investments*

<!-- al:1065 -->

\[424\] 100 Investments claims to be a secured creditor of Lichfield Ventures Ltd (**Lichfield**), by way of an assignment from Propertyfinance Securities Ltd (**Propertyfinance Securities**). It pleads that:

<!-- al:1066 -->

> <!-- al:1067 -->
>
> (a) On 30 March 2005, Lichfield and Propertyfinance Securities entered into a GSA under which Lichfield granted to Propertyfinance Securities a security interest in all its property.
>
> <!-- al:1068 -->
>
> (b) On 30 March 2005, PVL (and other PVL companies) executed a deed of guarantee of Lichfield’s indebtedness in favour of Propertyfinance Securities.
>
> <!-- al:1069 -->
>
> (c) On 24 November 2006, Lichfield and Propertyfinance Securities entered into a loan agreement under which Propertyfinance Securities agreed to lend $2,662,500 to Lichfield.
>
> <!-- al:1070 -->
>
> (d) By a deed of assignment dated 27 January 2016, Propertyfinance Securities assigned to 100 Investments all its debt and security interests in Lichfield.

<!-- al:1071 -->

\[425\] Mr Henderson gave evidence of Lichfield granting the GSA and PVL granting the guarantee and produced the GSA and guarantee ((a) and (b) above). The defendants did not seriously challenge that those transactions had occurred. I find they did.

<!-- al:1072 -->

\[426\] Mr Henderson gave evidence of the 24 November 2006 loan agreement and produced it. The defendants had objected to admission of the agreement on the ground of hearsay. Mr Henderson gave evidence that he signed the document on behalf of Lichfield (and on behalf of PVL, a covenantor under the agreement). The defendants did not pursue the objection. The document is admissible, and I find the loan agreement was entered into.

<!-- al:1073 -->

\[427\] Mr Henderson did not give any evidence as to any loan having been advanced under the loan agreement. He instead addressed the loan balance at the date of the alleged assignment. I return to that below.

<!-- al:1074 -->

\[428\] The alleged deed of assignment from Propertyfinance Securities to 100 Investments was in issue. Mr Henderson did not sign this deed. The 100 Parties did not call the two signatories as witnesses. The defendants objected to the admission of the deed of assignment on the ground it was hearsay.

<!-- al:1075 -->

\[429\] Under [s 17](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ea200680/s17.html) of the [Evidence Act 2006](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ea200680/index.html), a hearsay statement is generally not admissible. [Section 4](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ea200680/s4.html) of the Act defines a “hearsay statement” as one made by a person other than a witness which is offered in evidence to prove the truth of its contents. Under [s 4](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ea200680/s4.html), a “statement” means, relevantly, a spoken or written assertion by a person of any matter.

<!-- al:1076 -->

\[430\] The deed of assignment contains some assertions. For example, recital A says: “The Borrower \[(Lichfield)\] is indebted to the Lender \[(Propertyfinance Securities)\] for the Debt.” As I understood their closing submissions, the 100 Parties rely on this recital (among other things) to prove that Lichfield was indebted to Propertyfinance Securities at the date of the assignment. Mr Barker emphasised in closing that Mr Henderson was closely involved in negotiating the deed of assignment. But that does not mean that Mr Henderson was the maker of the statement in recital A. None of the makers of the statement were witnesses. It is therefore a hearsay statement.

<!-- al:1077 -->

\[431\] The 100 Parties submitted that any hearsay statements in the deed of assignment should be admitted under the business records exception in [s 19](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ea200680/s19.html) of the [Evidence Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ea200680/index.html). They submitted that no useful purpose would be served by requiring the person who supplied the information used to compose the deed to be a witness, as they could not reasonably be expected to recollect the matters dealt with in the information [(s 19(1)(b))](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ea200680/s19.html), or that undue expense or delay would be caused if the person were required to be a witness [(s 19(1)(c)).](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ea200680/s19.html) I will assume, without deciding, that the deed is a “business record” in terms of [s 16(1)](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ea200680/s16.html) of the Act. I consider that neither [s 19(1)(b)](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ea200680/s19.html) nor (c) is satisfied. This was, self-evidently, not a transaction that occurred on a regular basis in the business of Propertyfinance Securities or 100 Investments. There is no reason to think that whoever supplied the information reflected in the recitals could not recollect (particularly by reference to other documents) whether Lichfield was indebted to Propertyfinance Securities at the time. Any expense or delay in calling that person as a witness would not have been undue, given the importance of the issue.

<!-- al:1078 -->

\[432\] In other respects, however, the deed of assignment does not contain assertions. Its operative provisions, such as the assignment provisions, do not assert anything. The assignment provisions effect an assignment rather than assert that an assignment

<!-- al:1079 -->

> has taken place. They are not statements (as defined in the [Evidence Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ea200680/index.html)), let alone hearsay statements. The deed of assignment is therefore admissible to prove that the assignment from Propertyfinance Securities to 100 Investments occurred.

<!-- al:1080 -->

\[433\] The defendants did not raise any issue as to the authenticity of the deed of assignment or as to whether it had been signed: they did not object to the application of r 9.5(1)(b)–(e) of the [High Court Rules](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_reg/hcr2016170/index.html) to the deed of assignment and did not challenge Mr Henderson on these matters. I find that an assignment did take place from Propertyfinance Securities to 100 Investments.

<!-- al:1081 -->

\[434\] A question remains, however, as to what was assigned under the deed of assignment. The deed says that Propertyfinance Securities assigned all its right and title to the “Debt” and the “Security”. Those terms are each defined generically in the deed. “Debt” means “the total amount owed by \[Lichfield\] to \[Propertyfinance Securities\] as at the Assignment Date”. “Security” means “all securities given by \[Lichfield\] and/or the Guarantors in favour of \[Propertyfinance Securities\] to secure the Debt”.

<!-- al:1082 -->

\[435\] 100 Investments therefore had to prove that there was a debt owing by Lichfield to Propertyfinance Securities as at the assignment date (which the deed defined as 23 December 2015) and that Propertyfinance Securities held, on that date, the GSA from Lichfield to secure that debt (this being the security which 100 Investments claimed gave it secured creditor status).

<!-- al:1083 -->

\[436\] 100 Investments pleaded that the debt that was assigned was one owing under the loan agreement dated 24 November 2006, in which Propertyfinance Securities was the lender. It opened its case on the same basis. Mr Henderson’s evidence-in-chief was to the same effect.

<!-- al:1084 -->

\[437\] In cross-examination, however, Mr Henderson was taken to a later loan agreement, dated 24 September 2009, between Lichfield and a different lender, Propertyfinance Funding Nominees Ltd (**Propertyfinance Funding**). This loan agreement was for the same principal sum, $2,662,500, as the earlier loan agreement

<!-- al:1085 -->

> dated 24 November 2006 between Lichfield and Propertyfinance Securities. The later loan agreement, which was signed by Mr Henderson, included the following:

<!-- al:1086 -->

It is acknowledged that this agreement amends and restates the Prime First Loan Agreement dated the 24th day of November 2006 between propertyfinance securities limited (“pfsl”) as Lender (the rights and interests of pfsl under the Prime First Loan Agreement having been transferred to Propertyfinance Funding Nominees Limited) and the Borrower and the Covenantor.

<!-- al:1087 -->

\[438\] In cross-examination, Mr Henderson accepted this acknowledgement. This was problematic for 100 Investments. In the 24 November 2006 loan agreement, Lichfield had agreed that security for the loan included the GSA. Thus, if the acknowledgement in the later loan agreement were true, the rights and interests of Propertyfinance Securities in both the debt under the earlier loan agreement and the GSA had been transferred to Propertyfinance Funding, whereas the deed of assignment on which 100 Investments relied was from a different entity, Propertyfinance Securities. Mr Henderson did not comment on this matter in cross-examination and was not re-examined on it.

<!-- al:1088 -->

\[439\] Mr Cooper submitted in closing that this meant 100 Investments had not proved it was a secured creditor. There was no evidence that after the 24 September 2009 loan agreement there had been any assignment of the debt and the GSA back to Propertyfinance Securities.

<!-- al:1089 -->

\[440\] The response in the closing submissions of the 100 Parties was surprising. They accepted that the debt and the GSA had been transferred from Propertyfinance Securities to Propertyfinance Funding. They said that the debt and the GSA had subsequently been assigned back to Propertyfinance Securities before being assigned to 100 Investments. Indeed, they said there had been intermediate assignments of the debt and the GSA before they found their way back to Propertyfinance Securities.[38](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark37?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:1090 -->

\[441\] 100 Investments had not pleaded the subsequent assignments. None of those assignments were in evidence. Mr Henderson produced registered financing

<!-- al:1091 -->

1.  This part of the closing submission was contained in detailed appendices. Mr Barker did not address me orally on this detail.

<!-- al:1092 -->

> statements on which 100 Investments now relies, but he did not say that they recorded subsequent assignments.

<!-- al:1093 -->

\[442\] It was not open to 100 Investments to close its case in this way. The defendants had no opportunity to investigate and challenge the subsequent assignments on which 100 Investments now relies. On its pleaded case, it has not proved that the deed of assignment dated 27 January 2016 assigned to 100 Investments either the debt owing under the loan agreement dated 24 November 2006 or the GSA.

<!-- al:1094 -->

\[443\] In case I am wrong about that, I will address the case that 100 Investments made in closing.

<!-- al:1095 -->

\[444\] As to the debt itself, Mr Barker submitted that Mr Walker had, in the PVL proceedings, taken the position that the debt was owed by Lichfield to Propertyfinance Securities. This was said to be evidence that the debt was owed to Propertyfinance Securities rather than to Propertyfinance Funding at the time of the assignment to 100 Investments in January 2016. It was also said that Mr Walker could not resile from this position.

<!-- al:1096 -->

\[445\] I do not accept that Mr Walker took the position that the debt was owed to Propertyfinance Securities. The initial statement of claim in the PVL proceedings (dated 12 November 2012) said, in appendix B, that the debt was owed to “Property Finance”. That could refer to either Propertyfinance Securities or Propertyfinance Funding. The second amended statement of claim in the separate proceeding brought by the PVL subsidiaries (dated 24 September 2013) alleged that Lichfield had borrowed funds from “Property Finance Funding Nominees Limited”, which can only be a reference to Propertyfinance Funding. The consolidated statement of claim (dated 11 July 2014) said, in appendix E, that the debt was owed to “Property Finance”.

<!-- al:1097 -->

\[446\] Next, Mr Barker submitted that in July 2017 Mr Walker had instructed Matthew Ashby (an expert witness who was engaged by Mr Walker in the PVL proceedings) that the debt was owed to Propertyfinance Securities. That is not quite correct. In the instruction to which Mr Barker referred, Mr Walker provided Mr Ashby with information about loans “entered into” by PVL subsidiaries. The list of loans did

<!-- al:1098 -->

> not include loan balances or identify the current creditor. For Lichfield, both the 24 November 2006 and the 24 September 2009 loans were listed, and for each loan the lender was described as “Propertyfinance securities”. That is more likely to be an error in recording the lender than an acknowledgement by Mr Walker that the debt was by that time one owed to Propertyfinance Securities (a proposition that was not put to Mr Walker).

<!-- al:1099 -->

\[447\] Mr Barker also submitted there was other extrinsic evidence of the debt being owed to Propertyfinance Securities at the time of the assignment to 100 Investments:

<!-- al:1100 -->

> <!-- al:1101 -->
>
> (a) He said financing statements registered on the PPSR showed that Propertyfinance Securities held the GSA (and therefore the debt) at the time of the purported assignment to 100 Investments. To the extent that 100 Investments relies on these financing statements to prove that Propertyfinance Securities held the GSA and therefore the debt at a particular date, the evidence is hearsay and inadmissible. Although the defendants did not record any objection to the admissibility of the financing statements when they were included in a supplementary bundle filed during the trial, it is unlikely to have been apparent to them until the 100 Parties closed their case that the documents were to be used to prove the truth of their contents (rather than merely the fact of registration). Even if they were to be admitted, I would give them little weight. They are no substitute for the purported assignments of the GSA and debt back to Propertyfinance Securities, which should have been in evidence so that their validity and effect could have been assessed.
>
> <!-- al:1102 -->
>
> (b) He said that in the deed of assignment Propertyfinance Securities had warranted that the debt owed by Lichfield was as set out in a statement provided to Lichfield. Mr Henderson’s evidence was that he had received that statement and that it recorded that Lichfield owed Propertyfinance Securities about $4.5 million at the date of the assignment. This statement is hearsay. 100 Investments sought to admit it as a business record. It is not clear to me that it is a business
>
> <!-- al:1103 -->
>
> record (there having been no evidence as to who made the statement or who supplied the information in it, and those matters not being apparent on the document itself). Even if it was, I would not admit it under s 19, for the same reasons I gave in relation to hearsay statements in the deed of assignment.

<!-- al:1104 -->

\[448\] As to the GSA, 100 Investments relied on the financing statements registered on the PPSR. As I have just explained, these are hearsay and inadmissible and, had I admitted them, I would have given them little weight.

<!-- al:1105 -->

\[449\] Finally, in respect of both the debt and the GSA, Mr Barker submitted that an issue estoppel arose from three previous High Court judgments, by which Mr Walker (though not LPF or SPF) was prevented from denying that Propertyfinance Securities held the debt and the GSA at the date of the alleged assignment to 100 Investments. The judgments relied on were *100 Investments Ltd v IAG New Zealand Ltd* (a decision of Thomas J),[39](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark38?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) *PVG Securities Trustee Ltd v 100 Investments Ltd* (a decision of Hinton J)[40](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark39?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) and *100 Investments Ltd v Walker* (a decision of Associate Judge Johnston).[41](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark40?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:1106 -->

\[450\] The 100 Parties should have pleaded this by way of reply. They did not. Nor did they refer to it in opening. The third judgment (that of Judge Johnston) was not in evidence. The 100 Parties raised the issue estoppel, and referred to the third judgment, only in their closing submissions. It was not open to 100 Investments to raise the matter in this way. I will nonetheless deal with it, as I can do so briefly.

<!-- al:1107 -->

\[451\] A party raising an issue estoppel against another party must show that those two parties are the same parties (or their privies) as in the earlier judgment. However, someone who litigates in different capacities is a different party in each capacity.[42](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark41?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) In *100 Investments Ltd v IAG New Zealand Ltd*, Mr Walker intervened as liquidator of Lichfield. In *100 Investments Ltd v Walker*, he was sued as liquidator of PVL. In the

<!-- al:1108 -->

39 *100 Investments Ltd v IAG New Zealand Ltd* [\[2018\] NZHC 3244.](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/cases/NZHC/2018/3244.html "View Case")

<!-- al:1109 -->

40 *PVG Securities Trustee Ltd v 100 Investments Ltd* [\[2019\] NZHC 1847](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/cases/NZHC/2019/1847.html "View Case"), (2019) 20 NZCPR 280.

<!-- al:1110 -->

41 *100 Investments Ltd v Walker* [\[2020\] NZHC 1947.](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/cases/NZHC/2020/1947.html "View Case")

<!-- al:1111 -->

1.  Patrick Keane *Spencer Bower and Handley: Res Judicata* (6th ed, LexisNexis, London, 2024) at \[9.21\].

<!-- al:1112 -->

> present proceeding Mr Walker is sued for what he allegedly did or failed to do as liquidator of those (and other) companies. But he is sued in his personal capacity, rather than in his capacity as liquidator of either of those companies. No issue estoppel therefore arises from those judgments.

<!-- al:1113 -->

\[452\] As to *PVG Securities Trustee Ltd v 100 Investments Ltd*, Mr Walker was not a party to that judgment. Mr Barker submitted that Mr Walker was the privy of PVG Securities. I do not accept that. Mr Walker’s unchallenged evidence was that he was not a director of PVG Securities and that he did not have a lot to do with that proceeding. He acknowledged that PVG Securities was an entity associated with, and operated for the benefit of, the liquidation of PVL. At most, there was a mutuality of interest — to use the test in *Shiels v Blakeley* — between PVG Securities and Mr Walker in his capacity as liquidator of PVL[.43](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark42?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) As explained in the previous paragraph, different capacities mean different parties. Again, no issue estoppel arises.

<!-- al:1114 -->

\[453\] Further, the issues determined in the three judgments were not the same as the issues I am currently considering. The first two judgments were concerned with the rights of 100 Investments as assignee of insurance proceeds over a property. Both judgments assumed, but did not have to decide, that Lichfield was indebted to Propertyfinance Securities. The third judgment was concerned with whether 100 Investments was a creditor of PVL (not a creditor of Lichfield). None of the judgments made any determinations concerning the GSA.

<!-- al:1115 -->

\[454\] For all these reasons, 100 Investments has not proved, on the balance of probabilities, that it is a secured creditor of Lichfield.

<!-- al:1116 -->

*FTG Securities Ltd*

<!-- al:1117 -->

\[455\] FTG Securities Ltd (**FTG Securities**) claims to be a secured creditor of two PVL subsidiaries, Tuam Ventures Ltd (**Tuam Ventures**) and Cashel Ventures Ltd (**Cashel Ventures**), by way of assignment of the right to be repaid loans advanced to those subsidiaries by South Canterbury Finance Ltd (**South Canterbury Finance**).[44](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark43?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:1118 -->

43 *Shiels v Blakeley* [\[1986\] NZCA 445](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/cases/NZCA/1986/445.html "View Case"); \[1986\] 2 NZLR 262 (CA) at 268.

<!-- al:1119 -->

1.  Some of these loans were advanced by Canterbury Finance Ltd. That company was subsequently amalgamated with various companies to become South Canterbury Finance.

<!-- al:1120 -->

> FTG Securities also claims to be a secured creditor of Cashel Ventures by way of assignment of the right to be repaid a loan advanced to Cashel Ventures by DFG.

<!-- al:1121 -->

*FTG Securities and the South Canterbury Finance loans*

<!-- al:1122 -->

\[456\] I first address the loans themselves before addressing the alleged assignments. In respect of the loans advanced by South Canterbury Finance to Tuam Ventures, FTG Securities pleads that:

<!-- al:1123 -->

> <!-- al:1124 -->
>
> (a) On 7 October 2005, Tuam Ventures and South Canterbury Finance entered into a GSA under which Tuam Ventures granted to South Canterbury Finance a security interest in all its property.
>
> <!-- al:1125 -->
>
> (b) On 17 October 2006, PVL executed a deed of guarantee of Tuam Ventures’ indebtedness in favour of South Canterbury Finance.
>
> <!-- al:1126 -->
>
> (c) On 10 May 2007, Tuam Ventures and South Canterbury Finance entered into a loan agreement under which South Canterbury Finance agreed to lend $1,750,000 to Tuam Ventures.
>
> <!-- al:1127 -->
>
> (d) On 29 October 2007, funding approval for $1,200,000 was agreed between South Canterbury Finance and Tuam Ventures.
>
> <!-- al:1128 -->
>
> (e) On 1 November 2007, Cashel Ventures gave a deed of guarantee and indemnity to South Canterbury Finance in respect of Tuam Ventures.
>
> <!-- al:1129 -->
>
> (f) On 25 February 2008, there was a variation to the term loan agreement between Tuam Ventures and South Canterbury Finance.
>
> <!-- al:1130 -->
>
> (g) On 23 December 2008, a cross-guarantee deed was entered into between various guarantors (including Tuam Ventures and Cashel Ventures) in favour of South Canterbury Finance.

<!-- al:1131 -->

\[457\] Mr Henderson gave evidence of the GSA and the 10 May 2007 term loan agreement. I am satisfied both were entered into. He said Tuam Ventures “drew

<!-- al:1132 -->

> down” on the term loan agreement facility on 18 May 2007 but did not provide any documentary evidence to support this and did not say how much was drawn down.

<!-- al:1133 -->

\[458\] He also gave evidence of the deed of guarantee given by PVL on 17 October 2006 and the deed of guarantee and indemnity given by Cashel Ventures on 1 November 2007, and I am satisfied they were entered into.

<!-- al:1134 -->

\[459\] As to the funding approval on 29 October 2007, Mr Henderson’s evidence was that South Canterbury Finance provided Tuam Ventures with a standby letter of credit and a guarantee approving funding facilities up to $1,200,000 in favour of the Bank of New Zealand. He produced a “formal approval of funding facilities” between South Canterbury Finance and Tuam Ventures, and I am satisfied it was entered into. He said Tuam Ventures drew down on this facility on 28 October 2008 but he did not say how much was drawn down.

<!-- al:1135 -->

\[460\] Mr Henderson also produced a deed of subordination and priority dated 25 February 2008 between Tuam Ventures, the Bank of New Zealand and South Canterbury Finance. I am satisfied this deed was entered into. By cl 15 of the deed, the Bank of New Zealand and South Canterbury Finance agreed they would not transfer or assign any interest in or right to their securities in Tuam Ventures unless the assignee agreed to be bound by the deed of priority.

<!-- al:1136 -->

\[461\] Finally, Mr Henderson produced the cross-guarantee deed dated 23 December 2008. I am satisfied this deed was entered into.

<!-- al:1137 -->

\[462\] In respect of the loans advanced by South Canterbury Finance to Cashel Ventures, FTG Securities pleads that:

<!-- al:1138 -->

> <!-- al:1139 -->
>
> (a) On 17 October 2006:
>
> <!-- al:1140 -->
>
> (i) Cashel Ventures and South Canterbury Finance entered into a GSA under which Cashel Ventures granted to South Canterbury Finance a security interest in all its property.
>
> <!-- al:1141 -->
>
> (ii) Cashel Ventures and South Canterbury Finance entered into a loan agreement under which South Canterbury Finance agreed to lend $16 million to Cashel Ventures.
>
> <!-- al:1142 -->
>
> (iii) PVL executed a deed of guarantee of Cashel Ventures’ indebtedness in favour of South Canterbury Finance.
>
> <!-- al:1143 -->
>
> (b) On 12 August 2009, a term loan agreement was entered into between Cashel Ventures and South Canterbury Finance for up to $27,425,000.

<!-- al:1144 -->

\[463\] Mr Henderson gave evidence of the GSA and the two loan agreements, and I am satisfied they were entered into. His evidence was also that the principal amount under the first loan was increased to $20 million on 25 February 2008. He did not explain the purpose of the second loan or its interaction with the first loan. The impression conveyed from his evidence was that it was an entirely separate loan. However, the second loan says that its main purpose (as to $24,825,000 of the

<!-- al:1145 -->

> $27,425,000) was “Existing facility” and there were additional guarantors under the second loan agreement. I find that the second loan was used to repay the first loan and so was in substitution for it.

<!-- al:1146 -->

\[464\] When giving evidence of the second loan, Mr Henderson produced a copy of what he said was the term loan agreement. The term loan agreement referred to South Canterbury Finance’s standard terms and conditions and purported to annex a copy of those terms and conditions. The version of the agreement produced by Mr Henderson omitted those standard terms and conditions. In cross-examination, he was taken to another copy of the agreement that did include the standard terms and conditions. They included, at cl 32, a term that the lender (defined in cl 1.1 to include successors and assigns) could assign its rights only to a bank or financial institution. Mr Henderson accepted that he had read cl 32 before. He was unable to explain why the standard terms and conditions were not included in the version of the agreement that he produced.

<!-- al:1147 -->

\[465\] I now turn to the alleged assignments of the right to be repaid the South Canterbury Finance loans. FTG Securities pleads that:

<!-- al:1148 -->

> (a) On 1 June 2012, South Canterbury Finance and Crown Asset Management Ltd (**CAML**) entered into an asset sale agreement (the **SCF Asset Sale Agreement**) under which South Canterbury Finance sold its debt and security interests in Tuam Ventures and Cashel Ventures to CAML.
>
> <!-- al:1149 -->
>
> (b) By a deed of transfer dated 15 June 2015, CAML transferred its debt and security interests in Tuam Ventures and Cashel Ventures to FTG Securities (the **CAML Transfer**).

<!-- al:1150 -->

\[466\] Mr Henderson produced an incomplete copy of the SCF Asset Sale Agreement. The defendants objected to the document on the ground it was hearsay. But FTG Securities was merely relying on the operative provisions of this document. This evidence was therefore not hearsay.

<!-- al:1151 -->

\[467\] The SCF Asset Sale Agreement, broadly speaking, did two things. First, in cl 2 South Canterbury Finance agreed to sell to the Crown and to transfer to CAML, amongst other things, all of its “Assets” (as defined in the Agreement). Secondly, in cl 9 South Canterbury Finance assigned to CAML all its “Assigned Property” (also defined). The definitions of “Assets” and “Assigned Property” are broad but are subject to exclusions. The exclusions are fully or partly redacted from the copy of the Sale Agreement produced by Mr Henderson. The incomplete copy of the Sale Agreement therefore does not itself prove that South Canterbury Finance’s loans to Tuam Ventures and Cashel Ventures (and the associated securities) were assigned to CAML.

<!-- al:1152 -->

\[468\] Mr Barker submitted there was other evidence demonstrating that the Tuam Ventures and Cashel Ventures loans and securities were assigned to CAML. First, he said that Mr Walker had acknowledged in the PVL proceedings that there had been an assignment to CAML. But that was true only in respect of one Tuam Ventures loan, which was recorded in the pleadings as owing to CAML. Mr Walker’s instructions to Mr Ashby did not refer to CAML in respect of any of the Tuam Ventures or Cashel Ventures loans. Overall, Mr Walker’s statements were equivocal.

<!-- al:1153 -->

\[469\] Secondly, Mr Barker submitted that CAML subsequently transferred the loans to FTG Securities (in the CAML Transfer), and that CAML would only have done so had it first received an assignment from South Canterbury Finance. I accept that to the extent the CAML Transfer purports to transfer loans and securities, it is evidence that those loans and securities were in the first place assigned to CAML from South Canterbury Finance. I therefore turn to examine which loans and securities the CAML Transfer purported to transfer.

<!-- al:1154 -->

\[470\] Under the CAML Transfer, CAML transferred to FTG Securities all its right, title and interest in the “Transferred Property”. The scope of “Transferred Property” depended on a series of definitions:

<!-- al:1155 -->

“**Borrowers**” means Tuam Ventures Limited, Cashel Ventures Limited and Anthem Holdings Limited.

<!-- al:1156 -->

...

<!-- al:1157 -->

“**Debt**” means all indebtedness of the Borrowers to CAML under the Transaction Documents that is owing at, or becomes due and payable after, the date of this deed\[.\]

<!-- al:1158 -->

...

<!-- al:1159 -->

“**Securities**” means all securities given by the Borrowers to secure the Debt including all guarantees, mortgages of land, security interests and includes all priority arrangements in relation to the securities and includes the documents set out in Schedule 1.

<!-- al:1160 -->

“**Transaction Documents**” means all loan agreements entered into by the Borrowers which have been acquired by CAML as set out in Schedule 1 and the Securities.

<!-- al:1161 -->

“**Transferred Property**” means:

<!-- al:1162 -->

> <!-- al:1163 -->
>
> (a) all the right, title and interest of CAML in the Debt;
>
> <!-- al:1164 -->
>
> (b) all the right, title and interest of CAML to, under and by virtue of the Transaction Documents;
>
> <!-- al:1165 -->
>
> (c) all rights of action and claims of CAML arising out of or relating to the Transaction Documents and the Debt; and
>
> <!-- al:1166 -->
>
> (d) all moneys payable to or to become payable to the Transferor under or by virtue of or in respect of the Transaction Documents and the Debt.

<!-- al:1167 -->

\[471\] The CAML Transfer thus distinguished, in its definition of “Transaction Documents”, between “loan agreements” (which were limited to those set out in Schedule 1) and “Securities” (which included, but were not limited to, those set out in Schedule 1).

<!-- al:1168 -->

\[472\] Schedule 1 to the CAML Transfer set out eleven documents. Some were loan agreements and some were securities. They did not include all the loans and securities relating to Tuam Ventures and Cashel Ventures that FTG Securities pleaded were ultimately assigned to it.

<!-- al:1169 -->

\[473\] Of the pleaded loans and securities relating to Tuam Ventures, Schedule 1 included:

<!-- al:1170 -->

> <!-- al:1171 -->
>
> (a) The GSA granted by Tuam Ventures to South Canterbury Finance on 7 October 2005.
>
> <!-- al:1172 -->
>
> (b) The funding approval dated 29 October 2007 between South Canterbury Finance and Tuam Ventures.
>
> <!-- al:1173 -->
>
> (c) The deed of guarantee and indemnity dated 1 November 2007 given by Cashel Ventures to South Canterbury Finance in respect of Tuam Ventures.
>
> <!-- al:1174 -->
>
> (d) The deed of priority dated 25 February 2008 between Tuam Ventures, South Canterbury Finance and the Bank of New Zealand.
>
> <!-- al:1175 -->
>
> (e) The cross-guarantee deed dated 23 December 2008 in which Tuam Ventures and Cashel Ventures (and others) granted guarantees of each other’s indebtedness to South Canterbury Finance.

<!-- al:1176 -->

\[474\] Of these, the only loan agreement was the funding approval dated 29 October 2007. I am satisfied that South Canterbury Finance’s rights under this funding approval were assigned to CAML, as otherwise CAML would not have purported to transfer it to FTG Securities. I am also satisfied that any rights that CAML had under the funding approval were assigned to FTG Securities. The

<!-- al:1177 -->

> difficulty for FTG Securities is that there is no evidence that anything was owing under the funding approval at the time of the assignment to CAML (or thereafter). As I explained earlier, Mr Henderson’s evidence was that Tuam Ventures drew down on the facility offered under the funding approval on 28 October 2008, but he did not say how much was drawn down. Nor did he say whether the draw down had or had not been repaid. He did give evidence that Tuam Ventures’ indebtedness to CAML “under various facilities” was about $6.5 million on 30 April 2015. Leaving aside the admissibility objections to that evidence, it did not identify what, if anything, was owing under the facility provided under the funding approval (as opposed to other facilities such as the loan agreement dated 10 May 2007).

<!-- al:1178 -->

\[475\] Schedule 1 did not include the loan agreement dated 10 May 2007 in which South Canterbury Finance agreed to lend $1,750,000 to Tuam Ventures. Mr Barker submitted that the 10 May 2007 loan was nonetheless transferred to FTG Securities, because it was subject to the 7 October 2005 GSA, which was in Schedule 1. He submitted that the definitions were so broad that there was a transfer of any debt that was the subject of a security set out in Schedule 1. I do not accept that. I agree the definitions are broad. But the definition of “Debt”, while it refers to indebtedness under the “Transaction Documents” (which includes the GSA), is limited to indebtedness “to CAML”. Similarly, parts (b)–(d) of the definition of “Transferred Property” are limited to rights “of CAML” or monies payable “to the Transferor” (CAML). FTG Securities therefore had to show that the 10 May 2007 loan had first been assigned to CAML (so that Tuam Ventures was indebted to CAML under the loan). There is no evidence that it had been so assigned. Indeed, the loan’s absence from Schedule 1 suggests it had not been assigned to CAML.

<!-- al:1179 -->

\[476\] Mr Barker also submitted that the effect of the 23 December 2008 cross-guarantee was to make Tuam Ventures liable for the term loan agreement entered into by Cashel Ventures on 12 August 2009 (for $27,425,000), and that the effect of CAML transferring the cross-guarantee was that this liability was now owed to FTG Securities. That may have been the effect of the cross-guarantee. But, as I explain below, there is no evidence that the 12 August 2009 loan was assigned to CAML and

<!-- al:1180 -->

> so no evidence that Cashel Ventures (and therefore Tuam Ventures) was indebted to CAML under it. I therefore reject the submission.[45](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark44?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:1181 -->

\[477\] Finally, in cl 15 of the deed of priority dated 25 February 2008, the Bank of New Zealand and South Canterbury Finance agreed they would not transfer or assign any interest in or right to their securities in Tuam Ventures unless the assignee agreed to be bound by the deed of priority. Mr Henderson explained that as South Canterbury Finance and CAML had not sought the Bank of New Zealand’s consent to either of their respective assignments, litigation arose involving all those parties. The issues between them were resolved by a deed of assignment and novation dated 19 November 2019 and a deed of settlement dated 31 May 2021. I accept that these resolved any issue that may have arisen from the non-compliance with cl 15. But the deeds did not alter the scope of the assignment to CAML or the transfer from CAML to FTG Securities.

<!-- al:1182 -->

\[478\] As to the pleaded loans and securities relating to Cashel Ventures, Schedule 1 included:

<!-- al:1183 -->

> <!-- al:1184 -->
>
> (a) The term loan agreement dated 17 October 2006 between South Canterbury Finance and Cashel Ventures.
>
> <!-- al:1185 -->
>
> (b) The deed of guarantee and indemnity dated 17 October 2006 given by PVL to South Canterbury Finance in respect of Cashel Ventures.
>
> <!-- al:1186 -->
>
> (c) The deed of guarantee and indemnity dated 1 November 2007 given by Cashel Ventures to South Canterbury Finance in respect of Tuam Ventures.
>
> <!-- al:1187 -->
>
> (d) The variation to term loan agreement dated 25 February 2008 between South Canterbury Finance and Cashel Ventures. (This variation was not pleaded, but Mr Henderson’s evidence was that it was a variation to the term loan agreement dated 17 October 2006.)

<!-- al:1188 -->

45 Below at \[480\].

<!-- al:1189 -->

> (e) The cross-guarantee deed dated 23 December 2008 in which Tuam Ventures and Cashel Ventures (and others) granted guarantees of each other’s indebtedness to South Canterbury Finance.

<!-- al:1190 -->

\[479\] Schedule 1 included only one loan agreement, that dated 17 October 2006 (and varied on 25 February 2008). I have found that this loan was repaid by the second loan agreement between Cashel Ventures and South Canterbury Finance dated 12 August 2009.[46](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark45?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) It follows that CAML had no rights under the first loan agreement. There was nothing for it to transfer to FTG Securities under that agreement.

<!-- al:1191 -->

\[480\] Schedule 1 did not refer to the second loan agreement dated 12 August 2009. Whatever rights CAML may have had under that agreement were not transferred to FTG Securities. In any event, the absence of any reference to that loan agreement indicates that it was never assigned to CAML by South Canterbury Finance in the first place. This means it is unnecessary to address the effect of cl 32 of the standard terms and conditions of the second loan agreement, the effect of which is that the lender (or an assignee) can assign their rights under the agreement only to a bank or financial institution. It suffices to say that there was no evidence that FTG Securities is a bank or financial institution, so any purported assignment by CAML would have been ineffective.

<!-- al:1192 -->

\[481\] Schedule 1 referred to the cross-guarantee dated 23 December 2008. This is of no moment, as I am not satisfied that Tuam Ventures was at any stage indebted to CAML.

<!-- al:1193 -->

\[482\] In summary, FTG Securities has not proved, on the balance of probabilities, that it is a secured creditor of Tuam Ventures or Cashel Ventures by virtue of any assignment or transfer of rights originating in South Canterbury Finance.

<!-- al:1194 -->

*FTG Securities and the DFG loan*

<!-- al:1195 -->

\[483\] In respect of the loan advanced by DFG, FTG Securities pleads that:

<!-- al:1196 -->

46 Above at \[463\].

<!-- al:1197 -->

> (a) On 1 October 2006, DFG and Cashel Ventures entered into a loan agreement under which DFG agreed to lend $8 million to Cashel Ventures.
>
> <!-- al:1198 -->
>
> (b) By a deed of assignment dated 25 June 2009, DFG assigned its debt and security interests in Cashel Ventures to South Canterbury Finance.
>
> <!-- al:1199 -->
>
> (c) On 1 June 2012, under the SCF Asset Sale Agreement, South Canterbury Finance sold its debt and security interests in Cashel Ventures to CAML.
>
> <!-- al:1200 -->
>
> (d) On 15 June 2015, under the CAML Transfer, CAML transferred its debt and security interests in Cashel Ventures to FTG Securities.

<!-- al:1201 -->

\[484\] There was no dispute that the loan agreement was entered into and that DFG subsequently assigned its debt and security interests to South Canterbury Finance on 25 June 2009.

<!-- al:1202 -->

\[485\] Issue was taken with whether South Canterbury Finance sold that debt and those security interests to CAML under the SCF Asset Sale Agreement and whether CAML subsequently transferred them to FTG Securities under the CAML Transfer. As for the SCF Asset Sale Agreement, the problem (as already explained) is that FTG Securities produced an incomplete copy. It does not prove that the debt originally owed to DFG was assigned to CAML. As for the CAML Transfer, Schedule 1 does not refer either to the original loan agreement dated 1 October 2006 or the deed of assignment from DFG to South Canterbury Finance dated 25 June 2008. This suggests that loan was never assigned to CAML in the first place. Even if it was, CAML did not transfer its rights under the loan (or any associated security) to FTG Securities.

<!-- al:1203 -->

\[486\] In closing submissions, FTG Securities submitted that the DFG debt was owed under the cross-guarantee that had been entered into on 23 December 2008. This had not been pleaded and was not addressed by the defendants in closing. I reject the submission. DFG was not a party to the cross-guarantee.

<!-- al:1204 -->

\[487\] FTG Securities has therefore not proved that it is a secured creditor of Cashel Ventures by virtue of any assignment or transfer of rights originating in DFG.

<!-- al:1205 -->

*RFD Finance Ltd*

<!-- al:1206 -->

\[488\] RFD Finance Ltd (**RFD Finance**) claims to be a secured creditor of three PVL subsidiaries, Castle Street Ventures Ltd (**Castle Street Ventures**), Tay Ventures Ltd (**Tay Ventures**) and Livingspace Properties Ltd (**Livingspace**), by way of assignment of loans advanced to those subsidiaries by Equitable Life Insurance Ltd (**Equitable Life**).

<!-- al:1207 -->

*RFD Finance and Castle Street Ventures*

<!-- al:1208 -->

\[489\] In respect of Castle Street Ventures, RFD Finance claims that:[47](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark46?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:1209 -->

> <!-- al:1210 -->
>
> (a) On 11 September 2006:
>
> <!-- al:1211 -->
>
> (i) Equitable Life and Castle Street Ventures entered into a development funding facility for up to $8,500,000, guaranteed by Mr Henderson, PVL and Livingspace.
>
> <!-- al:1212 -->
>
> (ii) Castle Street Ventures granted a GSA to Equitable Life.
>
> <!-- al:1213 -->
>
> (iii) A deed of guarantee and indemnity was entered into in respect of Castle Street Ventures between Equitable Life, Mr Henderson, PVL and Castle Street Ventures.
>
> <!-- al:1214 -->
>
> (b) On 11 December 2006, a deed of priority was entered into between Castle Street Ventures, Equitable Life and DFG.
>
> <!-- al:1215 -->
>
> (c) Equitable Life subsequently assigned the Castle Street Ventures loan (that is, under the development funding facility) to Equitable Property Holdings Ltd (**Equitable Property**).

<!-- al:1216 -->

1.  This reflects the case put at trial. RFD Finance had pleaded that Equitable Property was the original lender and had not pleaded any assignment from Equitable Life to Equitable Property.

<!-- al:1217 -->

> (d) On 14 February 2014, by a deed of assignment and settlement, Equitable Property assigned its debt and security interests in the Castle Street Ventures loan to RFD Finance (the **Equitable Property Assignment**).

<!-- al:1218 -->

\[490\] There was no real dispute that the transactions at (a) and (b) above were entered into. It is notable, however, that cl 8.2 of the development funding facility provided that Equitable Life would assign its rights under the facility to its trustee (defined as Trustees Executors Ltd) on the first drawdown date. Clause 8.6 provided that Equitable Life could not assign its rights to any person other than that trustee.

<!-- al:1219 -->

\[491\] There was a dispute as to whether Equitable Life assigned its rights under the Castle Street Ventures loan to Equitable Property. Mr Henderson’s evidence of this was a bald assertion that Equitable Life had “subsequently assigned” the Castle Street Ventures loan “to its trustee company, \[Equitable Property\]”. RFD Finance never pleaded this assignment. It pleaded that the loan had been from Equitable Property from the outset. Mr Henderson did not produce any documentary evidence of the assignment. The asserted assignment is contrary to the provisions of the development funding facility, which provided that Equitable Life would assign its rights to Trustees Executors Ltd and could not assign to anyone else. I note also that appendix E to the consolidated statement of claim in the PVL proceedings claimed that the lender was Equitable Life. Mr Walker’s instructions to Mr Ashby were the same.

<!-- al:1220 -->

\[492\] In closing, Mr Barker submitted that Equitable Property was a nominee company of Equitable Life. There was no evidence of that. He also submitted that financing statements registered on the PPSR were consistent with the Castle Street Ventures GSA (and therefore the debt) being held by Equitable Property. To the extent RFD Finance relies on these financing statements to prove that Equitable Property held the GSA and therefore the debt at a particular date, the evidence is hearsay and inadmissible. Even if they were to be admitted, I would give them little weight. Given that the alleged assignment was contrary to the provisions of the loan and there is no documentary evidence of an assignment (or of a notice of assignment to Castle Street Ventures), I am not satisfied that it took place.

<!-- al:1221 -->

\[493\] There was also a dispute as to the alleged assignment from Equitable Property (assuming, contrary to my finding, that it had taken an assignment from Equitable Life) to RFD Finance under the Equitable Property Assignment dated 14 February 2014. LPF submitted that, contrary to Mr Henderson’s evidence, the Equitable Property Assignment effected an assignment only of Livingspace’s liability as guarantor of the loans to Castle Street Ventures and Tay Ventures. LPF acknowledged that in cl 2.6 Equitable Property agreed that, following the retirement of receivers from Livingspace, Castle Street Ventures and Tay Ventures, it would assign any remaining debt owing by Castle Street Ventures or Tay Ventures to RFD Finance. But LPF said there was no evidence that the assignment contemplated by cl 2.6 had occurred.

<!-- al:1222 -->

\[494\] As I understood its closing, RFD Finance accepted that the deed contemplated an initial assignment of Livingspace’s indebtedness followed by a later assignment of the indebtedness of Castle Street Ventures and Tay Ventures. It submitted that the second assignment had occurred automatically on the retirement of the receivers on 20 February 2018.

<!-- al:1223 -->

\[495\] This was different to RFD Finance’s pleaded case and the case on which it opened (which was that the assignment occurred on or shortly after the deed of 14 February 2014). In any event, I do not accept that any assignment occurred automatically on the retirement of the receivers. Clause 2.6 provided that there would be further steps taken by both Equitable Property and RFD Finance to effect the assignment. There was no evidence those steps were taken.

<!-- al:1224 -->

\[496\] Finally, Mr Barker submitted that an issue estoppel arose from the decision of Judge Johnston in *100 Investments Ltd v Walker*, by which Mr Walker was prevented from denying that there had been assignments to Equitable Property and then to RFD Finance.[48](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark47?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) I dealt with much the same submission in relation to 100 Investments and so I can state my reasons briefly. In this proceeding Mr Walker is sued in his personal capacity, whereas in *100 Investments Ltd v Walker* he was sued as liquidator of PVL. No issue estoppel arises.

<!-- al:1225 -->

48 *100 Investments Ltd v Walker*, above n 41.

<!-- al:1226 -->

*RFD Finance and Tay Ventures*

<!-- al:1227 -->

\[497\] In respect of Tay Ventures, RFD Finance claims:

<!-- al:1228 -->

> <!-- al:1229 -->
>
> (a) On 19 September 2006:
>
> <!-- al:1230 -->
>
> (i) Equitable Life and Tay Ventures entered into a loan agreement for up to $4,200,000, guaranteed by Mr Henderson, PVL and Livingspace.
>
> <!-- al:1231 -->
>
> (ii) Tay Ventures granted a GSA to Equitable Life.
>
> <!-- al:1232 -->
>
> (iii) A deed of guarantee and indemnity was entered into in respect of Tay Ventures between Equitable Life, Mr Henderson, PVL and Tay Ventures.
>
> <!-- al:1233 -->
>
> (iv) A deed of priority was entered into between Tay Ventures, Equitable Life and DFG.
>
> <!-- al:1234 -->
>
> (b) Equitable Life subsequently assigned the Tay Ventures loan to Equitable Property.
>
> <!-- al:1235 -->
>
> (c) On 14 February 2014, under the Equitable Property Assignment, Equitable Property assigned its debt and security interests in the Tay Ventures loan to RFD Finance.

<!-- al:1236 -->

\[498\] There was no real dispute that the transactions at (a) above were entered into. However, an issue arose as to the terms of the 19 September 2006 loan agreement. The loan agreement states that it includes the “Facility Terms and Conditions” but these were not included within the copy of the agreement produced by Mr Henderson. Mr Henderson was unable to say where they were. Mr Cooper submitted it was therefore impossible to tell whether the loan agreement contained a restriction on assignment (in the way that the Castle Street Ventures loan agreement did). Mr Barker, on the other hand, submitted there was no evidence suggesting that there were any restrictions on assignment.

<!-- al:1237 -->

\[499\] I consider it likely, and I infer, that the missing terms and conditions in the Tay Ventures loan agreement did contain the same restriction on assignment as found in the Castle Street Ventures loan agreement. Clause 10 of the loan schedule in the Tay Ventures loan agreement provided:

<!-- al:1238 -->

**Transfer to Trustee:** All Equitable’s rights, title, entitlements and interests under the Facility Terms (including this Facility Agreement and the mortgage(s) specified in \[the\] Securities Schedule) will be transferred and assigned to the Trustee on the Date of Advance and the provisions specified in 5.1 to 5.6 of the Conditions will then apply.

<!-- al:1239 -->

\[500\] The “Conditions” referred to in cl 10 were defined as the “Facility Terms and Conditions” (the ones missing from the agreement produced by Mr Henderson). Given that cl 10 provided for an assignment to a trustee in the same way as did the Castle Street Ventures loan agreement (entered into with the same lender just eight days earlier), it is likely that cls 5.1–5.6 of the Facility Terms and Conditions were to the same or similar effect as the assignment provisions in the Castle Street Ventures loan agreement.

<!-- al:1240 -->

\[501\] There was a dispute as to whether Equitable Life assigned its rights under the Tay Ventures loan to Equitable Property, and a dispute as to the alleged assignment from Equitable Property to RFD Finance under the Equitable Property Assignment. The issues and submissions were the same ones that arose in relation to Castle Street Ventures. My conclusions (and reasons) are the same. I am not satisfied there was an assignment from Equitable Life to Equitable Property, nor (if I am wrong on that) from Equitable Property to RFD Finance.

<!-- al:1241 -->

*RFD Finance and Livingspace*

<!-- al:1242 -->

\[502\] RFD Finance’s pleading in respect of Livingspace was short. It pleaded that:

<!-- al:1243 -->

> <!-- al:1244 -->
>
> (a) On 19 October 2009, Livingspace granted a GSA to Secured Finance Ltd and Secured Lending Ltd and a financing statement in respect of the GSA was registered.
>
> <!-- al:1245 -->
>
> (b) On 18 March 2010, Livingspace granted a GSA in favour of Equitable Life.

<!-- al:1246 -->

\[503\] RFD Finance did not plead that either of these GSAs had been assigned to it.

<!-- al:1247 -->

\[504\] Mr Henderson gave evidence that Livingspace had granted the GSA to Secured Finance Ltd and Secured Lending Ltd on 19 October 2009. He did not give any evidence of that GSA having been assigned to RFD Finance. In their closing submissions the 100 Parties did not refer to this GSA. I find that RFD Finance has no rights under it.

<!-- al:1248 -->

\[505\] Mr Henderson produced the other GSA, dated 18 March 2010. He correctly said it was granted in favour of Equitable Property (not Equitable Life, as pleaded). The defendants objected to this document as hearsay but did not question its authenticity. It was not hearsay, as the 100 Parties were producing it merely to prove the transaction effected by the GSA.

<!-- al:1249 -->

\[506\] Mr Henderson then said that the Livingspace “debts and securities” were assigned to RFD Finance as part of the Equitable Property Assignment dated 14 February 2014. He did not specify which debts and securities he was referring to. This became clear in closing submissions. RFD Finance’s case was that there had been an assignment to it of Equitable Property’s rights against Livingspace under:

<!-- al:1250 -->

> <!-- al:1251 -->
>
> (a) the guarantees that Livingspace had given in the Castle Street Ventures development funding facility dated 11 September 2006 and the Tay Ventures loan agreement dated 19 September 2006; and
>
> <!-- al:1252 -->
>
> (b) the GSA that Livingspace granted to Equitable Property on 18 March 2010.

<!-- al:1253 -->

\[507\] All three of these documents are identified as “Transaction Documents” in the Schedule to the Equitable Property Assignment. The GSA had been granted to Equitable Property, so that entity was able to assign it to RFD Finance. But Livingspace had given the guarantees to Equitable Life, not to Equitable Property. RFD Finance did not plead that the guarantees had been assigned from Equitable Life to Equitable Property and did not lead any evidence of such an assignment. As noted,

<!-- al:1254 -->

> Mr Henderson made a bald assertion that the “loans” had been assigned to Equitable Property, but he did not assert that the guarantees had been assigned.

<!-- al:1255 -->

\[508\] I am, therefore, not satisfied that the guarantees were assigned to RFD Finance. There was an assignment of the GSA, but there are no underlying obligations in favour of RFD Finance secured by that GSA.

<!-- al:1256 -->

*Tomanovich Holdings Ltd*

<!-- al:1257 -->

\[509\] Tomanovich Holdings Ltd (**Tomanovich Holdings**) claims to be a secured creditor of St Asaph Ventures Ltd (**St Asaph Ventures**), by way of an assignment from Perpetual Trust Ltd (**Perpetual Trust)** and Marac Finance Ltd (**Marac Finance**). Tomanovich Holdings pleads:

<!-- al:1258 -->

> <!-- al:1259 -->
>
> (a) On 2 May 2007:
>
> <!-- al:1260 -->
>
> (i) St Asaph Ventures granted a GSA to Perpetual Trust and Marac Finance.
>
> <!-- al:1261 -->
>
> (ii) St Asaph Ventures and Perpetual Trust entered into a project facility agreement for $6,000,000, guaranteed by Mr Henderson and PVL.
>
> <!-- al:1262 -->
>
> (iii) A deed of guarantee and indemnity was entered into in respect of St Asaph Ventures between Mr Henderson and PVL as guarantors and Perpetual Trust and Marac Finance as lenders.
>
> <!-- al:1263 -->
>
> (b) On 30 October 2009, Perpetual Trust and Marac Finance assigned their debt and security interests in St Asaph Ventures to Real Estate Credit Ltd (**RECL**).
>
> <!-- al:1264 -->
>
> (c) On 29 June 2010, RECL and Tomanovich Holdings entered into a deed of assignment and transfer of RECL’s debt and security interests in St Asaph Ventures.

<!-- al:1265 -->

\[510\] The defendants did not dispute that the transactions in (a) occurred. Nor was there any real issue about the second assignment from RECL to Tomanovich Holdings.

<!-- al:1266 -->

\[511\] What was in issue was whether there had been an initial assignment from Perpetual Trust and Marac Finance to RECL. The alleged assignment document was not produced. Mr Henderson said in evidence that “\[d\]espite requests for this document, we have never received a copy”.

<!-- al:1267 -->

\[512\] The deed of assignment and transfer between RECL and Tomanovich Holdings records that Tomanovich Holdings was to pay a purchase price to RECL (though the price is redacted). It is surprising that Tomanovich Holdings would pay for an assignment from RECL without first obtaining a copy of the assignment showing that RECL had itself obtained the debt and security interests in St Asaph Ventures. It called for some explanation. Mr Henderson, who signed the deed of assignment on behalf of Tomanovich Holdings, provided none. In closing, the 100 Parties offered some sort of explanation by saying that RECL was a wholly owned subsidiary of Marac Finance. They referred to a Companies Register search. As far as I can recall, the search was never put in evidence.
