Even if it was, it shows that RECL is a wholly owned subsidiary of a different company (Marac Financial Services Ltd), shows the shareholding in RECL as at 28 February 2024 (not as at the date of the alleged assignment, which was almost 15 years earlier, on 30 October 2009) and indicated no relationship between RECL and the other original lender, Perpetual Trust.

<!-- al:1268 -->

\[513\] Mr Henderson was not a party to the alleged assignment to RECL. His evidence that there was such an assignment to RECL was hearsay as the 100 Parties did not call any witness from RECL, Marac Finance or Perpetual Trust. The assignment from RECL to Tomanovich Holdings recites that there was an assignment to RECL, but that recital is also hearsay.

<!-- al:1269 -->

\[514\] The 100 Parties submitted that there was other evidence to support the assignment to RECL. First, they referred to the registration of financing statements on the PPSR. These are hearsay to the extent that the 100 Parties rely on them to prove that RECL held the GSA at a particular date (and, by inference, that there had been an assignment to RECL).

<!-- al:1270 -->

\[515\] Secondly, the 100 Parties submitted that “Mr Walker himself had determined that there had been a transfer and \[pursued\] the debt”. They referred to an undated draft memorandum that Mr Walker prepared sometime between 2015 and 2018. In the memorandum, Mr Walker noted that Marac Finance “had been subsumed into Heartland Bank and they are less than co-operative, partially as a result of disarray in their record keeping”. This says nothing about an assignment from Marac Finance and Perpetual Trust to RECL. It does not support the 100 Parties’ submission. I note also that the pleadings in the PVL proceedings, and Mr Walker’s instructions to Mr Ashby, refer to the lender to St Asaph Ventures being simply “Marac”, not RECL.

<!-- al:1271 -->

\[516\] For these reasons, Tomanovich Holdings has not proved, on the balance of probabilities, that it is a secured creditor of St Asaph Ventures.

<!-- al:1272 -->

*Summary*

<!-- al:1273 -->

\[517\] None of the 100 Parties have proved that they are secured creditors of any PVL subsidiary.

<!-- al:1274 -->

\[518\] This conclusion means it is not necessary for me to address LPF’s contention that the 100 Parties had surrendered any securities they might have held in the PVL subsidiaries.

<!-- al:1275 -->

**Issue 7: If the 100 Parties are secured creditors of the PVL subsidiaries, what portion of the settlement proceeds are the PVL subsidiaries entitled to?**

<!-- al:1276 -->

\[519\] If, contrary to the conclusion I have just reached, the 100 Parties are secured creditors of PVL subsidiaries, how much of the proceeds from the PVL proceedings are subject to their securities? That turns on two issues. First, what portion, if any, of the settlement proceeds are the PVL subsidiaries entitled to? Secondly, to what extent does Mr Walker enjoy a right to recoup from the PVL subsidiaries’ portion of the proceeds, in priority to any claims by the 100 Parties as secured creditors of the subsidiaries, some or all of his costs and remuneration in obtaining those proceeds?

<!-- al:1277 -->

\[520\] Strictly speaking I do not have to decide these issues. But it is appropriate that I decide them, as they were the focus of much evidence and argument. I deal with the first issue here and the second in the next section of this judgment.

<!-- al:1278 -->

\[521\] Mr Barker submitted that part of the settlement proceeds included funds that belonged to the PVL subsidiaries as a consequence of settlement of claims on their behalf. He said the subsidiaries’ share of the proceeds should depend on their respective contributions to the proceeds. He said that the PVL proceedings depended on the idea that PVL and its subsidiaries were allowed to continue to trade after the point in time when their businesses should have been brought to an end, with the resultant loss being the additional indebtedness they incurred during that time. The same loss was seen in the PVL subsidiaries and in PVL (via the guarantees it had given of each subsidiary’s indebtedness). Each subsidiary’s share was, he submitted, therefore best assessed by looking at the indebtedness of the relevant subsidiaries (those over which the 100 Parties claimed to be secured creditors) as a proportion of the total indebtedness of the PVL subsidiaries, which he said was 20 per cent.

<!-- al:1279 -->

\[522\] Mr Salmon made the principal submissions for the defendants on this issue. He said the 100 Parties bore the burden of proving that the PVL subsidiaries were entitled to a portion of the settlement proceeds. He submitted they had failed to discharge that burden.

<!-- al:1280 -->

*Principles governing the allocation of the settlement proceeds*

<!-- al:1281 -->

\[523\] Only Mr Barker made submissions on the principles governing the allocation of the settlement proceeds. He said the settlement proceeds constituted a mixed fund to which PVL and each of the PVL subsidiaries had, through settlement of their respective claims, made contributions. He said the allocation principles were found in cases dealing with the allocation of mixed funds of money, such as *Foskett v McKeown* and *Loo v Quinlan*[.49](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark48?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) In the former, Lord Browne-Wilkinson said that where a trustee mixes their own money with trust moneys, the balance belongs to “the trustee and the beneficiaries rateably according to their respective contributions”.[50](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark49?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) In

<!-- al:1282 -->

49 *Foskett v McKeown* [\[2000\] UKHL 29](http://www.bailii.org/uk/cases/UKHL/2000/29.html "View Case"); \[2001\] 1 AC 102 (HL); and *Loo v Quinlan* [\[2021\] NZCA 561.](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/cases/NZCA/2021/561.html "View Case")

<!-- al:1283 -->

50 *Foskett v McKeown*, above n 49, at 110.

<!-- al:1284 -->

> the latter, the Court of Appeal said that in order to distribute a deficient mixed fund, “it is necessary to value the relative contributions” of each of the contributors.[51](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark50?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:1285 -->

\[524\] I accept that these general principles apply to allocating the settlement proceeds between PVL and the PVL subsidiaries. The settlement proceeds constitute a mixed fund, in the sense that the proceeds are at least arguably the result of contributions by not only PVL but also the PVL subsidiaries. I acknowledge that, unlike *Foskett v McKeown* or *Loo v Quinlan*, the fund did not derive from simple monetary contributions. I consider that this does not mean that a different allocation principle applies. It just means that applying the principle is more complex.

<!-- al:1286 -->

\[525\] Mr Barker’s submissions on how the principle should be applied in this case tended to focus solely on the quantum of the respective claims of PVL and the PVL subsidiaries. I consider, with respect, that approach to be too simplistic. To assess each claimant’s relative contribution to the settlement proceeds, it is necessary to investigate what caused the defendants to pay the proceeds. That will involve, at the least, consideration of both the quantum of each claim and each claim’s relative strength.

<!-- al:1287 -->

*Fright Aubrey settlement proceeds*

<!-- al:1288 -->

\[526\] Mr Barker noted that the claims against Fright Aubrey were on behalf of both PVL and the PVL subsidiaries, that the PVL subsidiaries were parties to the settlement with Fright Aubrey and that there was no allocation in that settlement between PVL and its subsidiaries. He submitted these settlement proceeds should be apportioned by reference to the relative indebtedness of each subsidiary.

<!-- al:1289 -->

\[527\] The settlement with Fright Aubrey was complicated by two factors. First, the receivers of DFG had made a claim against Fright Aubrey in respect of two valuations that were also the subject of the claims in the PVL proceedings: a valuation for Five Mile and a valuation for Kapiti Ventures Ltd (**Kapiti Ventures**). Secondly, Fright Aubrey was in liquidation, and the only way in which DFG or the PVL parties could

<!-- al:1290 -->

51 *Loo v Quinlan*, above n 49, at \[44\].

<!-- al:1291 -->

> recover was through a claim against Five Mile’s insurer, QBE Insurance (International) Ltd (**QBE**), under [s 9](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/lra1936123/s9.html) of the [Law Reform Act 1936](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/lra1936123/index.html) (since repealed).

<!-- al:1292 -->

\[528\] These factors had two consequences. First, DFG and PVL negotiated an agreement as to how they would allocate any settlement proceeds received from Fright Aubrey and QBE. Secondly, both DFG and PVL (and its subsidiaries) were parties to the Fright Aubrey settlement, as was QBE.

<!-- al:1293 -->

\[529\] Under the settlement, QBE agreed to pay a sum in full and final settlement of DFG’s claims and the claims of PVL and its subsidiaries. The parties agreed to allocate the settlement sum to the valuations for Five Mile and Kapiti Ventures.

<!-- al:1294 -->

\[530\] There is nothing to suggest that that allocation was a contrivance. It appears to have reflected the parties’ assessments of the merits of the claim against Fright Aubrey and QBE. DFG’s claims were only in respect of those valuations. In the PVL proceedings, the consolidated statement of claim made specific allegations in respect of the Five Mile and Kapiti Ventures valuations and then more generic allegations in respect of other valuations. In respect of an earlier version of the pleading, Mr Tim Smith commented that some of the valuations were included in the pleading merely to avoid expiry of limitation periods and “without any real evidence of negligence”. PVL had also brought a separate proceeding against Fright Aubrey in respect of the Kapiti Ventures valuation. That proceeding was also settled in the Fright Aubrey settlement.

<!-- al:1295 -->

\[531\] In these circumstances I consider that the only parties that made a contribution to the Fright Aubrey settlement proceeds were those PVL companies that had claimed losses in respect of the Five Mile and Kapiti Ventures valuations. Those companies do not include any of the PVL subsidiaries over which the 100 Parties claim to have a security interest.

<!-- al:1296 -->

\[532\] In reaching that conclusion, I acknowledge that all the PVL subsidiaries were parties to the settlement. But it does not follow that they all made valuable contributions to the settlement payment. It is unusual for defendants facing claims from multiple plaintiffs to agree to pay money in settlement only on the basis that all

<!-- al:1297 -->

> plaintiffs agree to a full and final settlement. This does not mean that all settling plaintiffs contributed value to the settlement.

<!-- al:1298 -->

\[533\] It follows that the 100 Parties, even if they had established that they were secured creditors of the PVL subsidiaries, would not have any security interest in the Fright Aubrey settlement proceeds of $2,180,286.

<!-- al:1299 -->

*PwC settlement proceeds*

<!-- al:1300 -->

\[534\] The PwC settlement agreement did not attempt to allocate the settlement payment to any particular PVL company. In determining whether any of the PVL subsidiaries made a contribution to the settlement proceeds, and if so the relative size of the contribution, I will look at how the claims were advanced against PwC in the period leading up to settlement and consider the relative merits of the claims made by PVL and the PVL subsidiaries against PwC.

<!-- al:1301 -->

\[535\] The key negotiations that led to the settlement with PwC occurred from May to August 2017. When those negotiations commenced, the claims were found in a consolidated statement of claim dated 11 July 2014. This pleaded two causes of action against PwC. One was by PVL alleging PwC had breached contract by failing to exercise due care in its audit. After pleading the alleged breaches, PVL claimed that had PwC exercised due care, PVL and its subsidiaries would have been placed into liquidation on an earlier date (April 2007). PVL claimed as damages the deterioration in its financial position from April 2007. PVL claimed this amount was $302.7 million up to July 2010, with interest continuing to accrue.

<!-- al:1302 -->

\[536\] The other cause of action was by PVL and the PVL subsidiaries, alleging a breach of a duty of care in tort. This cause of action relied on the same alleged breaches set out in PVL’s cause of action in contract and pleaded the same causal consequences. PVL and the PVL subsidiaries sought judgment for the sum of

<!-- al:1303 -->

> $302.7 million or, alternatively, “judgments for the sums lost by each of the Subsidiary Plaintiffs as set out at Appendix K”. Appendix K listed losses for four subsidiaries, only two of which (Castle Street Ventures and Livingspace) are subject to the 100 Parties’ alleged securities. The financial deterioration of the four subsidiaries from
>
> <!-- al:1304 -->
>
> 2007 until 2010 was claimed to be about $64 million, most of which ($50 million) was for Five Mile.

<!-- al:1305 -->

\[537\] An amended consolidated statement of claim was filed on 30 June 2017, which was in the middle of the negotiations with PwC. It did not materially alter the causes of action that I have just outlined.

<!-- al:1306 -->

\[538\] Meanwhile, on 13 June 2017, Mr Newland had provided draft loss calculations to PwC from PVL’s expert, Mr Ashby. Mr Ashby then finalised his loss calculations in a brief dated 28 July 2017. He assessed the financial deterioration caused by the ongoing trading of PVL and its subsidiaries from 2007 to February 2018 (the scheduled trial date) as being $625 million.

<!-- al:1307 -->

\[539\] Mr Ashby’s brief was in evidence before me, but he was not called as a witness. The 100 Parties relied on his brief in support of their claim that the PVL subsidiaries had a share of the PwC settlement proceeds. The defendants, by contrast, noted that although Mr Ashby’s brief was not subject to challenge by PwC’s experts (given the settlement) it was challenged by the defendant directors’ experts. The defendants said that because the defendant directors’ experts’ evidence was not before me, Mr Ashby’s brief was not tested. They therefore submitted it was not a reliable source of data from which to assess the value of the PVL subsidiaries’ claims.

<!-- al:1308 -->

\[540\] I consider that Mr Ashby’s brief is relevant to an assessment of the contribution that the PVL subsidiaries made to the PwC settlement proceeds. It shows how the plaintiffs in the PVL proceedings were putting forward and quantifying their claims to PwC at the time of the settlement.

<!-- al:1309 -->

\[541\] That said, Mr Ashby’s brief is of limited assistance to my present enquiry. That is because he assessed the financial deterioration on a PVL group basis. He did not make that assessment for PVL itself or for each PVL subsidiary. It is possible to use his figure of $625 million as a reliable guide for the assessed financial deterioration of PVL itself, given that PVL guaranteed most or all of the debts of the PVL subsidiaries. But that guide does not work for the PVL subsidiaries themselves.

<!-- al:1310 -->

\[542\] Mr Barker submitted that I could use the projected loan balances contained in Mr Ashby’s analysis as a guide to the financial deterioration suffered by each PVL subsidiary. As part of Mr Ashby’s estimate of net financial realisations for the PVL group as at 28 February 2018, he projected the loan balances for each PVL subsidiary as at that date. The loan balances totalled $673 million. Mr Barker calculated, using Mr Ashby’s figures, that the PVL subsidiaries over which the 100 Parties claim to have securities had loan balances of $139 million, or 20 per cent of the total. He submitted that 20 per cent of the PwC settlement proceeds (and the Fright Aubrey and directors settlement proceeds) therefore properly belonged to those PVL subsidiaries.

<!-- al:1311 -->

\[543\] I consider that Mr Ashby’s projected loan balances provide only a very crude guide to the claimed financial deterioration of each PVL subsidiary. They could only be a reliable guide if it could be safely assumed that each PVL subsidiary had suffered the same financial deterioration, relative to its financial position in 2007, as every other subsidiary. I was given no basis on which to make such an assumption.

<!-- al:1312 -->

\[544\] Even if the projected loan balances were a reliable guide as to the claimed financial deterioration of each PVL subsidiary over which securities are claimed, it would not follow that 20 per cent of the settlement proceeds belonged to those PVL subsidiaries. Mr Barker’s analysis ignored the quantum of the claim by PVL itself, which essentially matched that of the PVL group. Obviously, the PVL subsidiaries and PVL could not recover twice for what was the same underlying loss, but that does not mean that the subsidiaries would have recovered to the exclusion of PVL. If anything is to be taken from Mr Ashby’s projected loan balances, it is that the PVL subsidiaries that were subject to the 100 Parties’ alleged securities were claiming about 10 per cent of the overall loss.

<!-- al:1313 -->

\[545\] Allowance must also be made for the relative strength of each claim. The defendants submitted that the PVL subsidiaries’ claims were meritless and therefore had no value. They said the subsidiaries were added to the proceedings only at a time when it was considered that PVL faced a formidable limitation problem arising from PwC’s terms of engagement, which required a proceeding to be brought within four years rather than the usual six years. They said it was subsequently realised that the [Securities Act 1978](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/repealed_act.20250905.OLD/sa1978139/index.html) (since repealed) made such a limitation provision ineffective. The

<!-- al:1314 -->

> defendants said that once that limitation issue was cleared away, the only claim on which real weight was placed was that advanced by PVL.

<!-- al:1315 -->

\[546\] I accept that well before the settlement with PwC, PVL and its advisers had come to the view that PVL did not face a limitation hurdle from PwC’s terms of engagement. But, as Mr Barker submitted, that was merely something that went to the strength of PVL’s claim. It did not mean that the claims of the subsidiaries had no merit or made no contribution to the settlement with PwC. While the subsidiaries’ claims were an afterthought, they were not a makeweight. Had they been a makeweight, I expect I would have seen contemporaneous communications to that effect in the period leading up to settlement. But I saw none. By that time, the liquidators and their advisers were aware that Mr Henderson’s interests were likely to make a claim to an interest in the settlement proceeds (essentially the claim advanced in these proceedings). The liquidators and their advisers turned their minds to ways in which the PVL subsidiaries could be cut out of the PwC settlement so as to make it more difficult for Mr Henderson’s interests to advance such a claim. Their actions are more consistent with the subsidiaries’ claims having some value than no value.

<!-- al:1316 -->

\[547\] However, I do consider that the claims of the subsidiaries were not as strong as the claim of PVL. PVL engaged PwC and therefore had a claim in contract. The subsidiaries did not engage PwC. They had to establish a duty of care in tort. That was not an immaterial hurdle. They also faced a limitation issue that PVL did not. There was a risk that PwC might be able to rely on the limitation clause in its terms of engagement against the subsidiaries. If so, the subsidiaries could not have relied on the relevant [Securities Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/repealed_act.20250905.OLD/sa1978139/index.html) provision (as that was available only to a public issuer such as PVL).

<!-- al:1317 -->

\[548\] There is one final matter that I take into account. As between the PVL subsidiaries, it appears that some had stronger claims against PwC than others. Only four of the subsidiaries were listed in Appendix K to the consolidated statement of claim. Of those, Five Mile was by far the most prominent. Mr Smith KC’s evidence was that he considered Fright Aubrey’s negligent valuation of Five Mile’s land to be at the heart of the case against PwC. Mr Tim Smith had expressed the same view in

<!-- al:1318 -->

> an email dated 15 October 2014. The 100 Parties do not claim to have a security over Five Mile’s assets.

<!-- al:1319 -->

\[549\] Taking all these matters into account, I consider that the PVL subsidiaries over which the 100 Parties claim securities made more than a nominal contribution to the PwC settlement. However, keeping in mind that the onus is on the 100 Parties and also the uncertainty over quantum, the relative contribution has only been shown to be very modest. I consider it to be in the region of four per cent of the settlement proceeds, or $1,352,000.

<!-- al:1320 -->

*Directors settlement proceeds*

<!-- al:1321 -->

\[550\] When the separate proceeding on behalf of the PVL subsidiaries was commenced, it was brought only against PwC and Fright Aubrey and not against the directors. When the proceedings were consolidated, six causes of action were pleaded against the directors. They were brought by the first to third plaintiffs: Mr Walker and Mr Scutter (as liquidators of PVL and four PVL subsidiaries, two of which are subject to the 100 Parties’ alleged securities), PVL and Five Mile. The causes of action claimed that the directors were liable to PVL or to “the plaintiffs”, which I take to mean (in those causes of action) the first to third plaintiffs. None of the causes of action were brought in the name of any of the PVL subsidiaries over which the 100 Parties claim to have securities.

<!-- al:1322 -->

\[551\] The PVL subsidiaries therefore had almost no part to play in the claims against the directors. Mr Barker did not explain what contribution the PVL subsidiaries had made to the settlement with the directors, other than noting that all the PVL subsidiaries were parties to the settlement and that the PVL subsidiaries were responsible for about 20 per cent of Mr Ashby’s projected loan balances. I consider that more needed to be done to establish that the PVL subsidiaries subject to the 100 Parties’ alleged securities made more than a nominal contribution to the directors settlement.

<!-- al:1323 -->

*Summary*

<!-- al:1324 -->

\[552\] If I had found the 100 Parties to be secured creditors of the PVL subsidiaries, I would have found $1,352,000 of the settlement proceeds from the PVL proceedings to be subject to their securities.

<!-- al:1325 -->

**Issue 8: If the 100 Parties are secured creditors of the PVL subsidiaries, to what extent does Mr Walker enjoy a prior right to recoup from the PVL settlement proceeds his costs and remuneration in obtaining those proceeds?**

<!-- al:1326 -->

\[553\] It is common ground that Mr Walker had a right to recoup from the proceeds, in priority to any claims by the 100 Parties as secured creditors of the PVL subsidiaries, some or all of his costs and remuneration in obtaining those proceeds. However, the extent of Mr Walker’s right was very much in dispute.

<!-- al:1327 -->

*A liquidator’s right to recoup costs and remuneration in priority to a secured creditor*

<!-- al:1328 -->

\[554\] Where a liquidator has incurred costs and performed services in preserving and realising secured assets, the liquidator is entitled to recoup their reasonable costs and reasonable remuneration for their services from the proceeds of realisation in priority to the claim of the secured creditor. This is a long-standing principle.[52](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark51?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:1329 -->

\[555\] This principle is sometimes referred to as the “salvage principle”.[53](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark52?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) The parties used that terminology. I will not. In particular, I decline Mr Barker’s invitation to map the bounds of the principle by reference to the law of salvage in admiralty. The admiralty analogy is inapt. Secured assets (and, in this case, causes of action) are not ships in distress. I prefer to draw on leading statements of the principle in the liquidation context in which it has been recognised and applied.

<!-- al:1330 -->

\[556\] The most authoritative discussion of the principle is found in a decision of the High Court of Australia, *Stewart v Atco Controls Pty Ltd (in liq)*[.54](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark53?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) The Court surveyed

<!-- al:1331 -->

52 *Buchler v Talbot* [\[2004\] UKHL 9](http://www.bailii.org/uk/cases/UKHL/2004/9.html "View Case"), \[2004\] 2 AC 298 at [\[19\]](http://www.bailii.org/uk/cases/UKHL/2004/9.html#para19) per Lord Nicholls, \[31\] per Lord Hoffmann and \[63\] per Lord Millett. The nineteenth-century Chancery cases from which the principle derives, and other authorities, are discussed by the High Court of Australia in *Stewart v Atco Controls Pty Ltd (in liq)* [\[2014\] HCA 15](http://www.austlii.edu.au/au/cases/cth/HCA/2014/15.html "View Case"), (2014) 252 CLR 307 at [\[11\]](http://www.austlii.edu.au/au/cases/cth/HCA/2014/15.html#para11)–\[21\].

<!-- al:1332 -->

53 For example, in *McKay v Johnson* [\[2016\] NZHC 1691](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/cases/NZHC/2016/1691.html "View Case"), \[2018\] NZAR 543 at [\[56\]](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/cases/NZHC/2016/1691.html#para56). Similarly, in *Gibson v Official Assignee* \[2019\] NZHC 532, (2019) 29 NZTC 24-005 Jagose J said the liquidator’s priority was for “salvage costs”: at \[28\].

<!-- al:1333 -->

54 *Stewart v Atco Controls Pty Ltd (in liq)*, above n 52.

<!-- al:1334 -->

> the authorities. Notably, the Court did not use the language of “salvage”, let alone draw on the law of salvage in admiralty. The Court stated the principle as follows:[55](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark54?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:1335 -->

\[22\] The principle ... may be more shortly stated as: a secured creditor may not have the benefit of a fund created by a liquidator’s efforts in the winding up without the liquidator’s costs and expenses, including remuneration, of creating that fund being first met. To that end, equity will create a charge over the fund in priority to that of the secured creditor.

<!-- al:1336 -->

\[23\] The circumstances in which the principle will apply are where: there is an insolvent company in liquidation; the liquidator has incurred expenses and rendered services in the realisation of an asset; the resulting fund is insufficient to meet both the liquidator’s costs and expenses of realisation and the debt due to a secured creditor; and the creditor claims the fund. In these circumstances, it is just that the liquidator be recompensed. To use the language of Deane J in *Hewett v Court*, it might be said that a secured creditor would be acting unconscientiously in taking the benefit of the liquidator’s work without the liquidator’s expenses being met. ...

<!-- al:1337 -->

\[557\] The High Court of Australia also made clear that the principle applies even if the creditor has not requested the steps taken by the liquidator.[56](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark55?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:1338 -->

\[558\] The principle applies only to the liquidator’s *reasonable* costs and remuneration in preserving and realising secured assets.[57](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark56?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) This aspect of the principle was not addressed by the High Court of Australia in *Stewart*, as it was not in issue in that case.

<!-- al:1339 -->

\[559\] Where the fund consists of the proceeds of litigation pursued by the liquidator, the priority under this principle extends to a success fee payable by the liquidator to a litigation funder, so long as it is reasonably incurred. This is because the success fee is one of the costs of realising those proceeds. This was recognised by the Full Court of the Federal Court of Australia in *IMF (Australia) Ltd v Meadow Springs Fairway Resort Ltd (in liq)*[.58](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark57?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) Meadow Springs Fairway Resort Ltd had been a property developer but had gone into liquidation. To fund a proceeding against a valuer, the liquidator entered into a litigation funding agreement with IMF (Australia) Ltd which provided for a success fee of 35 per cent of the resolution sum. The claim against the

<!-- al:1340 -->

55 Footnote omitted.

<!-- al:1341 -->

56 At \[38\] and \[48\].

<!-- al:1342 -->

1.  *IMF (Australia) Ltd v Meadow Springs Fairway Resort Ltd (in liq)* [\[2009\] FCAFC 9](http://www.austlii.edu.au/au/cases/cth/FCAFC/2009/9.html "View Case"), (2009) 253 ALR 240 at [\[74\]](http://www.austlii.edu.au/au/cases/cth/FCAFC/2009/9.html#para74) and \[79\]–\[80\]; and *Gibson v Official Assignee*, above n 53, at \[28\].

<!-- al:1343 -->

58 *IMF (Australia) Ltd v Meadow Springs Fairway Resort Ltd (in liq)*, above n 57.

<!-- al:1344 -->

> valuer was settled on the basis it would pay $6.95 million to Meadow Springs. One of Meadow Springs’ secured creditors was Balanced Security Ltd. Its security extended to the settlement proceeds. The issue was whether the liquidator’s obligation to pay the success fee to IMF could be charged against the settlement proceeds in priority to Balanced’s claim. The Full Court of the Federal Court held that it could. The success fee was reasonably incurred because it had been reasonable for the liquidator to enter into the litigation funding agreement with IMF.[59](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark58?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) Notably, Balanced argued that there was a distinction to be drawn between the reasonableness of entering into the litigation funding agreement and whether all the amounts payable under that agreement were reasonably incurred. The Full Court, while saying that such a distinction “may fairly be drawn”, approved the approach of the first instance Judge that the latter follows from the former.[60](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark59?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:1345 -->

\[560\] The principle does not extend to the costs of, and remuneration for, the general work of liquidation. The liquidator can recoup those costs and that remuneration only from the assets that are free from the claims of any securities.[61](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark60?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:1346 -->

\[561\] Mr Barker submitted that the principle applied only where the liquidator established that the secured creditor received an incontrovertible benefit from the costs incurred and services performed by the liquidator. Among other things, he relied on a statement of Muir J, in *McKay v Johnson*, that the work undertaken by the liquidator “must result in an indisputable benefit to the secured creditor”.[62](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark61?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:1347 -->

\[562\] Muir J’s statement is correct, in the sense that the liquidator must show that it was their work that created the fund. This aspect of the principle is illustrated by *Dean-Willcocks v Nothintoohard Pty Ltd*[.63](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark62?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) This case concerned steps taken by receivers (rather than liquidators) to sell land over which there was a registered first mortgage. After a time, the mortgagee decided to act itself and exercised its power of sale. The receivers claimed priority over the proceeds of sale for their costs and

<!-- al:1348 -->

59 At \[74\] and \[79\]–\[81\].

<!-- al:1349 -->

60 At \[74\].

<!-- al:1350 -->

61 *Buchler v Talbot*, above n 52, at \[62\]–\[63\] per Lord Millett.

<!-- al:1351 -->

62 *McKay v Johnson*, above n 53, at \[57\]. Mr Barker also relied on statements in Peter Blanchard and Michael Gedye *Private Receivers of Companies in New Zealand* (LexisNexis, 2008, Wellington) at \[6.04\] and \[6.08\].

<!-- al:1352 -->

63 *Dean-Willcocks v Nothintoohard Pty Ltd (in liq)* [\[2006\] NSWCA 311](http://www.austlii.edu.au/au/cases/nsw/NSWCA/2006/311.html "View Case"), (2007) 25 ACLC 109.

<!-- al:1353 -->

> expenses in connection with the steps they had taken. Their claim failed because it was held that their initial steps had not created the proceeds.[64](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark63?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:1354 -->

\[563\] But Mr Barker submitted that this aspect of the principle went further. He said the liquidator could claim priority for costs and remuneration only to the extent that a net benefit would remain for the secured creditor. Alternatively, he said there was a distinction between the liquidator’s costs and remuneration. Mr Barker drew on the law of salvage, in which, he said, the courts recognise that the owner is entitled to be left (after payment to the salvor) with a substantial portion of the salved property. He submitted that while a liquidator’s claim for costs might consume the entire proceeds of realisation, the liquidator’s claim for remuneration could not do so.

<!-- al:1355 -->

\[564\] I do not accept that submission. It draws inaptly on the law of salvage. It is not reflected in any authorities dealing with a liquidator’s priority for costs and remuneration. The size of the fund that might be generated by the liquidator’s realisation efforts will be a relevant factor in determining what costs and remuneration are reasonable. But, once the reasonable costs and remuneration are quantified, the liquidator enjoys a priority for them, even if they consume the entirety of the actual fund.

<!-- al:1356 -->

\[565\] I now turn to determine the reasonable costs and remuneration that Mr Walker was entitled to recoup from the PVL settlement proceeds in priority to the claims of any putative secured creditors. I start with remuneration.

<!-- al:1357 -->

*What reasonable remuneration could Mr Walker recoup from the settlement proceeds?*

<!-- al:1358 -->

\[566\] Mr Walker employed several staff as part of his insolvency practice, which he carried on in his own name. He was entitled to recoup from the PVL settlement proceeds, in priority to any secured claims, reasonable remuneration for both his time and the time of his staff, but only to the extent that their work led to the realisation of those proceeds. Remuneration for general liquidation work did not enjoy a priority over secured claims.

<!-- al:1359 -->

64 At \[109\] per Beazley JA and \[119\] per McColl JA.

<!-- al:1360 -->

\[567\] Mr Walker was remunerated directly by SPF for some of his and his staff’s time on the PVL litigation, as part of SPF’s obligation to fund Project Costs. That part of Mr Walker’s remuneration can be put to one side for the moment. It is captured by Mr Walker’s entitlement to recoup from the settlement proceeds the Project Costs (and the Services Fee) that he paid to SPF, which I deal with separately.

<!-- al:1361 -->

\[568\] Mr Walker’s evidence was that not all of his and his staff’s time relating to the PVL litigation was funded by SPF as Project Costs. One reason was that he received “minimal payment” for the work on the claims before funding was secured from SPF. The other and more substantial reason was that “very significant amounts” of time during the course of the PVL proceedings were not charged to SPF.

<!-- al:1362 -->

\[569\] Mr Walker explained that a budget had been agreed for the litigation and the total costs ended up being far greater than had been expected, with the budgeted allocation for liquidator fees quickly exhausted. He referred to an email from Mr Tim Smith on 1 October 2015 which noted that “the liquidator budget is now all but used up”. Mr Walker said he therefore charged only part of his fees to keep Project Costs within a level that SPF would meet.

<!-- al:1363 -->

\[570\] Mr Scutter’s evidence supported Mr Walker’s account. In cross-examination, Mr Scutter said the understanding with Mr Walker was not to bill everything until a settlement was reached with PwC. Contemporaneous documents also support Mr Walker’s evidence. A memorandum that Mr Tim Smith sent to Mr Woodhams on 20 November 2015 said that between March 2013 and November 2015, Mr Walker’s uncharged time was approximately $520,000. In addition, Mr Walker sent a memorandum to Mr Newland on 28 January 2016 in which he said that his unbilled time meant his “investment” in the PVL proceedings “is measured in the many millions”. Following that memorandum, in February 2016, SPF paid $150,000 to Mr Walker as part of Project Costs.

<!-- al:1364 -->

\[571\] Mr Walker explained that after the Distribution Agreement was entered into, he rendered two invoices for his fees and disbursements that totalled $4.08 million (including GST). This was the amount that on 25 August 2017 was paid to his practice account (from which the salaries and other expenses of his insolvency practice were

<!-- al:1365 -->

> paid). Mr Walker’s evidence was that even after payment of those invoices there was a substantial shortfall in the fees owing to him. He produced tables comparing the amount due to him based on his and his staff’s total time as at August 2017 (which he said was $8,387,571.50 excluding GST) and the total paid to him as at the same date (which he said was $5,344,890.57 excluding GST). He said the shortfall was
>
> <!-- al:1366 -->
>
> $3,042,690.93. Mr Eathorne, when examined by Mr Whitley, confirmed that Mr Walker’s actual costs were higher than the August 2017 invoices.

<!-- al:1367 -->

\[572\] Mr Walker was not cross-examined on this evidence. Mr Cooper submitted I should therefore conclude that the $4.08 million in fees paid to Mr Walker on 25 August 2017 were reasonably incurred “in the recovery of the fund”. By contrast, Mr Barker submitted that those fees did not relate to the PVL proceedings but rather to the more general liquidation, with the consequence that no part of those fees could be recouped from the settlement proceeds.

<!-- al:1368 -->

\[573\] I reject both submissions. The correct result lies somewhere in between. As to Mr Cooper’s submission, Mr Walker did not say that the entirety of the $4.08 million represented work done on the PVL proceedings. Nor can that be assumed from the material that Mr Walker referred to. The comparative tables he produced do not identify how much of his and his staff’s time was spent on the PVL proceedings as opposed to general liquidation work. That also cannot be discerned from the underlying timesheets that Mr Walker produced. Likewise, no such distinction is made in the invoices that Mr Walker rendered when the settlement was reached with PwC.

<!-- al:1369 -->

\[574\] As to Mr Barker’s submission, Mr Walker’s unchallenged evidence was that “very significant amounts” of time during the course of the PVL proceedings were not charged to SPF. This was supported by Mr Scutter’s evidence and by contemporaneous documents. I consider it follows that a substantial part of the

<!-- al:1370 -->

> $4.08 million was for work done on the PVL proceedings. The question is: how much?

<!-- al:1371 -->

\[575\] The original budget for the PVL proceedings included $555,000 for liquidator’s costs. By late November 2015 that had been used up and Mr Walker was saying that he had uncharged time of about $520,000. In the absence of contrary evidence, I infer that Mr Walker continued to charge SPF for only some of his and his

<!-- al:1372 -->

> staff’s time on the PVL proceedings, in roughly the same proportions as he had up to November 2015. I give no weight to Mr Walker’s 28 January 2015 memorandum in which he suggested his unbilled time was in the millions of dollars. Mr Walker acknowledged that this was a plea for payment. I regard it as an exaggeration for the purposes of negotiating with SPF.

<!-- al:1373 -->

\[576\] William Apps, an expert called by LPF, calculated that the total amount paid by SPF to Mr Walker as Project Costs (including the $150,000 paid by SPF in February 2016) was $1,679,061 excluding GST[.65](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark64?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) I therefore infer that Mr Walker undercharged SPF by about $1,573,000 through the course of the PVL proceedings. Allowing for GST, I conclude that Mr Walker’s remuneration for work that led to the realisation of the settlement proceeds amounts to approximately $1.8 million. This is encompassed within the invoices rendered by Mr Walker following the PwC settlement.

<!-- al:1374 -->

\[577\] Ordinarily, I would also have to assess whether that remuneration was reasonable. However, there was no issue between the parties as to the reasonableness of Mr Walker’s remuneration. That is to be determined in the separate remuneration application brought by Mr Whitley in the PVL liquidation proceeding.

<!-- al:1375 -->

*What reasonable costs could Mr Walker recoup from the settlement proceeds?*

<!-- al:1376 -->

\[578\] The only costs that it is suggested Mr Walker could recoup from the settlement proceeds are the costs he incurred in making payments to SPF. Two issues arise:

<!-- al:1377 -->

> <!-- al:1378 -->
>
> (a) What amount of SPF costs did Mr Walker incur in realising the settlement proceeds?
>
> <!-- al:1379 -->
>
> (b) How much of those SPF costs were reasonable?

<!-- al:1380 -->

\[579\] Before addressing those issues, I clarify one matter. These issues are about the rights to the settlement proceeds as between Mr Walker and the 100 Parties. They are not about the rights of SPF. If I were to conclude that Mr Walker had unreasonably incurred costs to SPF, that would affect Mr Walker’s right of recoupment. But it would

<!-- al:1381 -->

65 I have not included the relatively insignificant amount paid by SPF for Mr Scutter’s fees.

<!-- al:1382 -->

> not necessarily follow that SPF was liable to repay, either to Mr Walker, the 100 Parties or anyone else, the “unreasonable” payment. Any such liability could arise only from the application of some principle other than the principle governing Mr Walker’s right of recoupment. This distinction was not always observed in the plaintiffs’ case.

<!-- al:1383 -->

*What amount of SPF costs did Mr Walker incur in realising the settlement proceeds?*

<!-- al:1384 -->

\[580\] A distinction needs to be drawn between, on the one hand, the payments made to SPF under the Services Deed (as amended by the Distribution Agreement) for Project Costs and the Services Fee and, on the other, the payments made to SPF under the Allied security and the DFG security. Only the former can be characterised as costs incurred by Mr Walker in realising the settlement proceeds. The latter cannot. They were, rather, a distribution of part of the proceeds in accordance with SPF’s rights under the Allied security and the DFG security. This is not to question the validity of SPF’s claim to those payments under those securities. But the present inquiry is into the extent of Mr Walker’s claim to priority (as against putative secured creditors) for costs incurred in realising the proceeds.

<!-- al:1385 -->

\[581\] The parties to the Distribution Agreement agreed, in cl 1.3, that some payments to SPF were made under the Services Deed and other payments were made under the Allied security and the DFG security. It will be convenient to repeat cl 1.3 in full:

<!-- al:1386 -->

For the avoidance of any doubt, all payments made to or on behalf of SPF in accordance with:

<!-- al:1387 -->

> <!-- al:1388 -->
>
> (a) paragraphs 1.1(a)–(c) (as the case may be) are made in accordance with the Services Deed and in reliance by SPF on the security created under the Services Deed;
>
> <!-- al:1389 -->
>
> (b) paragraphs 1.1(d) or 1.2 (as the case may be) are made pursuant to and in enforcement by SPF of the Allied Debts and the Dominion Debts.

<!-- al:1390 -->

\[582\] I see no reason to go behind the Agreement. Clause 1.3(b) identifies that two payments to SPF are under the Allied security and the DFG security. One of the payments is easy to quantify: the payment under cl 1.2 was the payment from the directors settlement. SPF received $2.3 million from that settlement. That payment is not a cost that Mr Walker is entitled to recoup from the settlement proceeds in priority to any secured creditors with an interest in the proceeds.

<!-- al:1391 -->

\[583\] The other payment referred to in cl 1.3(b) — the payment under cl 1.1(d) — is not so easy to quantify. Under cl 1.1(d), SPF was to receive the balance of the PwC settlement proceeds ($33.8 million) after (a) payment of its Project Costs and the Services Fee in accordance with the Services Deed and (b) payment of $5,380,000 to PVL. In order to quantify that balance, it is necessary to quantify the amount of Project Costs, and the Services Fee, that SPF was entitled to be paid. The Distribution Agreement did not quantify that amount and the parties to the Agreement did not quantify it at the time. Quantification therefore has to be done retrospectively.

<!-- al:1392 -->

\[584\] Before undertaking the quantification exercise, I note that there is a limit on how much the payment of Project Costs and the Services Fee could have been under cl 1.1(d). The PwC settlement proceeds were $33.8 million, of which $5.38 million was paid to PVL. The balance — and the most that the payment of Project Costs and the Services Fee could have been — was $28,420,000.

<!-- al:1393 -->

\[585\] I now turn to quantification. The focus is on Project Costs, as the Services Fee is a product of that amount. While the parties to the present proceedings agreed on some elements that were Project Costs, there were some significant disputes.

<!-- al:1394 -->

\[586\] In resolving those disputes, what needs to be established are the Project Costs at the time of the PwC settlement, not Project Costs at the end of the litigation in early 2018. This is because the effect of cl 4.1 of the Distribution Agreement (the full and final settlement provision) was that SPF had no entitlement to be repaid any Project Costs that it might advance after the PwC settlement, or to be paid any further Services Fee in respect of those Project Costs. Further, SPF’s entitlement to Project Costs needs to be determined in accordance with any amendments made to the Services Deed by the Agreement.

<!-- al:1395 -->

\[587\] Mr Apps analysed SPF’s records of Project Costs. His evidence, based on those records, was that the Project Costs paid or payable at the time of the PwC settlement totalled $9,691,710. There was no contrary analysis in evidence. To that amount Mr Apps added allowances for further payments to DFG ($1.15 million) and

<!-- al:1396 -->

> Allied ($97,757).[66](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark65?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) He therefore assessed total Project Costs at that time as
>
> <!-- al:1397 -->
>
> $10,939,468. He said SPF’s entitlement to the Services Fee at that time was
>
> <!-- al:1398 -->
>
> $21,878,935 (two times total Project Costs).

<!-- al:1399 -->

\[588\] I accept Mr Apps’ analysis. However, Mr Apps was merely giving evidence of his analysis of SPF’s records. He was not purporting to give an expert opinion on whether the various disputed items were or were not Project Costs. I now turn to those disputes.

<!-- al:1400 -->

\[589\] First, the plaintiffs challenged $1.95 million of security for costs that was provided by SPF in the PVL proceedings by way of bank bonds. In closing, Mr Moss acknowledged that the definitions of “Project Costs” and “Security for Costs” in the Services Deed appeared to cover the provision of security for costs in this manner. But Mr Moss said that the evidence of John Walker (who was called by LPF to give expert evidence on litigation funding) was that the face value of a bond would not normally be considered a Project Cost. Mr Moss submitted, based on that evidence, that security for costs could be claimed as a Project Cost only to the extent that SPF incurred an out-of-pocket cost (for example, by paying cash into court or backing a bond with cash).

<!-- al:1401 -->

\[590\] I do not accept that submission. The Services Deed defines “Project Costs” as meaning:[67](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark66?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:1402 -->

... all costs, disbursements and *liabilities* of any nature that are incurred or assumed (whether absolutely *or contingently*) by ... SPF in relation to or in connection with ... the Proceedings.

<!-- al:1403 -->

> <!-- al:1404 -->
>
> Those words themselves cover a bank bond or bank guarantee, whether backed by cash or not. The matter is put beyond any doubt by the definition then saying that Project Costs “includ\[es\] ... the provision of Security for Costs *in any form* acceptable to a Court” and by the definition of “Security for Costs”, which means “any gross cash

<!-- al:1405 -->

66 The amount that Mr Apps allowed for the Allied payment was significantly less than the amount that SPF eventually paid. The reason for this is not clear. However, it reduced the amount of Project Costs that Mr Apps assessed as having been paid or accrued at that time.

<!-- al:1406 -->

67 Emphasis added.

<!-- al:1407 -->

> amount *or the face value* of any security”.[68](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark67?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) Mr John Walker’s evidence is not relevant to the plain meaning of these words.

<!-- al:1408 -->

\[591\] Secondly, the plaintiffs said that the further anticipated payments to Allied and DFG did not fall within the definition of Project Costs. Mr Moss accepted that the initial payment to Allied of $115,000 (including GST) was a Project Cost. But he submitted that the further payment to Allied was to be five percent of any net proceeds received by SPF from the litigation. He said this was a payment made after the costs under the Services Deed had already been paid. He said the same was true of the payment that was to be made by SPF to DFG. In addition, he said that the DFG payment arose from SPF’s own negligence in allowing the Allied security to lapse.

<!-- al:1409 -->

\[592\] Those submissions ignore the plain words of the Distribution Agreement. Clause 1.1(a) said that the outstanding Project Costs were to be paid to SPF, “such Project Costs to include the purchase price of the Allied Debts ... and the purchase price of the \[DFG\] Debts”.

<!-- al:1410 -->

\[593\] It is likely that the definition of Project Costs in the Services Deed was broad enough to capture the purchase prices payable by SPF for the Allied security and the DFG security. That definition covers liabilities, including contingent liabilities, incurred by SPF “in relation to or in connection with” the Deed or the PVL proceedings. But cl 1.1(a) of the Distribution Agreement puts the matter beyond any doubt.

<!-- al:1411 -->

\[594\] Thirdly, in closing Mr Moss submitted that SPF’s payment of $150,000 to Mr Walker in February 2016 was not for costs relating to the PVL litigation. This was not pleaded. Further, Mr Walker’s and Mr Woodham’s evidence about this payment (which I addressed when looking at Mr Walker’s remuneration) was not challenged. I find this payment did relate to the PVL litigation.

<!-- al:1412 -->

\[595\] Fourthly, Mr Moss challenged several miscellaneous costs:

<!-- al:1413 -->

68 Emphasis added.

<!-- al:1414 -->

> (a) Mr Smith KC’s fee of October 2012 for his initial opinion. Mr Moss said the definition of Project Costs only includes costs incurred “from the date of this Deed (not before)”. But the Services Deed, although not signed by SPF until March 2013, bears the date of 31 October 2012. Further, Mr Smith KC’s fee was not paid until December 2012.
>
> <!-- al:1415 -->
>
> (b) Mr Bigio’s fees during 2017. Mr Bigio was engaged by SPF in relation to the hearing before the Supreme Court and in relation to the distribution dispute. These matters plainly relate to or are in connection with both the Services Deed and the PVL proceedings. They are therefore Project Costs.
>
> <!-- al:1416 -->
>
> (c) Solicitors’ fees in 2016 and 2017 for attendances relating to the priority dispute with DFG, the Supreme Court hearing and the distribution dispute. I find that all these were “in relation to or in connection with” the Deed or the PVL proceedings.
>
> <!-- al:1417 -->
>
> (d) Fees for media consultancy. Up to the PwC settlement, these total about
>
> <!-- al:1418 -->
>
> $21,000. Mr Moss submitted that an explanation for these had not been given and that they cannot have been for the benefit of the PVL proceedings. I disagree. It is not unusual for media consultants to be engaged on significant litigation. The consultants’ invoices reference “Property Ventures Limited / PwC”. Mr Woodhams gave evidence that all the miscellaneous costs were within the definition of Project Costs (and, therefore, incurred in relation to the PVL proceedings). His evidence was not challenged. I find these were Project Costs.
>
> <!-- al:1419 -->
>
> (e) Further expenses of Mr Woodhams of $1,616.15. Mr Woodhams’ evidence, to which I have just referred, was not challenged. I find these were Project Costs.

<!-- al:1420 -->

\[596\] I therefore find that, as at the date of the PwC settlement, the total Project Costs were $10,939,468. This meant that the Services Fee (two times Project Costs) was

<!-- al:1421 -->

> $21,878,935. These amounts totalled $32,818,403. From this, there had to be deductions for the following, both of which Mr Apps noted in his analysis:
>
> <!-- al:1422 -->
>
> (a) SPF had already received partial repayment of Project Costs of
>
> <!-- al:1423 -->
>
> $2,180,500 from the Fright Aubrey settlement.[69](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark68?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))
>
> <!-- al:1424 -->
>
> (b) SPF was going to enjoy, as a result of the PwC settlement, a reduction in some of the security for costs that it had provided. This reduction was quantified a few weeks after the settlement as $560,000.

<!-- al:1425 -->

\[597\] Allowing for those deductions, SPF’s entitlement to be paid Project Costs and the Services Fee at the time of the PwC settlement was $30,077,903. In fact, SPF was paid less than that for Project Costs and the Services Fee under the Distribution Agreement. As explained earlier, the most that SPF could have been paid for Project Costs and the Services Fee under the Agreement was $28,420,000. That is what SPF was paid for those items under the Agreement.

<!-- al:1426 -->

\[598\] Together with the earlier partial repayment from the Fright Aubrey settlement, this means that Mr Walker incurred costs to SPF totalling $30,600,500 in realising the settlement proceeds.

<!-- al:1427 -->

*How much of the SPF costs were reasonable?*

<!-- al:1428 -->

\[599\] The defendants noted that Mr Walker incurred the costs to SPF pursuant to obligations undertaken in the Services Deed, as amended by the Distribution Agreement. They submitted that the reasonableness of those costs should therefore be assessed by considering whether it was reasonable for Mr Walker to enter into the Deed and the Agreement. That, they submitted, had to be assessed as at the respective times the Deed and the Agreement were entered into.

<!-- al:1429 -->

\[600\] Mr Barker, by contrast, submitted that the reasonableness of the costs had to be assessed as at the time the costs were paid. Mr Barker relied on Australian authorities dealing with the approval of settlements of class actions. He said these

<!-- al:1430 -->

1.  It appears the Fright Aubrey settlement proceeds were slightly smaller, $2,180,286. The difference is immaterial.

<!-- al:1431 -->

> authorities showed that the test for approval is whether the settlement was “fair and reasonable” and that the terms of any funding agreement are not determinative of whether a settlement will be approved.

<!-- al:1432 -->

\[601\] The authorities to which Mr Barker referred are concerned with s 33V of the [Federal Court of Australia Act 1976](http://www.austlii.edu.au/au/legis/cth/consol_act/fcoaa1976249/index.html) (Cth). Section 33V(1) provides that a representative proceeding may not be settled or discontinued without the approval of the Court. Section 33V(2) says that if the Court gives such an approval, it may “make such orders as are just with respect to the distribution of any money paid under a settlement or paid into the Court”. Mr Barker referred me to *Blairgowrie Trading Ltd v Allco Finance Group Ltd (recs and mgrs apptd) (in liq) (No 3)*, in which Beach J held that s 33V(2) empowers the Court to modify a funding agreement.[70](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark69?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:1433 -->

\[602\] Mr Barker did not provide a comprehensive review of the Australian authorities. I have not found it necessary to undertake such a review myself.[71](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark70?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) This is because the Court’s power to review and modify a funding agreement, recognised in *Blairgowrie*, derives from s 33V, which of course is inapplicable here.

<!-- al:1434 -->

\[603\] In my view, the reasonableness of the costs that Mr Walker incurred has to be assessed by reference to the circumstances at the time Mr Walker entered into the obligations that gave rise to those costs. Otherwise, hindsight will be brought to bear on the assessment. It is important to resist using hindsight in assessing the reasonableness of costs under litigation funding agreements. The hindsight will only ever be employed at a time when all the litigation risks are over and proceeds have been realised from a settlement or judgment. It is then all too easy to forget the risks that the funder undertook and to regard the funder’s return as being unreasonable. Here, for example, SPF would likely have lost almost its entire investment had the PwC stay application succeeded.

<!-- al:1435 -->

70 *Blairgowrie Trading Ltd v Allco Finance Group Ltd (recs and mgrs apptd) (in liq) (No 3)* [\[2017\] FCA 330](http://www.austlii.edu.au/au/cases/cth/FCA/2017/330.html "View Case"), (2017) 343 ALR 476 at [\[101\]](http://www.austlii.edu.au/au/cases/cth/FCA/2017/330.html#para101).

<!-- al:1436 -->

71 In a later judgment (to which Mr Barker also referred me), Lee J declined to express a view on whether s 33V of the [Federal Court of Australia Act 1976](http://www.austlii.edu.au/au/legis/cth/consol_act/fcoaa1976249/index.html) (Cth) empowers the Court to review a funding agreement: *Clarke v Sandhurst Trustees Ltd (No 2)* [\[2018\] FCA 511](http://www.austlii.edu.au/au/cases/cth/FCA/2018/511.html "View Case") at [\[17\]](http://www.austlii.edu.au/au/cases/cth/FCA/2018/511.html#para17)–\[18\].

<!-- al:1437 -->

\[604\] I find that it was reasonable for Mr Walker to enter into the Services Deed on the terms that it contained. At the time, there appeared to be claims against PwC and the directors that had the potential to produce returns to creditors. There were no assets within PVL to fund the PVL proceedings. Mr Walker had tried, unsuccessfully, to interest Allied and another third party (the Wellington family) to fund the proceedings. No other secured creditor showed any interest in funding the proceedings. In particular, Tomanovich Holdings (which purports to have become a secured creditor of a PVL subsidiary in June 2010) expressed no interest in funding the proceedings. This meant that the proceedings were only going to be brought if Mr Walker entered into an agreement with a litigation funder.

<!-- al:1438 -->

\[605\] Mr John Walker’s evidence was that in 2012 there were six litigation funders in the Australian market. Only one of them was active in the New Zealand market, and it had a budget limit of $2 million, which was too small for the PVL proceedings. The only other funder active in the New Zealand market was LPF, which had been operating since 2009. LPF was the only funder taking on claims the size of the PVL proceedings. This meant that Mr Walker’s options for litigation funding were very limited.

<!-- al:1439 -->

\[606\] Mr John Walker identified a multitude of risks in each of the claims against PwC and the directors. He said these risks made the claims a riskier endeavour than most other large-scale funded cases. He said that Mr Walker had to persuade a litigation funder to assume those risks on the basis of non-recourse funding.

<!-- al:1440 -->

\[607\] In his opinion, the terms of the Services Deed were generally standard for the Australian and New Zealand market in 2012, including the definition of Project Costs (subject to one caveat) and the Services Fee. He noted that in 2012 most funding agreements with companies in liquidation were on the basis that the funder would be reimbursed any project costs and be paid a fee of the greater of three times project costs and 20 to 45 per cent of the gross proceeds of the claim. He would not have expected any other funder in the market to agree to fund the PVL proceedings for lesser rates than in the Deed or at all.

<!-- al:1441 -->

\[608\] Mr John Walker was cross-examined extensively, but much of the cross-examination explored the way in which the Australian courts review the reasonableness of settlements of class actions. The aspects of his evidence that I have just outlined were not challenged.

<!-- al:1442 -->

\[609\] There was one caveat to Mr John Walker’s opinion that the definition of Project Costs was market standard. It concerned security for costs. He said that in 2012 it was market standard for any payments of security for costs by a funder in the form of cash into court or by cash-backed bonds to be included within the definition of project costs. In cross-examination and re-examination he clarified that this meant that if a bond was not backed by cash, the market standard would be that only the upfront cost of obtaining the bond, but not the potential liability under it, would be within the definition of project costs. Although he was not taken to it, this contrasted with the definitions of Project Costs and Security for Costs in the Services Deed, under which the potential liability would be a Project Cost.

<!-- al:1443 -->

\[610\] The plaintiffs submitted that this meant it was not reasonable to include within Project Costs the $1.95 million that SPF had provided as security for costs by way of bank bonds, because SPF had not shown that these bonds were backed by cash.

<!-- al:1444 -->

\[611\] I reject that submission, for two reasons. First, Mr Newland said in cross-examination that “\[w\]e had to back with hard cash the security for costs as it became available”. That evidence went unchallenged. Secondly, I consider the definitions of Project Costs and Security for Costs were reasonable in the wider context in which the Services Deed was entered into. SPF was the only viable funder in the market. SPF was taking on a much riskier endeavour than most other large-scale funded cases. SPF was charging a Services Fee lower than the market: the greater of two times (not three times) Project Costs or a percentage of net (not gross) proceeds. And there is nothing commercially unreasonable about counting the face liability of a bank bond as a Project Cost. If the proceedings had failed and the bank bond had been called on, that face value would have crystallised as a very real cost for SPF. So, even if the bank bonds had not been backed by cash, I would have regarded it as reasonable for Mr Walker to pay SPF on the basis (as agreed in the Services Deed) that they were Project Costs.

<!-- al:1445 -->

\[612\] The final issue is whether it was reasonable for Mr Walker to agree, in the Distribution Agreement, that Project Costs would include the purchase prices of the Allied and DFG debts and securities. In closing, Mr Moss accepted that SPF’s first payment to Allied ($115,000) was a Project Cost because the Services Deed was conditional on obtaining an assignment of the Allied security and so the initial payment was connected with the Deed and with the PVL proceedings. He submitted, however, that the second payment ($441,137.15) could not reasonably be considered a Project Cost because it was to be paid from the net proceeds after payments were made to SPF under the Deed. Among other things, he said it was not a cost to SPF at all. Mr Moss said that the same applied to the purchase price of the DFG security of

<!-- al:1446 -->

> $1.65 million. In addition, he submitted that it was not reasonable to count that purchase price as a Project Cost, given that the purchase of the DFG security became necessary only because of SPF’s failure to renew the PPSR registration of the Allied security.

<!-- al:1447 -->

\[613\] Whether these costs were reasonable needs to be determined at the time the Distribution Agreement was entered into. That includes taking into account the compromise that was reached in the Agreement. The following points are relevant.

<!-- al:1448 -->

\[614\] First, the amounts that Mr Moss put in issue total approximately $2.09 million of Project Costs. Given that the Services Fee was going to be calculated at two times Project Costs, Mr Moss submitted that the inclusion of the purchase prices of the Allied security and the DFG security as Project Costs meant that SPF received an additional $6.27 million from the overall settlement proceeds. But that would only have been the case had there been an Allied security agreement under which SPF was bound to hold the proceeds received under the Allied security for PVL. Given that there was no such agreement, the inclusion of these items as Project Costs had a much smaller impact on the parties’ respective returns than was submitted by Mr Moss. This can be most easily illustrated by considering the effect that the inclusion of these items as Project Costs would have had on the parties’ respective returns had SPF merely complied with the undertaking it had given to the Supreme Court (to pay to PVL 10 per cent of the balance of the settlement proceeds, after first deducting its entitlements under the Services Deed). In that case:

<!-- al:1449 -->

> (a) Inclusion of these two items would have increased SPF’s total entitlement to be paid Project Costs and the Services Fee by
>
> <!-- al:1450 -->
>
> $6.27 million.
>
> <!-- al:1451 -->
>
> (b) That would, however, have reduced the balance of the settlement proceeds and therefore reduced SPF’s entitlement under the Allied security by the same amount of $6.27 million.
>
> <!-- al:1452 -->
>
> (c) The effect of SPF’s undertaking was that the reduction in (b) was only 90 per cent of the $6.27 million.
>
> <!-- al:1453 -->
>
> (d) The inclusion of the two items would therefore have increased SPF’s overall return by 10 per cent of $6.27 million, which is $627,000.

<!-- al:1454 -->

\[615\] In actuality, of course, SPF did not merely comply with the undertaking. SPF paid more to PVL than it had initially undertaken to pay. Further, the structure of the payments agreed under cls 1.1 and 1.2 of the Distribution Agreement was such that the inclusion of the Allied and DFG payments as Project Costs made no difference to SPF’s overall return. Under the Agreement, SPF was to receive $28,420,000 from the PwC settlement (under either the Services Deed or the Allied security and the DFG security) and an agreed share of any directors settlement (under the securities). The only effect of including the Allied and DFG payments as Project Costs was to increase the proportion of the $28,420,000 that was attributable to SPF’s return under the Deed rather than its return under the securities. This, indeed, is the case for all the contested Project Costs.

<!-- al:1455 -->

\[616\] Secondly, the effect of cl 1.1 of the Distribution Agreement was that the most SPF could be paid for its return under the Services Deed was $28,420,000. That was significantly less than SPF’s entitlement under the Deed (assuming inclusion of the Allied and DFG payments as Project Costs) at the time of the PwC settlement. Mr Apps’ calculation of SPF’s entitlement at that time (net of the deductions for the payment from the Fright Aubrey settlement and the reduction in security for costs) was $30,077,903. However, in reaching that amount Mr Apps did not include as Project Costs the entire amount that Mr Moss put in issue. Mr Apps allowed for the

<!-- al:1456 -->

> full DFG purchase price but only $97,757 for the further Allied payment. Had Mr Apps allowed the full amount of that further payment ($441,137.15), the calculation of SPF’s entitlement would have been $31,108,043. That is approximately
>
> <!-- al:1457 -->
>
> $2.69 million more than the maximum SPF was able to recover under cl 1.1. This means that the consequence of the inclusion of the Allied and DFG purchase prices as Project Costs was that SPF received an additional $3.58 million under the Deed (not the $6.27 million suggested by Mr Moss) than it otherwise would have.

<!-- al:1458 -->

\[617\] Thirdly, this amount can only be treated as “additional” if SPF was not already, under the Services Deed, entitled to it. I consider that the definition of Project Costs in the Deed captured the purchase prices paid to Allied and DFG. The definition includes costs and liabilities of any nature that are assumed absolutely or contingently by SPF “in relation to or in connection with” the Deed or the PVL proceedings. SPF’s liabilities to Allied and DFG were “in connection with” both the Deed and the PVL proceedings, because SPF would never have entered into the agreements with Allied and DFG were SPF not funding the PVL proceedings under the Deed.

<!-- al:1459 -->

\[618\] Fourthly, Mr John Walker’s evidence did not assist, one way or another, in understanding whether it was market standard for the post-settlement payments to Allied and DFG to be included within Project Costs. In his brief he expressed the view that it was reasonable for the purchase prices paid to Allied and DFG to be included within Project Costs. However, in his oral evidence it emerged that he was unaware that part of those purchase prices was to be paid after settlement and that the amount of the payment depended on the amount of net proceeds. Once he became aware of those matters, he felt unable to say, without further information, whether including those payments as Project Costs was usual.

<!-- al:1460 -->

\[619\] Fifthly, the post-settlement payments that SPF made to Allied and DFG were real costs to SPF. They reduced the return that SPF would otherwise obtain (either as funder or as assignee of the debts and securities).

<!-- al:1461 -->

\[620\] These factors lead me to the view that, even before considering the other compromises made in the Distribution Agreement, it was reasonable for the further Allied payment to be treated as a Project Cost. That accounts for $1.32 million of the

<!-- al:1462 -->

> “additional” $3.58 million that SPF received under the Services Deed, leaving a balance of $2.26 million. That balance reflects the effect of including the DFG purchase price as a Project Cost on SPF’s return under the Services Deed (but noting that it made no difference to SPF’s overall return under the Deed and the securities).

<!-- al:1463 -->

\[621\] There is force in Mr Moss’s submission that it is unreasonable to include as a Project Cost a cost that has arisen from SPF’s failure to renew the PPSR registration for the Allied security. However, the submission loses much of its force when it is realised that the inclusion of this cost had no effect on SPF’s (or PVL’s) overall return. The inclusion merely affected the portion of the $28,420,000 that the parties agreed, in the Distribution Agreement, to attribute to SPF’s return under the Services Deed. I consider it was reasonable for the parties to reach that agreement, given the other benefits that accrued to PVL under the Distribution Agreement: SPF agreed to caps on its returns from the PwC and directors settlements and thereby agreed to pay significantly more to PVL than it had initially undertaken to pay; SPF remained obliged to advance Project Costs yet would enjoy no further return from those advances; and SPF agreed to transfer the Allied security and the DFG security to the liquidator for the benefit of PVL’s unsecured creditors.

<!-- al:1464 -->

\[622\] As to the second of those benefits, SPF advanced at least $1 million in Project Costs after the PwC settlement. Under the Services Deed, SPF would have been entitled to be repaid those costs and be paid an additional Services Fee of at least $2 million. Under the Distribution Agreement, the parties agreed that SPF’s share of the directors settlement proceeds should be attributed solely to a return under the Allied and DFG securities. This characterisation of SPF’s return outweighed the effect of including the DFG purchase price as a Project Cost.

<!-- al:1465 -->

\[623\] I therefore conclude that Mr Walker reasonably incurred $30,600,500 in costs to SPF in realising the settlement proceeds.

<!-- al:1466 -->

\[624\] I add some observations. The plaintiffs at times presented their cases as if it was obvious that it was unreasonable for a litigation funder to receive as much of the settlement proceeds as SPF did here. This overlooked three interrelated matters. First, litigation funding on a non-recourse basis is a high-risk investment in any

<!-- al:1467 -->

> circumstances and, as Mr John Walker identified, the PVL litigation was a riskier endeavour than most other large-scale funded cases. No other funder was prepared to touch it. Secondly, one of the risks for the funder is that the claim proves more costly and time-consuming than expected. This risk manifested itself here. The solicitors engaged on the PVL proceedings provided SPF with a budget and projected timeline for the proceedings at the outset. Even leaving to one side the Allied and DFG costs, the actual expenditure on key items such as legal costs was two to three times the budget and the proceedings took about twice as long to resolve as forecast. A funder naturally and reasonably seeks some protection from this risk by negotiating a return that can (depending on outcome) be a product of project costs. The level of SPF’s return therefore reflects the manifestation of this risk in the PVL proceedings. Thirdly, SPF was not merely providing funding. It was also providing the time and expertise of Mr Woodhams and Mr Newland. Among other things, they had a leading role in settlement negotiations with Fright Aubrey, PwC and the director defendants. Their efforts were instrumental in producing substantial settlements.

<!-- al:1468 -->

*Summary*

<!-- al:1469 -->

\[625\] Mr Walker was entitled to recoup from the settlement proceeds, in priority to any secured creditor, a total of $32,400,500: $1,800,000 for his remuneration and

<!-- al:1470 -->

> $30,600,500 in costs. This is 83 per cent of the total settlement proceeds.

<!-- al:1471 -->

\[626\] If I had found the 100 Parties to be secured creditors of the PVL subsidiaries, I would have found $1,352,000 of the settlement proceeds to be subject to their securities. That is only a part of the settlement proceeds. An issue then arises as to how the right of recoupment is exercised in such circumstances. Can Mr Walker recoup the first $1,352,000 of his costs from that portion, leaving nothing for the putative secured creditors, or can he recoup only a proportionate amount (83 per cent) of that portion?

<!-- al:1472 -->

\[627\] I did not receive any submissions on this point, though Mr Barker’s approach assumed that any recoupment would be proportional only. I agree with that approach.

<!-- al:1473 -->

\[628\] It follows that had I found the 100 Parties to be secured creditors of the PVL subsidiaries, the net amount available to them after Mr Walker exercised his right of recoupment would have been 17 per cent of $1,352,000, which is $229,840.

<!-- al:1474 -->

**Issue 9: Does [s 284](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s284.html) of the [Companies Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/index.html) confer jurisdiction to make the orders sought by Mr Whitley affecting the rights of Mr Walker, LPF and SPF?**

<!-- al:1475 -->

\[629\] In several of his causes of actions Mr Whitley seeks orders under [s 284(1)(a)](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s284.html) and (b) of the [Companies Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/index.html), which provides:

<!-- al:1476 -->

**284 Court supervision of liquidation**

<!-- al:1477 -->

(1) On the application of the liquidator, a liquidation committee, or, with the leave of the court, a creditor, shareholder, other entitled person, or director of a company in liquidation, the court may—

<!-- al:1478 -->

> <!-- al:1479 -->
>
> (a) give directions in relation to any matter arising in connection with the liquidation:
>
> <!-- al:1480 -->
>
> (b) confirm, reverse, or modify an act or decision of the liquidator:

<!-- al:1481 -->

...

<!-- al:1482 -->

\[630\] The orders that Mr Whitley seeks under [s 284(1)(a)](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s284.html) and (b) constitute substantive remedies or relief against Mr Walker, LPF or SPF. For example, Mr Whitley’s second cause of action makes claims about the validity of the Distribution Agreement and about the reasonableness of payments made under it. Mr Whitley then seeks relief that includes:

<!-- al:1483 -->

> <!-- al:1484 -->
>
> (a) a direction under s 284(1)(a) that various Project Costs were unreasonable expenses of the liquidations of PVL and the PVL subsidiaries and that the sum of $16,954,678 has to be repaid by SPF and LPF to the Whitley Parties; and
>
> <!-- al:1485 -->
>
> (b) an order under s 284(1)(b) reversing the decision of Mr Walker to enter into the Distribution Agreement.

<!-- al:1486 -->

\[631\] The defendants submitted that s 284(1)(a) and (b) do not confer jurisdiction to make substantive orders against third parties in the way sought by Mr Whitley.

<!-- al:1487 -->

*Can the court make directions under [s 284(1)(a)](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s284.html) of the [Companies Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/index.html) having the effect of substantive orders against third parties?*

<!-- al:1488 -->

\[632\] In relation to [s 284(1)(a)](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s284.html), the defendants relied on a judgment of Associate Judge Paulsen in *Dalton v Mackley*.[72](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark71?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) There, the liquidators of a company considered that the company had purchased certain assets from Mr Mackley prior to liquidation.
