Mr Mackley disputed this. The liquidators brought an originating application for directions under [s 284(1)(a)](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s284.html) to resolve the dispute. Although framed as an application for directions, the liquidators asked the court “to rule that \[the company\] owns the assets and to order Mr Mackley to deliver up the assets to them”.[73](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark72?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) The Judge was asked to determine whether the orders sought by the liquidators were ones that the court could make under [s 284(1)(a).](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s284.html)

<!-- al:1489 -->

\[633\] The Judge held that the orders sought were not ones that could properly be made under [s 284(1)(a).](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s284.html)[20](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/<a href=?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))25\_3486.html#\_bookmark73">74 As to the text of the provision, the Judge said:[75](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark74?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:1490 -->

\[24\] The text of [s 284](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s284.html) and other indications provided by the [Companies Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/index.html) are supportive of a view that the proper subject of directions under [s 284(1)(a)](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s284.html) is as to the manner in which a liquidation should be carried out under the control of the liquidator and not in the making of binding orders in the nature of judgments. The section appears in [pt 16](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/index.html#p16) of the Act, the scheme of which is to provide liquidators with as little interference from the court as is practicable while retaining a measure of court supervision of liquidators upon application. The section also appears in a subpart headed “Appointment and supervision of liquidators” and [s 284](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s284.html) itself is headed “Court supervision of liquidation”. The word “supervision” connotes management or oversight not the making of binding orders against third parties in the nature of a judgment.

<!-- al:1491 -->

...

<!-- al:1492 -->

\[27\] Third, central to the operation of the section is [s 284(3).](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s284.html) That provides a liquidator who has “obtained a direction with respect to a matter connected with the exercise of the powers or functions of liquidator” and who acts in accordance with the direction, with a defence to a claim in relation to anything done or not done in accordance with the direction. The use of the phrase “the exercise of the powers or functions of liquidator”, again supports the view that the section is concerned with directing the liquidator in the proper discharge of its functions. ...

<!-- al:1493 -->

72 *Dalton v Mackley* [\[2021\] NZHC 2329.](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/cases/NZHC/2021/2329.html "View Case")

<!-- al:1494 -->

73 At \[2\].

<!-- al:1495 -->

74 At \[42\].

<!-- al:1496 -->

75 Footnotes omitted.

<!-- al:1497 -->

\[634\] The Judge then reviewed case law, including Australian decisions on provisions equivalent to [s 284(1)(a).](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s284.html) One of the cases the Judge relied on was a decision of Goldberg J in the Federal Court of Australia, *Re Ansett Australia Ltd (No 3)*.[76](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark75?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) *Re Ansett* was concerned with the then Australian equivalent to [s 284(1)(a)](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s284.html), s 479(3) of the [Corporations Act 2001](http://www.austlii.edu.au/au/legis/cth/consol_act/ca2001172/index.html) (Cth). Judge Paulsen quoted the following passage from Goldberg J’s judgment:[77](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark76?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:1498 -->

\[44\] When liquidators and administrators seek directions from the Court in relation to any decision they have made, or propose to make, or in relation to any conduct they have undertaken, or propose to undertake, they are not seeking to determine rights and liabilities arising out of particular transactions, but are rather seeking protection against claims that they have acted unreasonably or inappropriately or in breach of their duty in making the decision or undertaking the conduct. They can obtain that protection if they make full and fair disclosure of all relevant facts and circumstances to the Court. In *Re G B Nathan & Co Pty Ltd (In Liq)* (1991) 24 NSWLR 674, McLelland J said at 679 – 680:

<!-- al:1499 -->

“The historical antecedents of s 479(3) ..., the terms of that subsection and the provisions of s 479 as a whole combine to lead to the conclusion that the only proper subject of a liquidator’s application for directions is the manner in which the liquidator should act in carrying out his functions as such ...

<!-- al:1500 -->

...

<!-- al:1501 -->

Modern Australian authority confirms the view that s 479(3) ‘does not enable the court to make binding orders in the nature of judgments’ and that the function of a liquidator’s application for directions ‘is to give him advice as to his proper course of action in the liquidation; it is not to determine the rights and liabilities arising from the company’s transactions before the liquidation’.” (Cases cited omitted.)

<!-- al:1502 -->

\[635\] Judge Paulsen concluded:

<!-- al:1503 -->

\[41\] I consider it most unlikely, given the legislative history and terms of s 284, as well as the themes advanced by the [Companies Act 1993](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/index.html), that Parliament intended to confer on liquidators the right to seek substantive judgments against third parties under the guise of seeking directions. Such claims are not in reality seeking directions and usually will be unsuitable for determination in a summary proceeding under [s 284](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s284.html) because of the need for particularised pleadings, discovery, interlocutory applications and the existence of significant factual disputes.

<!-- al:1504 -->

76 *Re Ansett Australia Ltd (No 3)* [\[2002\] FCA 901](http://www.austlii.edu.au/au/cases/cth/FCA/2002/901.html "View Case"), (2002) 115 FCR 409.

<!-- al:1505 -->

77 *Dalton v Mackley*, above n 72, at \[37\], citing *Re Ansett Australia Ltd (No 3)*, above n 76.

<!-- al:1506 -->

\[636\] The Judge then noted the possibility of regularising the proceeding by treating it as if it had been commenced in the correct manner. In that regard, he referred to an observation of McLelland J in *Re G B Nathan and Co Pty Ltd (in liq)*:[78](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark77?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:1507 -->

The procedures of the court are sufficiently flexible to enable proceedings commenced as an application for directions to be changed into proceedings for the determination of substantive rights, and this is sometimes a convenient course in order to avoid the need to commence further proceedings involving additional cost and delay... However it is important that the distinction between the two kinds of proceedings be not lost sight of or blurred, and such a fundamental change should not be permitted unless the court is satisfied that those affected either consent to that course ... or will not suffer injustice in consequence of the alteration to the status of the proceedings.

<!-- al:1508 -->

\[637\] Mr Moss accepted that *Dalton v Mackley* supported the general proposition that an application for directions should not be used as a shortcut to seek judgments against third parties. But he said that the case did not decide that an application for directions could not be used to determine third party rights where the third parties had been given a proper opportunity to be heard. He said that, in that respect, *Dalton* was distinguishable. There, the liquidators had brought an originating application, which (as Judge Paulsen noted) is a summary proceeding. Here, Mr Whitley was seeking directions under [s 284](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s284.html) in the context of an ordinary proceeding.

<!-- al:1509 -->

\[638\] In support of his submission, Mr Moss relied on a decision of the Federal Court of Australia, *Australian Securities Commission v Melbourne Asset Management Nominees Pty Ltd*[.79](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark78?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) There, the Court said:[80](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark79?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:1510 -->

It has been accepted that Courts have power to make final orders in preference claims on an application by a liquidator under sections similar to s 479(3) ... There is no logical reason why final orders binding on other persons cannot be made on applications under s 479(3) with respect to other subject matters. The second passage from \[*Re G B Nathan and Co Pty Ltd (in liq)*\] cited above refers to consent of parties or to cases where a party “will not suffer injustice in consequence of the alteration to the status of the proceedings”. This is the crucial matter. In proceedings brought by a liquidator under s 479(3), I can see no reason why binding orders cannot be made where the parties affected have been given the opportunity to be heard. ... \[In this case the\] parties affected by the directions and orders sought on the motion in the winding up proceedings are before the Court and have been heard.

<!-- al:1511 -->

78 *Re G B Nathan and Co Pty Ltd (in liq)* (1991) 24 NSWLR 674 (NSWSC) at 680, as quoted in

<!-- al:1512 -->

*Dalton v Mackley*, above n 72, at \[47\].

<!-- al:1513 -->

1.  *Australian Securities Commission v Melbourne Asset Management Nominees Pty Ltd* [\[1994\] FCA 1031](http://www.austlii.edu.au/au/cases/cth/FCA/1994/1031.html "View Case"); (1994) 49 FCR 334 (FCA).

<!-- al:1514 -->

80 At 352, referring to *Re G B Nathan and Co Pty Ltd (in liq)*, above n 78, at 680.

<!-- al:1515 -->

\[639\] Mr Moss noted that while Judge Paulsen had cited *G B Nathan* with approval, the Judge had not referred to *Melbourne Asset Management*. Mr Moss submitted that the correct position was that directions could be made under [s 284(1)(a)](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s284.html) affecting the substantive rights of third parties so long as those third parties had been given the opportunity to be heard. Here, he said, the defendants had been given every such opportunity.

<!-- al:1516 -->

\[640\] I consider, with respect, that the position is more nuanced than that suggested by the parties or by Judge Paulsen in *Dalton v Mackley*. There are two aspects to the issue regarding the scope of the directions that can be made under [s 284(1)(a).](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s284.html) One is procedural, the other substantive.

<!-- al:1517 -->

\[641\] The procedural aspect of the issue was the focus of cases such as *G B Nathan*, *Melbourne Asset Management* and *Dalton v Mackley*. Directions that affect the rights of third parties should, of course, be made only if the parties affected have had a proper opportunity to be heard. In most cases, a summary procedure (such as the originating application procedure used in *Dalton v Mackley*) will not provide a proper opportunity to be heard. But there may be some rare instances in which a summary procedure is sufficient. This was the position adopted by the Federal Court of Australia in *Meadow Springs Fairway Resort Ltd (in liq) v Balanced Securities Ltd*, in which the Court reconciled the apparently divergent positions taken in *Melbourne Asset Management* and *GB Nathan*[.81](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark80?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:1518 -->

\[642\] Here, I accept Mr Moss’s submission that the defendants were given a proper opportunity to be heard. Mr Whitley sought the directions in an ordinary proceeding. There was discovery. There were many interlocutory applications. There was a trial.

<!-- al:1519 -->

\[643\] But there is a substantive aspect to the issue as well. Mr Whitley advanced his claim on the basis that directions affecting the rights of third parties could be made under [s 284(1)(a)](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s284.html) even if the Whitley Parties did not succeed on their other causes of action against those third parties. Most obviously, the Whitley Parties sought repayment of various Project Costs from SPF and LPF under causes of action for breach of contract, knowing receipt and undue influence. Independent of those causes

<!-- al:1520 -->

81 *Meadow Springs Fairway Resort Ltd (in liq) v Balanced Securities Ltd* \[2007\] FCA 1443 at \[51\].

<!-- al:1521 -->

> of action, the Whitley Parties sought a direction under [s 284(1)(a)](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s284.html) that those various Project Costs were “unreasonable” expenses in the liquidations and that the sum of
>
> <!-- al:1522 -->
>
> $16,954,678 had to be repaid by SPF and LPF to the Whitley Parties.

<!-- al:1523 -->

\[644\] Mr Moss was unable to point me to any authority that [s 284(1)(a)](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s284.html) could be used in this way to bypass the requirements of the causes of action that a company in liquidation or a liquidator might have against a third party. I consider it is implicit in the Australian authorities that a provision such as [s 284(1)(a)](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s284.html) cannot be used in this way. In *Meadow Springs Fairway Resort*, for example, the Federal Court of Australia contemplated that if determination of an issue summarily under the equivalent to [s 284(1)(a)](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s284.html) was not appropriate the parties could instead “simply commence their own substantive proceedings”.[82](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark81?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) That observation makes sense only if the issue itself is to be resolved by applying the general law, rather than by some consideration of what is “reasonable” under [s 284(1)(a).](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s284.html)

<!-- al:1524 -->

\[645\] I therefore consider that [s 284(1)(a)](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s284.html) does not confer any jurisdiction to make substantive determinations of rights in the manner sought by Mr Whitley. The Whitley Parties’ claims against the defendants must be determined by applying the applicable substantive rules (such as of the law of contract and the law of knowing receipt). [Section 284(1)(a)](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s284.html) cannot be used to paper over any cracks that the Whitley Parties may have in those claims.

<!-- al:1525 -->

*Can the court make an order under [s 284(1)(b)](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s284.html) affecting rights acquired by a third party as a consequence of the challenged act or decision of the liquidator?*

<!-- al:1526 -->

\[646\] As noted, in his second cause of action Mr Whitley seeks an order under [s 284(1)(b)](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s284.html) reversing the decision of Mr Walker to enter into the Distribution Agreement. The Whitley Parties claim equivalent relief in their eighth cause of action in undue influence, seeking an order setting aside the Distribution Agreement.

<!-- al:1527 -->

\[647\] Mr Moss’s closing submissions made it clear that the order under s 284(1)(b) reversing Mr Walker’s decision was sought independently of the claim in undue influence. He said the ground on which the order was sought was that Mr Walker’s

<!-- al:1528 -->

82 At \[51\].

<!-- al:1529 -->

> decision to enter into the Distribution Agreement without first obtaining directions from the court was one no reasonable liquidator would have made. He acknowledged that an order reversing Mr Walker’s decision would affect LPF’s and SPF’s rights but said LPF and SPF knew that Mr Walker’s decision was unreasonable and proceeded to take the risk of entering into the Agreement anyway.

<!-- al:1530 -->

\[648\] Mr Salmon submitted that the power to reverse a liquidator’s actions under s 284(1)(b) does not extend to affecting rights acquired by an innocent third party as a consequence of the challenged act or decision of the liquidator. He relied on a decision of the Full Court of the Supreme Court of Victoria, *Re Chirnside*, in which Irvine CJ said, in relation to an equivalent provision in the bankruptcy context:[83](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark82?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:1531 -->

But I do not think that the actual words of the section, properly construed, cover, under the words “act or decision”, a completed sale by the trustee which, apart from this section, would pass the property to a purchaser. Had it been intended to vest in the Bankruptcy Court a general jurisdiction to set aside contracts entered into, for any reason, either for fraud, or innocent misrepresentation, or for imprudence, or for carelessness, or other breach of duty on the part of the trustee, or for any other reason, I think the Legislature would have adopted language much more apt for that purpose, and instead of saying that the “act or decision” may be “confirmed, reversed or modified,” would have used words apt to express such a power, with all its consequences.

<!-- al:1532 -->

...

<!-- al:1533 -->

... the words “act or decision” ... refer to matters between the trustee and some person aggrieved by a decision of his being disposed of, and were not intended to confer a general jurisdiction upon the Court of Bankruptcy in relation to rights acquired as a result of his act or decision by outsiders.

<!-- al:1534 -->

\[649\] While there is a great deal of sense in Irvine CJ’s decision, and it has been essentially adopted in one decision of the High Court,[84](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark83?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) it has not found universal favour. The Full Court of the Federal Court of Australia declined to follow it in *Willoughby v Official Trustee in Bankruptcy (WA)*.[85](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark84?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) In England and Wales it has been assumed, without the point having been decided, that the equivalent provision in that jurisdiction empowers a court to reverse a concluded transaction such that the rights of a third party are affected.[86](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark85?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:1535 -->

83 *Re Chirnside* [\[1929\] VicLawRp 22](http://www.austlii.edu.au/au/cases/vic/VicLawRp/1929/22.html "View Case"); \[1929\] VLR 217 (VSC) at 222–224.

<!-- al:1536 -->

84 *Commissioner of Inland Revenue v Hulst* (2000) 19 NZTC 15,693 at \[44\]–\[45\].

<!-- al:1537 -->

1.  *Willoughby v Official Trustee in Bankruptcy (WA)* [\[2000\] FCA 757](http://www.austlii.edu.au/au/cases/cth/FCA/2000/757.html "View Case"), (2000) 102 FCR 261 (FCAFC) at 32.
2.  *Re Edennote Ltd* [\[1996\] EWCA Civ 1349](http://www.bailii.org/ew/cases/EWCA/Civ/1996/1349.html "View Case"); \[1996\] 2 BCLC 389 (CA); and *Mahomed v Morris* [\[2000\] EWCA Civ 46](http://www.bailii.org/ew/cases/EWCA/Civ/2000/46.html "View Case"); \[2000\] 2 BCLC 536 (Ch) at 540, upheld on appeal in *Mahomed v Morris* \[2000\] All ER (D) 193 (CA).

<!-- al:1538 -->

\[650\] More significantly, and as Mr Moss submitted, the Court of Appeal in *Callis v Pardington* upheld a decision of Barker J under s 241(5) of the [Companies Act 1955](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_reg.20110506.OLD/ca1955r1994237/index.html) (the predecessor to s 284(1)(b)) setting aside an assignment that the liquidator had entered into.[87](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark86?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) This directly affected the rights of the putative assignee. While the Court did not directly engage with the question whether (the now) s 284(1)(b) empowers a court to reverse a transaction so as to affect the rights of a third party, nor refer to *Re Chirnside*, the decision is authority that s 284(1)(b) can be used in that way. I am bound to follow it.

<!-- al:1539 -->

\[651\] This merely means that s 284(1)(b) *can* be used in a way that affects the rights of third parties. The court has a discretion whether to make an order under s 284(1)(b), indicated by the “may” in the opening words. That a reversal would affect the rights of third parties will be a relevant consideration in determining whether to exercise the discretion. Further, the discretion will in any event only be exercised in cases of fraud, a lack of good faith or where the liquidator has acted unreasonably.[88](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark87?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:1540 -->

**Issue 10: In light of the Settlement Deed, are any of the claims against Mr Walker an abuse of process?**

<!-- al:1541 -->

\[652\] The Settlement Deed settled various claims made by Mr Walker (and entities associated with him) against Mr Henderson (and entities associated with him), and vice versa. Mr Henderson agreed that neither he nor any entity associated with him would bring any further claim against Mr Walker with respect to his conduct of the PVL liquidations, other than with respect to the claims outlined in the 1160 proceeding and any reasonable amendments as might be made to those claims.

<!-- al:1542 -->

\[653\] Mr Walker says that Mr Henderson has breached the Settlement Deed and that the 100 Parties and the Whitley Parties have either breached the Deed or knowingly induced breaches of it. Mr Walker alleges two breaches. First, he says Mr Henderson is “associated” with the Whitley Parties and so bringing the 274 proceeding in March 2022 and pursuing it was a breach of the Deed. Secondly, he says an amendment to the statement of claim in the 1160 proceeding in February 2022, to add a claim that Mr Walker breached his fiduciary duties, was a breach of the Deed.

<!-- al:1543 -->

87 *Callis v Pardington* (1996) 1 BCSLR 211 (CA).

<!-- al:1544 -->

88 At 215.

<!-- al:1545 -->

\[654\] Mr Walker says that the 274 proceeding and the breach of fiduciary duty claim in the 1160 proceeding are therefore abuses of process.

<!-- al:1546 -->

*The parties to and terms of the Settlement Deed*

<!-- al:1547 -->

\[655\] The Settlement Deed began by listing the following parties: Mr Walker in his personal capacity and as liquidator of PVL and the PVL subsidiaries; Mr Eathorne in his personal capacity and as liquidator of various companies; PVG Securities; Mr Henderson; Kristina Buxton (Mr Henderson’s partner); 100 Investments; RFD Finance; and FTG Securities.

<!-- al:1548 -->

\[656\] That list might suggest that PVL and the PVL subsidiaries were not themselves parties to the Settlement Deed. However, I find that they were parties to the Deed. Clause 1.11 states that Mr Walker agreed “on behalf of both himself personally as liquidator and on behalf of the \[PVL\] Companies” that the Deed settled various claims those companies had against Mr Henderson and Ms Buxton. Clause 11.2 is phrased similarly. Those clauses make sense only on the basis that PVL and the PVL subsidiaries were parties to the Deed. Further, the execution pages listed those companies as parties, with Mr Walker signing on their behalf as liquidator.

<!-- al:1549 -->

\[657\] The Settlement Deed includes the following provisions:

<!-- al:1550 -->

-   1.16 Ms Buxton and Mr Henderson or any entity associated with them or their interests agree not to bring any further claim against Mr Walker and Mr Eathorne and their advisors with respect to their conduct of the liquidations of the Companies up until the date of settlement, other than with respect to the claims outlined in the 100 Investments proceeding and subject to any reasonable amendments as may be made to that claim.

<!-- al:1551 -->

-   1.17 Ms Buxton and Mr Henderson or any entity associated with them or their interests agree not to bring any further claim against Mr Walker or his advisors with respect to his conduct of the liquidations of the Companies or touching on the issues in the proceedings, the liquidations or the Companies including any transactions involving property of the Companies, other than with respect to the claims outlined in the 100 Investment proceeding and subject to any reasonable amendments as may be made to that claim.

<!-- al:1552 -->

-   1.18 Ms Buxton and Mr Henderson agree to seek to procure that any liquidator appointed by them or their entities, or any person in any way associated with them, also will not bring any claims or take any steps \[of\] any nature against Mr Walker or Mr Eathorne arising out of

<!-- al:1553 -->

companies in which Mr Henderson and/or Ms Buxton have had an interest.

<!-- al:1554 -->

...

<!-- al:1555 -->

**Further assurances**

<!-- al:1556 -->

1.24 Each party shall do all things, execute and deliver all documents and use all reasonable endeavours to do all things as may be reasonably requested by that other party to obtain the full benefit of this deed according to its true intent.

<!-- al:1557 -->

\[658\] The following issues arise:

<!-- al:1558 -->

> <!-- al:1559 -->
>
> (a) Are the Whitley Parties associated with Mr Henderson?
>
> <!-- al:1560 -->
>
> (b) If so, is pursuit of the 274 proceeding a breach of the Settlement Deed and therefore an abuse of process?
>
> <!-- al:1561 -->
>
> (c) Is pursuit of the fourth cause of action in the 1160 proceeding an abuse of process?

<!-- al:1562 -->

*Are the Whitley Parties associated with Mr Henderson?*

<!-- al:1563 -->

\[659\] Mr Moss submitted that, reading the Settlement Deed as a whole, an entity was “associated” with Mr Henderson only if Mr Henderson both controlled the entity and had a beneficial interest in it. He said Mr Whitley and the other Whitley Parties were not under Mr Henderson’s control: Mr Whitley brought the 274 proceeding in discharge of his duty as liquidator; his duties were to the unsecured creditors of the PVL companies; and Mr Whitley was clear in his evidence that he was not beholden to Mr Henderson. Further, Mr Henderson did not have any beneficial interest in any of the Whitley Parties.

<!-- al:1564 -->

\[660\] I consider Mr Moss’s interpretation of “associated” is unduly narrow. I accept Mr Cooper’s submissions as to the meaning of that word in the Settlement Deed. It is clear, reading the Deed as a whole, that the purpose of the Deed was to settle a broad range of claims that had been made between the parties and to prevent further claims being made (other than the claims in the 1160 proceeding). The purpose was to protect the defendants from further claims rather than, as Mr Moss might have it, to prevent

<!-- al:1565 -->

> Mr Henderson (or any other claimant) from enjoying some beneficial return from further claims. That purpose could be undermined if Mr Henderson or Ms Buxton were at liberty to arrange for a third party to bring the further claims that they had undertaken not to bring. Clauses 1.16, 1.17 and 1.18 addressed that potential gap by extending the no-claim undertaking to any entity or person “associated” with Mr Henderson, Ms Buxton or their interests. In this context, I agree with Mr Cooper that “associated” means “\[j\]oined in companionship; united in action or purpose ... allied”.[89](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark88?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:1566 -->

\[661\] On that meaning, all the Whitley Parties are associated with Mr Henderson. The relevant facts all point one way. Mr Henderson paid Mr Whitley $10,000 (through FTG Securities) shortly before Mr Whitley was appointed liquidator of the PVL companies (the other Whitley Parties). Mr Henderson was instrumental in arranging for Mr Whitley to be appointed as liquidator. Mr Henderson introduced Mr Moss (with whom Mr Henderson has a long-term professional relationship) to Mr Whitley for the purpose of representing the Whitley Parties in these proceedings. Mr Henderson agreed to indemnify and fund Mr Whitley for the costs of the liquidations, including the costs of this litigation (and then actively concealed this arrangement). More recently, Mr Moss’s fees for these proceedings have been paid directly by Mr Henderson rather than Mr Whitley. There was ample evidence that Mr Henderson has been actively involved in the conduct of the 274 proceeding.

<!-- al:1567 -->

*Is pursuit of the 274 proceeding a breach of the Settlement Deed and therefore an abuse of process?*

<!-- al:1568 -->

\[662\] Mr Moss submitted that even if the Whitley Parties are associated with Mr Henderson, the 274 proceeding is materially the same as the 1160 proceeding that was excluded from cls 1.16 and 1.17 and that therefore the pursuit of the 274 proceeding does not breach the Settlement Deed.

<!-- al:1569 -->

\[663\] I do not accept that submission. First, the exclusion in cls 1.16 and 1.17 is of “the claims outlined” in the 1160 proceeding and “any reasonable amendments as may be made to \[those\] claim\[s\]”. It is true, as Mr Moss submitted, that the facts underlying

<!-- al:1570 -->

89 *Oxford English Dictionary* (2nd ed, Clarendon Press, Oxford, 1989) vol 1 at 718.

<!-- al:1571 -->

> the claims in the two proceedings are broadly the same and that there is some similarity in the legal basis of the claims advanced by the two sets of plaintiffs. But that is to focus solely on the basis of the claims. In identifying a claim, and therefore in determining whether one claim is the same as another, one must look not only at the basis of the claims but also at the parties to it (and at the remedy or relief sought). Here, the plaintiffs in the two proceedings are different and so the claims are different. Moreover, there are claims in the 274 proceeding that are not advanced in the 1160 proceeding. This means the claims in the 274 proceeding are not ones that are excluded from the operation of cls 1.16 and 1.17.

<!-- al:1572 -->

\[664\] Secondly, there is no equivalent exclusion in cl 1.18. There, Mr Henderson agreed:

<!-- al:1573 -->

... to seek to procure that any liquidator appointed by them or their entities, or any person in any way associated with them, also will not bring any claims or take any steps \[of\] any nature against Mr Walker ... arising out of companies in which Mr Henderson and/or Ms Buxton have had an interest.

<!-- al:1574 -->

> <!-- al:1575 -->
>
> The Whitley Parties are associated with Mr Henderson. They have brought claims and taken steps against Mr Walker in the 274 proceeding. Those claims and steps arise out of the PVL companies in which Mr Henderson had an interest.

<!-- al:1576 -->

\[665\] It follows that the pursuit of the 274 proceeding against Mr Walker is a breach of cls 1.16, 1.17 and 1.18 of the Settlement Deed by Mr Henderson and the 100 Parties (other than Tomanavich Holdings). This has implications for Mr Walker’s counterclaim against those parties but by itself does not mean that pursuit of the 274 proceeding against Mr Walker is an abuse of process. For there to be an abuse, the plaintiffs in the 274 proceeding, the Whitley Parties, must be abusing process.

<!-- al:1577 -->

\[666\] The Whitley Parties, other than Mr Whitley (that is, the PVL companies), are parties to the Settlement Deed. They are associated with Mr Henderson, and so it is arguable that they are in breach of cls 1.16, 1.17 and 1.18. I did not hear detailed argument on that point. It is unnecessary for me to decide it, because they are clearly in breach of the further assurances provision, cl 1.24. Clause 1.24 is engaged by Mr Walker making a request for another party to execute a document or do some other thing. While he made no formal request, it was implicit from his pleading of an

<!-- al:1578 -->

> affirmative defence based on the Deed that he was requesting the Whitley Parties to cease the 274 proceeding against him. The PVL companies breached cl 1.24 by continuing to pursue the proceeding against Mr Walker. This means it was an abuse of process for them to continue to pursue the 274 proceeding against Mr Walker.

<!-- al:1579 -->

\[667\] Mr Whitley was not a party to the Settlement Deed and so did not breach it. Mr Walker pleaded that it was nonetheless an abuse of process for Mr Whitley to pursue the 274 proceeding against him. Two essential grounds were pleaded. The first was that Mr Whitley’s conduct in pursuing the proceeding was in breach of the PVL companies’ obligations under cl 1.24 of the Deed. The second was that Mr Whitley had knowingly induced Mr Henderson’s breaches of the Deed or his abuse of process.

<!-- al:1580 -->

\[668\] As to the first ground, every claim that Mr Whitley makes in the 274 proceeding against Mr Walker is made on behalf of the PVL companies. I therefore consider that Mr Whitley can be in no better position, so far as the question of abuse is concerned, than the PVL companies. If it is an abuse for party A to bring a claim, the abuse does not disappear by party B bringing the claim on party A’s behalf. It is therefore just as much an abuse for Mr Whitley to continue to pursue the proceeding against Mr Walker as it is for the PVL companies to do so.

<!-- al:1581 -->

\[669\] This means it is not necessary for me to determine, for the purposes of determining the abuse of process point, whether Mr Whitley knowingly induced Mr Henderson to breach the Settlement Deed. It is, however, a matter to which I return when considering Mr Walker’s counterclaim against the Whitley Parties

<!-- al:1582 -->

*Is pursuit of the fourth cause of action in the 1160 proceeding an abuse of process?*

<!-- al:1583 -->

\[670\] The fourth cause of action in the 1160 proceeding alleges breach of fiduciary duty by Mr Walker (and knowing receipt by SPF and LPF). This claim was not pleaded at the time of the Settlement Deed. Mr Walker says the fiduciary duty claim against him is different from the claims that were pleaded at that time and is not a “reasonable amendment” to them. He says it is therefore barred by cls 1.16 and 1.17.

<!-- al:1584 -->

\[671\] Mr Barker submitted that the fourth cause of action was a reasonable amendment to the existing claims because it was largely a reformulation of those claims. He said the fiduciary duty claim was largely the same as the causes of action under s 301 of the Companies Act (now, the fifth and sixth causes of action).

<!-- al:1585 -->

\[672\] In cls 1.16 and 1.17 Mr Henderson, Ms Buxton and entities associated with them agreed not to bring any further “claim” against Mr Walker, other than with respect to the “claims” that were then outlined in the 1160 proceeding and subject to any reasonable amendments as may be made to those claims. In the context of a proceeding, a “claim” means a demand for some remedy or relief based on a particular set of alleged facts — in other words, a cause of action. The carve out in cls 1.16 and

<!-- al:1586 -->

> 1.17 was therefore for reasonable amendments to the existing causes of action. The fourth cause of action was entirely new. It was not an amendment to an existing cause of action. And even if “claim” were to be interpreted more broadly as meaning “allegation”, I do not consider the allegations in the fourth cause of action to be reasonable amendments to the allegations in the two causes of action under s 301 of the [Companies Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_reg.20110506.OLD/ca1955r1994237/index.html). There is some overlap between the allegations in those causes of action, but it is not significant. The alleged breaches are for the most part quite different.

<!-- al:1587 -->

\[673\] It follows that bringing the fourth cause of action against Mr Walker was a breach of the Settlement Deed by Mr Henderson and by the other parties to the Deed. Those parties included all the 100 Parties except Tomanavich Holdings. However, it is clear that Tomanavich Holdings knowingly — through Mr Henderson — participated in the breach. The 100 Parties’ pursuit of this cause of action is therefore an abuse of process.

<!-- al:1588 -->

**Issue 11: Are the claims under s 301 of the [Companies Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_reg.20110506.OLD/ca1955r1994237/index.html) out of time under the [Limitation Act 2010](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/index.html)?**

<!-- al:1589 -->

\[674\] Both sets of plaintiffs make claims against Mr Walker under s 301 of the [Companies Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_reg.20110506.OLD/ca1955r1994237/index.html). Section 301 provides, relevantly:

<!-- al:1590 -->

1.  **Power of court to require persons to repay money or return property**

<!-- al:1591 -->

(1) If, in the course of the liquidation of a company, it appears to the court that a person who has taken part in the formation or promotion of the company, or a past or present director, manager, administrator, liquidator, or receiver of the company, has misapplied, or retained, or become liable or accountable for, money or property of the company, or been guilty of negligence, default, or breach of duty or trust in relation to the company, the court may, on the application of the liquidator or a creditor or shareholder,—

<!-- al:1592 -->

> <!-- al:1593 -->
>
> (a) inquire into the conduct of the promoter, director, manager, administrator, liquidator, or receiver; and
>
> <!-- al:1594 -->
>
> (b) order that person—
>
> <!-- al:1595 -->
>
> (i) to repay or restore the money or property or any part of it with interest at a rate the court thinks just; or
>
> <!-- al:1596 -->
>
> (ii) to contribute such sum to the assets of the company by way of compensation as the court thinks just; or
>
> <!-- al:1597 -->
>
> (c) where the application is made by a creditor, order that person to pay or transfer the money or property or any part of it with interest at a rate the court thinks just to the creditor.

<!-- al:1598 -->

...

<!-- al:1599 -->

\[675\] The claims by the plaintiffs under s 301 are all claims for monetary relief. They are each therefore a “money claim” in terms of the [Limitation Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/index.html).[90](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark89?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) Under [s 11(1)](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/s11.html) of the [Limitation Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/index.html), a money claim is generally time-barred if it is filed at least six years after the date of the act or omission on which the claim is based (the claim’s “primary period”), unless the late knowledge provisions in the Act apply.

<!-- al:1600 -->

\[676\] However, [s 16](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/s16.html) of the [Limitation Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/index.html) specifies special start dates for various money claims. Under [s 16(1)(i)](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/s16.html), the start date for the primary period of a claim under s 301 is “the date on which the liquidator of the company ... was appointed”. Mr Walker says that means the date on which the first liquidator was appointed to the company. If he is correct, all the s 301 claims were filed after the six-year primary period: the first liquidators were appointed to the PVL companies at various dates up to 9 February 2012, and all claims were filed after 9 February 2018.

<!-- al:1601 -->

\[677\] The plaintiffs, by contrast, say that [s 16(1)(i)](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/s16.html) refers to the date of appointment of the current liquidator, in this case, Mr Whitley. If they are correct, the claims were

<!-- al:1602 -->

90 [Section 12(1).](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/s12.html)

<!-- al:1603 -->

> filed within the primary period, as Mr Whitley was not appointed as liquidator of the companies until various dates in 2021 and 2022. In the alternative, the plaintiffs rely on the late knowledge provisions of the [Limitation Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/index.html). As a further alternative, the plaintiffs rely on [s 50](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/s50.html) of the [Limitation Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/index.html), under which the court has a discretion to grant relief on a time-barred claim if it is ancillary to another claim which was made within time. The plaintiffs did not place any reliance on [s 49](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/s49.html) of the Act (which deals with the conversion of trust property).

<!-- al:1604 -->

*Does “the liquidator” in [s 16(1)(i)](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/s16.html) mean “the first liquidator” or “the current liquidator”?*

<!-- al:1605 -->

\[678\] Mr Walker and the plaintiffs each said that the other’s interpretation would produce absurd outcomes. The plaintiffs said Mr Walker’s interpretation would allow a liquidator to act with impunity and wait out the six-year period from their appointment to escape liability. Mr Walker said the plaintiffs’ interpretation would mean the limitation period could be continually restarted by the appointment of a new liquidator. For example, the 100 Parties first made their s 301 claims in June 2019, when Mr Walker was still liquidator. On the plaintiffs’ interpretation, those claims were time-barred when first made but were revived by the limitation period being restarted on Mr Whitley’s appointment as liquidator.

<!-- al:1606 -->

\[679\] The meaning of [s 16(1)(i)](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/s16.html) must be ascertained from its text and in the light of its purpose and its context[.91](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark90?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:1607 -->

\[680\] The text of [s 16(1)(i)](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/s16.html) provides no assistance on this interpretative issue. It refers merely to the date of appointment of “the liquidator” of the company. This could mean either the first liquidator or the current liquidator.

<!-- al:1608 -->

\[681\] The purpose of the [Limitation Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/index.html) provides limited assistance on the issue. [Section 3](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/s3.html) says the purpose of the Act is “to encourage claimants to make claims for monetary or other relief without undue delay by providing defendants with defences to stale claims”. Mr Walker can say that the plaintiffs’ interpretation, by allowing the limitation period to be continually reset, would mean stale claims could be advanced.

<!-- al:1609 -->

91 [Legislation Act 2019](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2019133/index.html), [s 10(1).](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2019133/s10.html)

<!-- al:1610 -->

> Mr Whitley can say that he did not act with undue delay once he was appointed liquidator. The position is different for creditors, as the 100 Parties claim to be, who make a s 301 claim. The plaintiffs’ interpretation would mean that the appointment of a new liquidator could rescue a creditor from the consequences of undue delay and strip a liquidator (or director or other defendant to a s 301 claim) of a defence to a stale claim.

<!-- al:1611 -->

\[682\] More assistance is obtained from the purpose of [s 16(1)(i).](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2019133/s16.html) This purpose can be ascertained by considering the way in which previous limitation statutes applied to claims under s 301 of the [Companies Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_reg.20110506.OLD/ca1955r1994237/index.html) (or its predecessors).

<!-- al:1612 -->

\[683\] Under previous limitation statutes, time started running when a cause of action accrued. Previous statutes did not have an equivalent to s 16 of the 2010 Act, which specifies special start dates for particular claims. A claim under s 301 of the [Companies Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_reg.20110506.OLD/ca1955r1994237/index.html) (or its predecessors) was therefore subject to the general rules found in limitation statutes.

<!-- al:1613 -->

\[684\] The Court of Appeal was concerned with the application of a limitation period to a claim under s 254 of the [Companies Act 1908](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/hist_act_1908/ca1908107/index.html) (a predecessor to s 301 of the 1993 Act) in *Re J E Hurdley and Son Ltd (in liq)*.[92](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark91?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) The issue was whether time began running under s 254 from the commencement of the company’s liquidation or from the date the cause of action arose. The Court said it was the latter. Myers CJ said the reason for this was plain:[93](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark92?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:1614 -->

\[Section 254\] does not create any new cause of action. It is merely procedural, and does no more than provide a summary method of procedure with the object of simplifying litigation and minimizing expense. What it contemplates is merely a new method of procedure for the determination of existing causes of action: it does not create new liabilities or new rights.

<!-- al:1615 -->

\[685\] In *Re Maney and Sons De Luxe Service Station Ltd*, the Court of Appeal addressed the application of the [Limitation Act 1950](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/repealed_act.20250905.OLD/la1950133/index.html) to a claim under s 320 of the [Companies Act 1955](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_reg.20110506.OLD/ca1955r1994237/index.html).[94](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark93?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) Section 320 does not have a direct equivalent in the

<!-- al:1616 -->

92 *Re J E Hurdley and Son Ltd (in liq)* [\[1941\] NZGazLawRp 45](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/cases/NZGazLawRp/1941/45.html "View Case"); \[1941\] NZLR 686 (CA).

<!-- al:1617 -->

93 At 723.

<!-- al:1618 -->

94 *Re Maney and Sons De Luxe Service Station Ltd* \[1969\] NZLR 116 (CA).

<!-- al:1619 -->

> [Companies Act 1993](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/index.html).[95](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark94?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) However, in its analysis of [s 320](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s320.html) the Court also addressed s 321 of the 1955 Act, which is the immediate predecessor to s 301. The Court held that s 320 created a new cause of action of which the liquidation of the company was a material part. This meant that time did not begin to run for limitation purposes until commencement of the liquidation. The Court contrasted that with the position under s 321, which they saw, as the Court had earlier done in *Hurdley*, as a procedural section that did not create any new cause of action.[96](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark95?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:1620 -->

\[686\] In *Arataki Properties Ltd v Craig*, the liquidator brought a claim under s 321 against a company’s directors.[97](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark96?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) One of the directors pleaded that the claim was time-barred. The liquidator argued that the cause of action accrued at the date the liquidation commenced rather than at the date on which the company could first have issued proceedings in respect of the alleged beach and invited the Court of Appeal not to follow *Hurdley*.

<!-- al:1621 -->

\[687\] The Court of Appeal declined the invitation. Cooke P delivered the leading judgment, with which McMullin, Somers and Casey JJ agreed. Cooke P acknowledged that it would be easier for liquidators to carry out their investigations of the affairs of failed companies if a cause of action under s 321 were treated as accruing at the date of liquidation rather than the date of breach.[98](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark97?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) But he said there was a “strong current” of authority behind *Hurdley*, and he was not persuaded to depart from it.[99](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark98?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) He added:[100](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark99?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:1622 -->

The settled interpretation that the section provides a new way of examining into and enforcing an existing liability to the company should not be disturbed by judicial decision. Perhaps there are arguments of policy for extending the time scope of the section; but to say the least they are not overwhelming. If there is to be any change it is best left to the legislature.

<!-- al:1623 -->

\[688\] The legislature did make a change by enacting [s 16(1)(i)](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/s16.html) of the [Limitation Act 2010](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/index.html). Clearly, the purpose of the provision was to address a perceived

<!-- al:1624 -->

95 [Sections 135](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s135.html) and [136](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s136.html) of the [Companies Act 1993](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/index.html) cover some of the ground previously covered by s 320 of the [Companies Act 1955](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_reg.20110506.OLD/ca1955r1994237/index.html) but are in different language and are not dependent on a company being in liquidation.

<!-- al:1625 -->

96 At 125 per North P, 130–131 per Turner J and 134 per Haslam J.

<!-- al:1626 -->

97 *Arataki Properties Ltd v Craig* \[1986\] 2 NZLR 294 (CA).

<!-- al:1627 -->

98 At 296.

<!-- al:1628 -->

99 At 298.

<!-- al:1629 -->

100 At 298.

<!-- al:1630 -->

> problem with the consequences of *Hurdley*, *Maney* and *Arataki*. Beyond that, the legislative history of the Act provides no assistance. There was no discussion of special start dates in the first two Law Commission Reports leading to the 2010 Act.[101](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark100?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) The potential for inclusion of special start dates for some claims was raised in the third Law Commission Report, but not in respect of s 301 claims.[102](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark101?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) The provision that became s 16(1)(i) made its first appearance as cl 15(1)(h) of the [Limitation Bill 2009](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/bill/lb2009143/index.html).[103](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark102?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) The explanatory note to the Bill said:[104](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark103?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:1631 -->

Clause 15 specifies special start dates for various money claims. The special start date for a claim under [section 301](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/s301.html) of the [Companies Act 1993](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/ca1993107/index.html) (which, in a liquidation, empowers the court to require persons to repay money or return property), for example, is the date on which the liquidator of the company or overseas company was appointed. When time should begin to run for claims under corresponding earlier provisions in the [Companies Act 1955](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_reg.20110506.OLD/ca1955r1994237/index.html) was discussed in *Re Maney and Sons De Luxe Service Station Limited, Maney v Cowan* \[1969\] NZLR 116 (CA).

<!-- al:1632 -->

\[689\] In the absence of any specific guidance in the legislative history, the purpose of s 16(1)(i) is best understood by considering the contest in each of *Hurdley*, *Maney* and *Arataki*. The contest was between time running from the date on which the underlying cause of action arose (which, in the case of a claim against directors or others involved in managing the company, will predate liquidation) and time running from the date of liquidation. The perceived problem that s 16(1)(i) remedies is that, as a consequence of the Court of Appeal opting for the former over the latter, in practice a liquidator would have less than six years (sometimes much less) from the date of liquidation to bring a claim under s 301 against a delinquent director or other manager. The purpose of s 16(1)(i), therefore, is to remedy the problems faced by liquidators in having (in)sufficient time to bring s 301 claims against directors or others whose acts or omissions predated liquidation.

<!-- al:1633 -->

\[690\] That this is the purpose of s 16(1)(i) is revealed by the drafter’s apparent lack of thought as to how the provision would work where the s 301 claim is against, rather than by, a liquidator. Such a claim will always be in respect of an act or omission that

<!-- al:1634 -->

1.  Law Commission *Limitation Defences in Civil Proceedings* (NZLC R6, 1988); and Law Commission *Tidying the Limitation Act* (NZLC R61, 2000).

<!-- al:1635 -->

102 Law Commission *Limitation Defences in Civil Cases: Update Report for the Law Commission*

<!-- al:1636 -->

(NZLC MP16, 2007) at \[64\] and following.

<!-- al:1637 -->

103 [Limitation Bill 2009](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/bill/lb2009143/index.html) (33-1).

<!-- al:1638 -->

104 [Limitation Bill 2009](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/bill/lb2009143/index.html) (33-1) (explanatory note) at 8.

<!-- al:1639 -->

> post-dates liquidation. If an aggrieved creditor or shareholder wishes to bring a s 301 claim alleging that the current liquidator has misapplied company property, the effect of s 16(1)(i) is that time will have started to run on the date on which the liquidator was appointed, even though the liquidator’s alleged misapplication will have occurred after appointment. So, although the evident purpose of s 16(1)(i) is to delay the start date of the limitation period, in most claims against a liquidator the effect of the provision will be to accelerate the start date.

<!-- al:1640 -->

\[691\] Given that the purpose of the provision is to remedy the problems faced by liquidators in having (in)sufficient time to bring s 301 claims against directors or others whose acts or omissions predated liquidation, the present interpretative issue

<!-- al:1641 -->

-   — which arises only where there have been successive appointments of liquidators — should be resolved by reference to that purpose. Mr Walker’s interpretation better matches that purpose. If time starts to run on the date on which the first liquidator is appointed, there are six years from that date for that liquidator and any successor liquidator to bring a s 301 claim against the delinquent director. On the plaintiffs’ interpretation, the limitation period would be restarted on the appointment of a successor liquidator, and there would be no limit on how many times it could be restarted by further appointments of liquidators. The [Limitation Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/index.html)’[s 15](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/s15.html)\-year longstop period then could not apply, as it applies only if a claim is made after its primary period.[105](2025_3486.html#_bookmark104) I can see no good reason for the [Limitation Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/index.html) operating in that way.

<!-- al:1642 -->

\[692\] I therefore consider that in [s 16(1)(i)](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/s16.html) the date on which “the liquidator” was appointed means the date on which “the first liquidator” was appointed. The effect of this interpretation is that time starts to run for a s 301 claim on the date of the company’s liquidation. I appreciate that the legislature could easily have drafted [s 16(1)(i)](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/s16.html) to say this expressly but did not. I also acknowledge Mr Moss’s point that there is a contrast with [s 16(1)(j)](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/s16.html), which says the start date for certain claims in respect of a corporation or bank subject to statutory management is “the date on which the corporation or bank was declared to be subject to statutory management”. But those points are answered by the fact that s 241(5) of the [Companies Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_reg.20110506.OLD/ca1955r1994237/index.html) says that the

<!-- al:1643 -->

105 [Limitation Act 2010](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/index.html), [s 11(2)(b).](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/s11.html)

<!-- al:1644 -->

> liquidation of a company “commences on the date on which ... the liquidator is appointed”. The date on which the liquidator is appointed is therefore the same as — and is understood as a proxy for — the date of commencement of the liquidation. The position is different under the respective statutes allowing for the statutory management of corporations or banks. Neither contains an equivalent to s 241(5). Both provide that the statutory management commences on the date and time specified in the Order in Council declaring that a corporation or bank is subject to statutory management.[106](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark105?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:1645 -->

\[693\] Finally, this interpretation does not produce the absurd outcome raised by the plaintiffs. They said that the interpretation would allow a liquidator to act with impunity and wait out the six-year period from their appointment to escape liability. This overlooks that s 301 is merely a procedural provision. It does not create any new cause of action. If a liquidator breaches their duties to the company, the company will have a cause of action against the liquidator. A replacement liquidator can enforce that cause of action by bringing an ordinary proceeding in the name of the company. The replacement liquidator is not limited to bringing a claim under s 301. The limitation period for the ordinary proceeding will, under [s 11(1)](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/s11.html), start on the date of the act or omission on which the claim is based. It will not be subject to the special start date in [s 16(1)(i).](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/s16.html)

<!-- al:1646 -->

\[694\] I have considered whether the claims that Mr Whitley has brought under s 301 could be characterised as ordinary proceedings by the PVL companies. I see no basis on which that could be done on the pleadings. Indeed, in his closing submissions Mr Moss stated that “it is Mr Whitley bringing these causes of action rather than all the Whitley plaintiffs”.

<!-- al:1647 -->

\[695\] The consequence of this interpretation is that all the plaintiffs’ s 301 claims are time-barred, unless the plaintiffs can avail themselves of the late knowledge or ancillary claims provisions of the [Limitation Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/index.html).

<!-- al:1648 -->

1.  [Corporations (Investigation and Management) Act 1989](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/cama1989422/index.html), [s 38(3)](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/cama1989422/s38.html); and [Banking (Prudential Supervision) Act 1989](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/bsa1989338/index.html), [s 117(4).](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/bsa1989338/s117.html)

<!-- al:1649 -->

*Can the plaintiffs avail themselves of the late knowledge provisions of the [Limitation Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/index.html)?*

<!-- al:1650 -->

\[696\] Under [s 11(2)](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/s11.html) of the [Limitation Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/index.html), if a claimant has late knowledge of the claim and the claim is made after its primary period, the applicable limitation period is three years after the late knowledge date (the claim’s “late knowledge period”).[107](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark106?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) The “late knowledge date” is defined in [s 14.](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/s14.html) It is the date on which the claimant gained knowledge (or, if earlier, ought reasonably to have gained knowledge) of all the facts on which the claim is based.

<!-- al:1651 -->

\[697\] The 100 Parties plead that the relevant facts on which their s 301 claims are based were not known to them until after September 2017, when they were advised of the disbursement of funds under the Distribution Agreement. Their s 301 claims plead that they suffered loss as a result of the distributions made under that Agreement. Plainly, they did not know of that loss until the distributions were made. Their s 301 claims were brought within three years of September 2017 and so are within the late knowledge period.

<!-- al:1652 -->

\[698\] As for the Whitley Parties, Mr Moss submitted that Mr Whitley was only appointed replacement liquidator of the PVL companies between 22 September 2021 and 25 February 2022 and that he could not have had knowledge of the relevant acts or omissions before those dates. He said the s 301 claims were brought within three years of those dates.

<!-- al:1653 -->

\[699\] I accept both points. However, Mr Moss’s submission assumes that it is Mr Whitley’s knowledge that counts for the purposes of the late knowledge provisions. The [Limitation Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/index.html) defines “claimant” as “the person on whose behalf a claim is made (whether by that person or another person)”.[108](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark107?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) A liquidator who makes a s 301 claim does so on behalf of the company in liquidation. It is the company that has the cause of action and, as cases such as *Arataki* make clear, s 301 is merely procedural. That the liquidator is making the claim on the company’s behalf is reinforced by s 301(1)(b), the effect of which is that any orders made on a successful claim by a liquidator will be for the benefit of the company.

<!-- al:1654 -->

107 [Section 11(3).](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/s11.html)

<!-- al:1655 -->

108 [Section 4](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/s4.html) definition of “claimant”.

<!-- al:1656 -->

\[700\] What matters, therefore, is when the PVL companies knew of the relevant acts and omissions. Several people who acted in the liquidation of the companies — primarily Mr Walker but also Mr Eathorne and, in the case of PVL, Mr Scutter — knew of the relevant facts by late 2017. If the knowledge of any of them is attributed to the PVL companies, the Whitley Parties cannot avail themselves of the late knowledge provisions. It is only if their knowledge is not attributed to the companies that the late knowledge provisions are available.

<!-- al:1657 -->

\[701\] Mr Cooper said the companies were fixed with knowledge of all relevant events as they occurred, but he did not elaborate. Mr Moss submitted that Mr Walker’s knowledge could not be attributed to Mr Whitley, but he did not address whether Mr Walker’s knowledge (or Mr Scutter’s or Mr Eathorne’s) could be attributed to the PVL companies.

<!-- al:1658 -->

\[702\] In support of his submission, Mr Moss relied on *PMT 20210 Ltd (in liq) v Mark*.[109](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark108?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) In *PMT* a liquidator brought a claim under [s 348](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/pla2007179/s348.html) of the [Property Law Act 2007](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/pla2007179/index.html) against the company’s directors to set aside a disposition of property. The claim was brought outside the primary period in the [Limitation Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/index.html). The liquidator therefore relied on the late knowledge provisions of the Act. The defendants contended that the liquidator ought reasonably to have known the relevant facts early in the liquidation, some time before he gained actual knowledge. If the defendants were right, the claim was out of time. Grice J held that the liquidator could not reasonably have gained the relevant knowledge at the time the defendants contended.[110](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark109?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) The claim was therefore brought within the late knowledge period. *PMT* is not relevant to the attribution issue in this case, as it was not contended that the claimant was the company (rather than the liquidator) and so there was no consideration of whether the directors’ knowledge should be attributed to the company.

<!-- al:1659 -->

\[703\] Whether the knowledge of an alleged wrongdoer should be attributed to a company for the purposes of the late knowledge provisions of the [Limitation Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/index.html) is a

<!-- al:1660 -->

1.  *PMT 20210 Ltd (in liq) v Mark* [\[2022\] NZHC 169.](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/cases/NZHC/2022/169.html "View Case") I note there is an error in the intituling of the judgment. The first plaintiff’s registered name was PMT 2010 Ltd.

<!-- al:1661 -->

110 At \[73\].

<!-- al:1662 -->

> difficult question. It is not necessary for me to answer it. Even if Mr Walker’s and Mr Scutter’s knowledge were not attributed to the PVL companies, Mr Eathorne’s would be. It follows that the Whitley Parties cannot avail themselves of the late knowledge provisions.

<!-- al:1663 -->

*Should relief be granted on the s 301 claims as ancillary claims?*

<!-- al:1664 -->

\[704\] [Section 50](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/s50.html) of the [Limitation Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/index.html) confers on the court a discretion to grant relief on a time-barred claim if it is ancillary to another claim which was made within time. The discretion may be exercised “if \[the court\] thinks it just to do so on an application made to it for \[that\] purpose”.[111](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark110?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\))

<!-- al:1665 -->

\[705\] The 100 Parties made, in their reply to Mr Walker’s defence, an application for relief under [s 50.](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/s50.html) It is not necessary to consider that application, given that their s 301 claims are within the late knowledge period.

<!-- al:1666 -->

\[706\] The Whitley Parties did not make any application for relief under [s 50.](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/s50.html) They did not raise it in opening. Their reliance on [s 50](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/s50.html) appeared for the first time in closing submissions. As a consequence, Mr Walker did not address in his closing the issues that would have been raised had an application been made or had reliance on [s 50](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/s50.html) at least been signalled in opening. In these circumstances, I decline to grant relief to the Whitley Parties under [s 50.](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/s50.html)

<!-- al:1667 -->

**100 Parties’ first cause of action (against Mr Walker, LPF and SPF): conversion**

<!-- al:1668 -->

\[707\] The 100 Parties’ first cause of action is in conversion. It depends on them showing that they are secured creditors of the PVL subsidiaries. They have failed to do so, and for that reason this cause of action fails.

<!-- al:1669 -->

\[708\] Had the 100 Parties shown that they are secured creditors of the PVL subsidiaries, I would have found the net amount to which they would have been entitled from the settlement proceeds (after Mr Walker exercised his right of recoupment) to be $229,840.

<!-- al:1670 -->

111 [Section 50(3).](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/s50.html)

<!-- al:1671 -->

\[709\] Given that the 100 Parties failed to show that they are secured creditors of the PVL subsidiaries, it is not necessary for me to determine whether a chose in action (the 100 Parties’ right to a portion of the settlement proceeds) can be converted.

<!-- al:1672 -->

**100 Parties’ second cause of action (against Mr Walker, LPF and SPF): money had and received**

<!-- al:1673 -->

\[710\] This cause of action fails for the same reason as the 100 Parties’ first cause of action. It is not necessary for me to determine whether the various other elements of the claim in money had and received could be established against the defendants.

<!-- al:1674 -->

**100 Parties’ third cause of action (against Mr Walker, LPF and SPF): tracing**

<!-- al:1675 -->

\[711\] Although the 100 Parties did not formally abandon the third cause of action, in closing Mr Barker said that “\[a\]s the proceeds themselves appear no longer to be identifiable, it is not possible to maintain a claim for tracing”. In any event, given that the 100 Parties failed to show that they are secured creditors of the PVL subsidiaries, their third cause of action fails.

<!-- al:1676 -->

**100 Parties’ fourth cause of action (against Mr Walker, LPF and SPF): breach of fiduciary duty and knowing receipt**

<!-- al:1677 -->

\[712\] The fourth cause of action is for breach of fiduciary duty (by Mr Walker) and knowing receipt (by SPF and LPF). This cause of action fails because:

<!-- al:1678 -->

> <!-- al:1679 -->
>
> (a) the 100 Parties have failed to show that they are secured creditors of the PVL subsidiaries;
>
> <!-- al:1680 -->
>
> (b) Mr Walker did not breach any fiduciary duties; and
>
> <!-- al:1681 -->
>
> (c) in respect of Mr Walker, pursuit of this cause of action is an abuse of process.

<!-- al:1682 -->

\[713\] It is not necessary for me to determine whether LPF received money paid in alleged breach of fiduciary duty or whether LPF and SPF had the requisite knowledge to incur liability in knowing receipt.

<!-- al:1683 -->

**100 Parties’ fifth cause of action (against Mr Walker): breach of duty as liquidator**

<!-- al:1684 -->

\[714\] The 100 Parties’ fifth cause of action is brought under s 301 of the [Companies Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_reg.20110506.OLD/ca1955r1994237/index.html) in their alleged capacity as unsecured creditors of the PVL subsidiaries. Mr Barker submitted that the 100 Parties have standing to make a s 301 application for the unsecured component of the debts allegedly owed by the PVL subsidiaries.

<!-- al:1685 -->

\[715\] I have found that none of the 100 Parties have proved that they are secured creditors of any PVL subsidiary. In making that finding, I found that the 100 Parties had not proved that the debts arising from the various loan agreements entered into by the PVL subsidiaries were assigned to the 100 Parties. It follows that they have not proved that any debts are owed by the PVL subsidiaries to them.

<!-- al:1686 -->

\[716\] This cause of action therefore fails.

<!-- al:1687 -->

**100 Parties’ sixth cause of action (against Mr Walker): breach of duty as liquidator**

<!-- al:1688 -->

\[717\] The 100 Parties’ sixth cause of action is brought under s 301 of the [Companies Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_reg.20110506.OLD/ca1955r1994237/index.html) in their alleged capacity as unsecured creditors of PVL.

<!-- al:1689 -->

\[718\] The 100 Parties plead that they were unsecured creditors of PVL pursuant to guarantees provided by PVL in respect of the loan liabilities of the PVL subsidiaries. Those loan liabilities were the liabilities (or debts) under the various loan agreements entered into by the PVL subsidiaries, which the 100 Parties pleaded had been assigned to them. The 100 Parties have not proved that any of those loan agreements were assigned to them. It follows that even if the benefit of some guarantees was assigned to the 100 Parties, there were no underlying liabilities owed to them which were caught by those guarantees.

<!-- al:1690 -->

\[719\] It follows that the 100 Parties have not proved that they are unsecured creditors of PVL. Their sixth cause of action fails.

<!-- al:1691 -->

**Whitley Parties’ first cause of action (against Mr Walker): voidable preference recoveries**

<!-- al:1692 -->

\[720\] The Whitley Parties’ first cause of action is distinct from all the other causes of action in this proceeding in that it has little to do with Mr Walker’s entry into the Services Deed and the Distribution Agreement. It focusses on voidable preference payments that Mr Walker recovered as liquidator.

<!-- al:1693 -->

\[721\] The Whitley Parties plead that Mr Walker had, as liquidator, recovered a total of $705,520 in the form of voidable preference payments that had been made to creditors of PVL or PVL subsidiaries. In closing, Mr Moss confined the claim to a total of $650,468. The Whitley Parties claimed that Mr Walker was under a duty to hold these recoveries on trust for the benefit of the unsecured creditors of the PVL company to which the recoveries belonged, or to apply the recoveries in a way which could benefit those unsecured creditors. They alleged that Mr Walker breached that duty by applying all the voidable preference recoveries to the costs of the PVL proceedings.

<!-- al:1694 -->

\[722\] There was some debate before me about whether, as the Whitley Parties pleaded, a liquidator holds voidable preference recoveries on trust for the benefit of the unsecured creditors of the company in liquidation.[112](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark111?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) I agree with Mr Moss that I do not have to resolve that debate. This is for two reasons. First, the authorities in this area are almost all concerned with the question whether a secured creditor’s security extends to the voidable preference recoveries. In (usually) answering that question in the negative, courts have sometimes said that the recoveries are held on trust for the unsecured creditors.[113](/nz/cases/NZHC/2025/2025_3486.html#_bookmark112?query=title\(%222025%20NZHC%203486%22\)) Here, the 100 Parties did not claim that their putative securities extend to the voidable preference recoveries.

<!-- al:1695 -->

\[723\] Secondly, in closing the Whitley Parties advanced their case on this cause of action on a narrow basis. Mr Moss said that whether or not the recoveries were held on trust, a liquidator could use the recoveries to fund a proceeding only if there was at least a chance that the unsecured creditors of the company would benefit from that proceeding. He submitted that Mr Walker’s use of the recoveries to fund the PVL

<!-- al:1696 -->

112 See Heath and Whale, above n 23, at \[24.72\].

<!-- al:1697 -->

113 See, for example, *Re Yagerphone Ltd* \[1935\] Ch 392.

<!-- al:1698 -->

> proceedings was a breach of duty because Mr Walker never expected the unsecured creditors of the PVL companies to receive any benefit from the proceedings.

<!-- al:1699 -->

\[724\] When dealing with issue 2, I rejected the contention that Mr Walker never thought there would be a return to unsecured creditors from the PVL proceedings. I held that he merely thought a return was unlikely. The factual basis upon which the Whitley Parties advanced this cause of action therefore fails.

<!-- al:1700 -->

\[725\] This cause of action fails also for other reasons. The Whitley Parties’ continued pursuit of it is an abuse of process (issue 10). To the extent that an order was sought under s 301 of the [Companies Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_reg.20110506.OLD/ca1955r1994237/index.html), the claim was filed out of time (issue 11).

<!-- al:1701 -->

\[726\] I note three other matters for completeness. First, Mr Walker’s evidence was that the voidable preference recoveries were used not only to pay for the PVL proceedings but also for other general costs of the liquidations, including other unsuccessful claims to recover voidable preference payments. This aspect of Mr Walker’s evidence was not seriously challenged.

<!-- al:1702 -->

\[727\] Secondly, in this cause of action the Whitley Parties also sought orders under s 284(1)(a) of the [Companies Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_reg.20110506.OLD/ca1955r1994237/index.html) that Mr Walker repay the amount of the voidable preference recoveries to the PVL companies to which they belonged. In contrast to other causes of action, here the Whitley Parties were not attempting to use s 284(1)(a) to paper over cracks in their substantive claim against Mr Walker. They were contending that Mr Walker had acted in breach of duty, not merely that it was “reasonable” to make an order under s 284(1)(a) (issue 9). Had I found that this cause of action succeeded, an order under s 284(1)(a) would have been available.

<!-- al:1703 -->

\[728\] Thirdly, in addition to pleading that the s 301 claim was out of time, Mr Walker pleaded that the entire cause of action was out of time because it was filed more than six years after the voidable preference recoveries were allegedly spent by Mr Walker on the PVL proceedings. The Whitley Parties responded by relying on the late knowledge provisions of the [Limitation Act](http://www.nzlii.org/nz/legis/consol_act.20250801.OLD/la2010133/index.html). I have not found it necessary to determine this affirmative defence.

<!-- al:1704 -->

**Whitley Parties’ second cause of action (against Mr Walker, LPF and SPF): declarations relating to Services Deed and Distribution Agreement**

<!-- al:1705 -->

\[729\] The Whitley Parties’ second cause of action makes a miscellany of complaints about the Services Deed and the Distribution Agreement. It is not well pleaded and is difficult to unpack. I will address it by reference to the relief sought.

<!-- al:1706 -->
